# Chapter 1

## Scene 01.01

Rendell’s training saber snapped across Ben’s shoulder with a crack that cut through the salle’s late-night hush.

Ben had seen the strike coming. His body had read the angle, the timing, the shift of weight onto Rendell’s back foot—and then his guard had dropped a half second late, the blade slipping through a gap that should never have been there. The hit landed clean, a sharp sting that would bloom into a bruise by morning.

He let out a breath and stepped back, lowering his blade.

Rendell shut off his saber and tugged off his helmet, sweat darkening the hair at his temples. “You all right?”

“Fine.” Ben rolled his shoulder once and felt the answer to that for himself. “Good hit.”

“You were open.” Rendell sounded more puzzled than critical, like he’d spotted something odd in a drill. “Third time this week.”

Ben knew that too well. The first time, on a Form V transition he’d been drilling since he was fourteen, he’d written it off as distraction. The second, in a three-on-one exercise, he’d blamed the hour, the week, and the usual weight of Temple life. Now this. A Soresu opening sequence—the sequence, the one his body knew by reflex—and the Force had come in behind the beat, late enough to trip him.

He didn’t say any of it.

“Late night,” he said, and thumbed his blade off. The hum died. “I’m done.”

Rendell hesitated. Ben could feel the question gathering—the reflex to press, to check on a peer who had gone off rhythm—but Rendell was tired too, and Ben was already moving toward the bench. The moment slid away.

“Get some sleep,” Rendell said at last.

“Yeah.” Ben didn’t look back. “You too.”

Footsteps crossed the salle floor. The door opened, then closed. Silence settled again, broken only by the faint chime of a turbolift somewhere in the corridor and the distant, steady click of a cleaning droid at work.

Ben stood alone in the empty salle.

The Temple at this hour was never truly quiet. Night-watch traffic murmured through the walls—Knights and Masters moving between meditation chambers and the refectory, staff keeping the Order running while most of Coruscant slept. The salle’s usual Force presence, the old accumulation of practice and focus and long hours, should have settled around him like a steady hand.

It didn’t.

That off-note in the field was still there. It had been there all week, a half-beat lag Ben kept trying to blame on fatigue or distraction or the lingering static everyone said the Force was still shedding after Abeloth. The analysts had a term for it. Residual echo. Background noise that hadn’t gone away.

Ben had stopped believing that three days ago. He hadn’t told anyone.

He lifted his training saber. Didn’t ignite it. Just held the hilt and moved into the Soresu opening sequence—slowly, deliberately, the way he’d first learned it in his father’s practice sessions. Step, pivot, guard high. The blade stayed dark. He wasn’t testing muscle memory. He was testing whatever sat underneath it.

Step, pivot, guard low.

The first three movements came clean. His body knew them the way it knew breathing. On the fourth—a tight rotation into a cross-block that should have flowed like water—his timing broke.

The Force came in late. Not absent. Not blocked. Just behind, like a musician coming in half a beat after the rest of the ensemble, all the right notes at the wrong instant. His body, expecting the field to be there at the turn, overcorrected. His shoulder pulled. His balance slipped.

Ben stopped.

He stood in the center of the salle, breathing hard, the dark hilt still in his hand. It wasn’t fatigue. It wasn’t the week or the hour or the weight of a Jedi Knight’s schedule. It was in the Force itself—a missing load-bearing beam, a gap where there should have been support. The field was lagging, and Ben’s body, built to move inside a rhythm he had never had to think about, was paying for it.

He didn’t think *structural*. He didn’t think *wound*. He felt it as a tightening in his chest, a sharpening of focus, a hard shift from *I’m failing* to *something’s wrong*. The difference came not as a theory but as a physical jolt—his shoulders squaring against a weight he couldn’t name, his jaw setting in a way that would have told his mother, if she’d been there, that he’d decided not to share.

He deactivated the blade he hadn’t ignited and set it on the bench.

The cleaning droid hummed past the salle door. A pair of voices drifted from the turbolift vestibule—Knights coming off a late meditation shift, talking too low to make out. The Temple was still alive around him, still carrying ordinary night traffic, and Ben was standing in the middle of it with a gap in the Force that nobody else could feel.

He didn’t know whether that made him the only reliable sensor in the room or the only broken one.

The door opened.

Ben turned. His father stood in the doorway with a datapad in one hand and the kind of stillness Ben had learned to read before he’d learned to read anything else—the look of a Jedi Master about to hand over a mission and waiting to see how it landed.

“Ben.” Luke’s voice was quiet, the way it always was this late. “I need you for a survey run.”

Ben’s hand tightened on the bench edge. Relief and dread hit together—relief that he might not be alone with this, dread that being alone with it had just stopped being an option.

“How bad?” he asked.

Luke’s expression stayed level. “I don’t know yet. That’s the problem.”

## Scene 01.02

Luke’s office smelled of old flimsi and the faint ozone tang of a holocomm left on too long. The door was open when Ben arrived. His father was already behind the desk, the datapad casting a blue glow over his hands, and the look on his face was the one Ben had learned to read before he’d learned much of anything else—the stillness of a Jedi Master who had already chosen his question and was waiting to see how it would land.

“Ben.” Luke didn’t stand. His voice was quiet, the way it always was this late. “Close the door.”

Ben did. The latch clicked shut, and the wash of Coruscant traffic through the window became the loudest thing in the room—speeders, airhaulers, the distant chime of a landing beacon, the city grinding on while the Grand Master of the Jedi Order sat behind a desk that had seen better decades.

“You were in the salle late,” Luke said.

It wasn’t a question. Ben didn’t answer it like one. “Couldn’t sleep.”

“So I gathered.” Luke held his gaze a moment longer, then looked down at the datapad. He slid it across the desk. “I need you for a survey run.”

Ben reached for the pad before his mind had quite caught up with the motion. He knew what one of his father’s mission briefings felt like—the pause, the careful neutrality, the way the whole thing came wrapped in words meant to sound ordinary. He’d been on the receiving end since he was old enough to hold a lightsaber. Familiarity settled into his shoulders and made them tight.

“Survey,” he said, flat. “Where?”

“Outer Rim. Darrpa Sector and points rimward.” Luke leaned back. At this distance, his Force presence was warm and steady, the same presence that had anchored Ben through half a dozen crises. “Seventeen flagged reports over the last six weeks. Anomalous Force readings. Nothing that matches any known phenomenon.”

Ben’s thumb found the scroll control. The pad lit with a string of entries—survey stations, observation posts, automated beacons all reporting the same thing. Yellow flags, not red. Anomaly, not emergency. But the pattern was wrong. The reports ran along a single bearing, a clean line through the Outer Rim that pointed toward a blank spot in the charts.

He zoomed in on three readings near the middle of the chain. The descriptions were clinical—*unclassifiable Force disturbance*, *persistent low-level variance*, *recommend follow-up*—but he didn’t need the words. He knew what the survey teams had been trying to name. The same sour taste in the Force that had been sitting under his ribs for weeks. The same half-beat lag. The same something-off that had refused to leave him alone.

His father had been reading it too.

“You flagged these yourself,” Ben said. The edge in his voice came out sharper than he’d meant.

“I did.” Luke kept his tone level. “I’ve been sitting on them.”

Ben looked up from the pad. The room felt smaller than it had a moment ago. Traffic still moved outside the window, still indifferent, but the air between desk and door had changed. His father was watching him with the patience of a man who had already done the math and was waiting for his son to catch up.

“You felt it,” Ben said.

“Yes.”

The word landed and stayed. Ben set the datapad down on the desk. His hand lingered a beat too long, blue light bleeding through his fingers, the Temple’s power grid humming under the silence.

“How long?” he asked.

“Weeks.” Luke didn’t look away. “The same as you, I suspect.”

Ben heard the difference before he could name it. His father was describing an anomaly the way a Grand Master would—a flagged report, a data point, a problem to be assigned. Ben had been carrying it as a physical thing, a sour note in the field his body had been trying to correct since the first sparring session it cost him. Luke saw the signal. Ben felt the wound.

The distinction sat between them like a third person in the room.

“It’s not residual echo,” Ben said. “Not from Abeloth.”

“I know.”

“The analysts are wrong.”

“I know that too.” Luke’s mouth shifted into something that wasn’t quite a smile. “That’s why I’m sending you.”

Ben’s jaw tightened. He looked down at the datapad again—the seventeen flags, the bearing, the blank spot at the end of the chain—and felt the pull of it, directional now. Not a diffuse ache. A current.

His father had given him a mission that ran straight along the bearing. That wasn’t coincidence. Luke didn’t do coincidence.

“You could send anyone,” Ben said. “You could send a full team.”

“I could.”

“But you’re not.”

Luke didn’t answer. He didn’t need to. The silence did the work—the same silence that had been working between them since Ben was old enough to understand that his father trusted him with things he didn’t trust anyone else with, and that the trust came wrapped in a distance neither of them had ever managed to close.

Ben pushed back from the desk. He needed to move. The window was three steps away, and he took them, his shoulders settling into readiness that looked a lot like retreat. The city spread below him, a river of light and motion that had been running for thousands of years without pausing for the Jedi Temple or the people inside it.

“The survey route,” he said. “It lines up.”

“Yes.”

“You planned that.”

Luke’s voice came from behind him, quiet and even. “I planned for the possibility that I wasn’t imagining it. The survey gives you a reason to be out there. The Order needs the data either way. If you find something, you find it. If you don’t, you’ve done your job.”

Ben didn’t turn around. His reflection in the transparisteel was a ghost over the city lights—a young man in Jedi robes, his father’s son, standing alone in an office that had seen a thousand briefings just like this one.

He didn’t ask for a partner. The thought rose, and he let it stay unspoken. He knew what operational silence meant. He’d learned it young, in a different uniform, with a different partner at his shoulder. Calling the Order meant calling people who would ask questions he wasn’t ready to answer. Desh wouldn’t ask. Desh would just be there, a steady hand on the controls and a warm absence in the Force, and Ben could carry this without explaining it to anyone who would try to put a name on it.

“I’ll need a pilot,” he said. “Someone who won’t file a lot of questions.”

Luke was quiet for a moment. When he spoke, his voice carried the weight of a man who understood exactly what his son was not saying.

“I assumed you’d have someone in mind.”

Ben turned from the window. His father was still behind the desk, still watching him with that steady patience, still holding the space between them open without pushing into it. The datapad sat dark between them.

“I’ll file the departure manifest in the morning,” Ben said.

Luke nodded once. “Keep me informed.”

“I will.”

Neither of them acknowledged what the silence around the word *partner* meant. Neither named the choice Ben had just made—to carry the burden with a man who couldn’t feel it, rather than with the Order that could. The distance between father and son tightened, held, and did not close.

Ben picked up the datapad. It was solid in his hand, the mission parameters already loading, the bearing already set.

“Ben,” Luke said.

Ben stopped at the door.

“Be careful.”

It wasn’t a command. It was the thing his father said when the mission was bigger than the briefing and they both knew it. Ben didn’t turn around.

“Yeah,” he said. “I will.”

The door opened. The traffic noise swelled to meet him. Ben walked out into the corridor with the datapad in his hand and the pull of that bearing already tugging at him like a direction he’d started following before he left the room.

## Scene 01.03

Ben’s ship was a modified SoroSuub Starfire, bought used three years ago and maintained by Ben ever since. The cockpit still carried a faint trace of the previous owner’s tabac habit, a ghost no amount of scrubbed air had managed to erase. Ben had stopped noticing it sometime during the second year. Now it just smelled like his ship.

Preflight was complete. The hangar bay tech had signed off ten minutes ago, and the Temple’s departure protocol indicators glowed green across the console. All Ben had to do was file his partner selection and lift.

His thumb slid past the Jedi roster without slowing.

The datapad rested in his lap, angled toward the cockpit lights. Three names waited in the recent-contacts queue—Knights he’d worked with before, competent people who would clear their schedules if he asked. He didn’t open their profiles. He didn’t open the roster at all. His thumb found the personal contacts list and scrolled down to the Ds while his eyes stayed on the preflight readouts, as if the choice had already been made somewhere below conscious thought and his hand was only delivering the news.

Desh Rolan. The entry was two years old and still listed a Kuat exchange code.

Ben opened the comm channel before his mind could catch up. The request pulsed on the console, riding Coruscant’s orbital relay network out toward the Core. He let it spin while he checked the nav-computer’s first-jump calculation—a short hop to the edge of the gravity well before the main hyperspace insertion—and found nothing wrong with it.

The channel answered in a crackle of long-range static.

“Ben Skywalker.” Desh’s voice came through tinny and warm at once, the way long-range comm always did when the relay was old and the signal had bounced through three satellites. “It’s past midnight on Coruscant. You’re either in trouble or about to be.”

“Neither,” Ben said. “I need a pilot.”

A pause. Not hesitation—Desh didn’t hesitate. It was the length of a man taking a sip of caf and deciding whether the conversation was worth setting the mug down for.

“Survey run,” Ben added. “Outer Rim. Should be routine.”

“Should be.” Desh sounded dry enough to strip paint. “The last time you said that, I spent three days in a medbay.”

“That was different.”

“That was a firefight in a fuel depot.”

“And you didn’t spend three days in medbay. You spent two.”

Desh snorted. The static took the edge off the sound, but the warmth underneath came through cleanly. “Where and when?”

“Terminus Station. Two days.”

Another pause. This one carried weight—years of it, the years neither of them discussed. The Galactic Alliance Guard had been dissolved for over a decade, but the habits it had carved into both of them were still there, worn smooth by time and never quite gone. Ben had been sixteen when he’d worn the gray uniform. Desh had been older—not old enough to be a mentor, but old enough to know what they were being asked to do and keep his mouth shut until they were off duty and the barracks door was closed.

Ben didn’t think about the GAG often. He didn’t let himself. But Desh’s voice on a long-range comm at midnight dragged it back anyway—the narrow corridors, the rules that only made sense if you didn’t stare at them too hard, the understanding that the Order had built something it didn’t know how to clean up after.

Desh knew what it meant when a Jedi Knight didn’t call his own Order for backup. He didn’t ask why Ben hadn’t opened the roster.

“I’ll clear my schedule,” Desh said.

Ben’s shoulders eased down a fraction. He hadn’t realized they’d been up. “I’ll send you the coordinates.”

“Send me a caf recommendation too. Terminus has terrible food.”

“Everything on Terminus is terrible. That’s why I picked it.”

“Considerate.” The channel clicked as Desh shifted something on his end—a datapad, probably, the sound of a man already pulling up his calendar. “Two days. Don’t get shot before I get there.”

“I’ll try.”

“Don’t try. Just don’t get shot.”

The channel closed. Ben sat in the cockpit with the preflight readouts still green and the partner roster still unopened and the wrongness in the Force sitting under his ribs like a stone he’d swallowed and couldn’t cough up.

He filed the departure manifest without listing a partner name. The form accepted it—the Temple’s administrative system didn’t require a second body for survey runs, only recommended one. Recommendations weren’t orders.

The hangar bay doors irised open above him. Coruscant’s night sky was a smear of city glow and orbital traffic, the stars hidden behind the light haze of a planet that hadn’t known true darkness in twenty thousand years. Ben’s ship rose on repulsors, hangar echoes fading as the landing struts retracted and the hull cleared the Temple’s outer perimeter.

He angled toward orbit. The Temple shrank in the rear display—a ziggurat of ancient stone and modern durasteel that had been the center of his life since he was old enough to walk its corridors. From altitude, it looked small. Everything did.

The wrongness didn’t diminish.

That was the first thing Ben noticed. The sensation in the Force should have eased as he left the Temple behind—disturbances tied to a location stayed with the location. But this one didn’t. It held steady through the atmospheric climb, through orbital insertion, through the nav-computer’s countdown to the first jump. The sour taste in the field didn’t fade. It sharpened.

Ben’s hands moved through the jump sequence on muscle memory. The nav-computer chimed readiness. The hyperdrive hummed up through the deck plates, a vibration he’d felt a thousand times before and barely registered anymore.

The stars stretched.

Hyperspace swallowed the ship. The blue-white tunnel of the alternate dimension closed around the hull, and the normal-space sensor feeds went dead, replaced by the steady pulse of the hyperdrive indicators. Everything was nominal. Everything was routine.

The wrongness stayed.

Ben’s hands tightened on the controls. The pressure didn’t just hold—it took on weight. Direction. It tugged at him the way a compass needle tugged toward magnetic north, a pull he felt in his chest and behind his eyes and somewhere deeper than either.

The Force did not work this way. Disturbances faded with distance. They didn’t follow ships through hyperspace. They didn’t pull.

This one did.

Ben’s breath caught. He stared at the hyperspace tunnel beyond the viewport, the endless blue-white stream of a dimension with no geography and no direction except the one the nav-computer assigned it. But the disturbance had a direction now. It had a bearing. It was drawing him toward something specific—something waiting at the end of the survey route his father had given him, the route that ran straight along that bearing, the route Luke had chosen for the possibility that he wasn’t imagining it.

The pressure stopped being a problem.

It became a destination.

# Chapter 2

## Scene 02.01

The *Steady Returns* dropped out of hyperspace like a groundcar hitting a pothole at speed.

Ben’s caf cup—a spare durasteel mug wedged against the copilot console—jumped. Brown liquid splashed across the instrument panel, spattering the nav display and comm board and streaking the sleeve of his tunic before he could get a hand up.

“Sorry,” Desh said, sounding not sorry at all. “Nav recalibration.”

Ben wiped caf off his wrist. It was still hot enough to sting. “You could warn a person.”

“Where’s the fun in that?”

The cockpit was cramped the way all light freighters got after too many modifications—extra sensor packages crowding the overhead, a jury-rigged coolant bypass running along the starboard bulkhead where some previous owner had decided not cooking the hyperdrive mattered more than looks. The *Steady Returns* smelled of recycled air, old upholstery, and the faint metallic tang of a ship that had carried people a long time. Ben had been aboard for three days, and already the smell had faded into the background.

Desh’s caf sat in its own cup—a chipped ceramic thing with a Twi’lek dance hall logo—wedged into a bracket he’d installed beside the pilot’s station. It hadn’t spilled. Ben suspected that wasn’t an accident.

“You timed that,” Ben said.

“I’ve been flying this ship for eight years. I know exactly when reversion’s going to rattle.” Desh tapped the nav display, still beaded with caf. “You’re the one who put your caf on my console.”

“Your console.”

“I’m the pilot. Everything’s my console.”

Ben pulled his sleeve down over his hand and started mopping the instrument panel. The nav display’s touchscreen flickered under the pressure, cycling through readouts—gravimetric stability, spatial positioning, hyperspace emergence variance. Most of the numbers were green. A few had gone yellow.

One was amber and edging toward orange.

“Diagnostic’s running,” Desh said, watching him. “You don’t have to do that with your sleeve.”

“I’m already wet.”

“That’s what towels are for.”

“You don’t own towels.”

“I own towels. They’re in the locker.” Desh lifted his mug and took a sip. “Clean towels. I’m a civilized person.”

Ben kept wiping. Caf had crept into the seam between the nav display and the console housing, a dark line his sleeve couldn’t quite reach. He pressed harder. The display flickered again.

The tug under his ribs hadn’t let up.

It had been there since Coruscant—before Coruscant, if he was honest, a slow pressure he’d been able to shove aside until it had become impossible to ignore. In hyperspace it became a pull, a direction under his ribs drawing him toward a point he couldn’t name. During reversion it had spiked, a sharp lurch like the deck dropping away beneath him, and now it was settling back into something steadier.

Something with edges.

“Diagnostic’s done,” Desh said.

“And?”

“And the nav-computer thinks I’m lying to it.” Desh spun the display so Ben could see. “Gravimetric drift on emergence. Point-three percent off the charts. Spatial positioning variance is worse—the computer keeps recalculating and coming up with a different answer each time.”

“That’s a sensor problem.”

“That’s what I thought.” Desh tapped the panel. The numbers reset, ran again, and came back with the same amber warning. “So I ran it three times. Same result. Then I ran it on the backup array.” Another tap. “Same result. Then I pulled the last six emergences from the log and ran a comparison.”

Ben stopped wiping. “And?”

“And the drift isn’t random.” Desh’s voice had gone dry, the voice of a man who had spent years in intelligence and knew when data was telling him something he didn’t want to hear. “It’s directional. Every emergence, the variance points the same way. Same bearing. And it’s getting stronger the farther we go along the route.”

The pull beneath Ben’s ribs tightened.

“Show me,” he said.

Desh brought up the log on the main display. Six emergences, six sets of variance data, all clustered around a single vector. The pattern was plain enough that Ben didn’t need the numbers; the scatter plot told the story. Tight grouping, each point a little farther from center than the last, all leaning the same direction.

“That’s not instrument error,” Ben said.

“No.” Desh took another sip of caf. “Instrument error doesn’t have a bearing.”

The cockpit went quiet. Hyperspace was gone now; stars held steady through the viewport, and the nav-computer was cycling through recalibration. In another minute they’d jump again. The first survey world was still two hours out.

Ben knew the bearing. He’d known it since Coruscant—the direction the unease kept pulling him, the line his instincts came back to no matter how often he shoved them aside. He’d been trying not to name it, trying to make it background noise, something he could outrun by staying in motion.

Desh’s instruments had named it for him.

“You’re quiet,” Desh said.

“Thinking.”

“That’s the bad kind of quiet.”

Ben almost smiled. It didn’t quite make it to his mouth. “You’ve got a sensor array reading a directional anomaly along our exact route, and you’re not worried?”

“I didn’t say I wasn’t worried.” Desh set his mug down. “I said the data has a bearing. I didn’t say I know what it means.”

“What do you think it means?”

“I think the nav-computer’s confused, and I think the backup array’s confused in the same way, and I think that’s interesting.” He looked at Ben. “I also think you’ve been twitchy since we left Terminus, and you’re not telling me something.”

The words hung in the recycled air. Ben felt the weight of them—the years of shared history between him and Desh, the things they didn’t discuss because discussing them would mean naming what they’d been part of. The GAG. The gray uniforms. The narrow corridors and the rules that only made sense if you didn’t stare too hard.

Desh knew what it meant when a Jedi Knight didn’t call his own Order for backup. He’d known since the first comm call. He hadn’t asked then, and he wasn’t asking now—just leaving the door open, the way he always did, in case Ben wanted to walk through it.

Ben didn’t.

“I’ve been sensing something,” he said. “Since Coruscant. A disturbance in the Force. I don’t know what it is.”

Desh took that in. His expression didn’t change; he just stayed calm and practical, the way he always did when a crisis turned up. “And this disturbance has a bearing.”

“It seems to.”

“The same bearing as my sensor drift.”

Ben’s hands had gone still. He made them stay that way. “It seems to.”

Desh glanced at the display, then at Ben, then back again.

“Well,” he said. “That’s unsettling.”

The nav-computer chimed. Recalibration complete. The first survey world was resolving on the long-range scanners—a small, rocky body around an unremarkable star, the sort of place the Jedi Order surveyed by the dozen every year.

The drift on the sensors didn’t stop. It climbed instead, the amber warning creeping toward red, the variance numbers rising, the bearing holding steady and true and aimed straight at the planet ahead.

Ben’s hands tightened on the armrest.

The pull had teeth now.

## Scene 02.02

The ground under Ben’s boots was the color of old rust, broken into plates that shifted and skated with every step. He picked his way across it with one hand hooked on the strap of his survey pack, the other out for balance. Dry wind came off the east, laden with grit fine enough to work between his collar and his neck.

Behind him, Desh’s boots crunched on gravel at a careful distance. Close enough to keep pace. Far enough not to crowd him.

“Survey record for this one goes back sixty years,” Desh said. “Uninhabited. No structures. No prior occupation. Filed by a Knight named Tarnis.”

“Never heard of him.”

“Her. And neither has anyone else. I checked the Temple registry while you were packing the scanner.” A pause. “She filed three surveys in one year and then transferred to the AgriCorps.”

Ben slowed, scanning the slope ahead. The ruins were visible now—low shapes breaking the rise, half-swallowed by wind erosion and time. Stonework. Definitely stonework. On a world that, according to the record, had been empty sixty years ago.

“AgriCorps,” he said.

“That’s what the file says.”

“That’s one way to bury a report.”

“I didn’t say it was buried. I said she transferred.” Desh’s voice had a dry edge to it. “I’m just giving you the facts.”

“Since when?”

“Since you asked me to come on this survey and I started paying attention to what the Order’s records say about the worlds we’re standing on.”

The ruins were larger than they’d looked from approach. What Ben had taken for a single collapsed structure resolved into a complex—three buildings, maybe four, arranged around a central space that might once have been a courtyard before wind and age chewed it down. Volcanic stone. Dark, dense blocks fitted together without mortar. The seams were tight enough that Ben couldn’t have slipped a fingernail between them.

Desh stopped at the edge of the courtyard and drew out his scanner, a compact civilian model with scuffed edges, something he’d had since his GAG days. “I’m getting nothing. No power signatures, no alloys, no active tech. Just rock.”

“That matches the survey.”

“That matches the survey that said there was nothing here at all.”

Ben walked into the courtyard. The wind dropped as he crossed the threshold, blocked by the surviving walls, and the sudden quiet pressed at his ears. The sensation was here. Not strong—nothing like the spike he’d felt during reversion, when the nav-computer had struggled to place them in space—but present. A low vibration without a source, as if the whole place were still ringing from a blow long after the strike had ended.

He didn’t mention it to Desh.

“The tool-marks,” he said instead, crouching beside an exposed block. “Look at the edges. These weren’t cut with modern equipment.”

Desh leaned over his shoulder. “Old Republic?”

“Older.” Ben ran a finger along the stone without touching it. The cut was clean, precise, made by something that had sliced through volcanic rock like soft wood. “I don’t recognize the technique.”

“That’s a short list.”

“It’s still a list.” Ben straightened and moved deeper into the ruins. The central building had held up better than the others—three walls still standing, the fourth collapsed inward, the roof long gone. Inside, the floor was stone flags, some cracked, most intact. On the far wall, sheltered from the wind by the angle of the surviving stone, were markings.

Ben stopped.

They were carved at chest height—a series of shapes that didn’t resolve into any alphabet he knew. Interlocking circles. A jagged line cutting through them at an angle, deliberate and precise, the edges still sharp after what had to be centuries. Whatever had cut them had been harder than the stone. Harder than anything in a standard survey kit.

“What is that?” Desh asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Guess.”

“I don’t guess.” Ben pulled the datapad from his pack and started recording. Wide shots first, establishing the wall, the context, the relation of the markings to the rest of the structure. Then closer. The circles. The line. The way the cut deepened at the intersection points, as if the carver had pressed harder there.

His professional curiosity ran clean and focused, the way it always did when a puzzle presented itself. Catalog first. Hypothesize later. The markings were data. The tool-marks were data. The age of the stone, the erosion patterns, the absence of any known architectural tradition—all data.

The low vibration sharpened.

He finished the visual record and lowered the datapad. The markings were right there at eye level, the stone dark and cool in the shadow of the wall. He could see the faint striations inside the cuts, the tiny variations in depth that spoke to a hand-guided tool rather than a machine. Whoever had carved this had done it slowly.

He reached out.

His fingers brushed the edge of the jagged line.

The jolt ran up his arm.

Not a shock—nothing electric, nothing that burned or stung. A vibration. Low, steady, with no physical source, climbing from his fingertips to his wrist to his elbow like a plucked string that refused to die away. The stone was stone. The cut was a cut. But the distance between his hand and the wall had gone bad, a fractional error his body registered before his mind could catch up. The stone was here. His fingers were here. The space between them wasn’t right.

He pulled his hand back hard enough to leave room between himself and the wall.

His breathing had changed. He could hear it. One breath. Two. He forced the air out slowly the way he’d been trained, and the vibration thinned to a hum, then to an aftertaste he couldn’t name.

Desh was watching him. “Ben?”

“Give me a minute.”

“You pulled back like it burned you.”

“It didn’t burn.” Ben stood. His legs worked. His hand was steady when he looked at it. “I’m fine.”

“You’re a terrible liar.”

“I’m an excellent liar. You’re just hard to fool.” He turned back to the datapad and started the scanner. The markings resolved on the screen—shapes, dimensions, depth measurements, mineral composition of the surrounding stone. Data. Clean, objective, transmittable data.

The vibration was still in his hand, a phantom sensation like the memory of a sound he couldn’t quite hear.

He recorded the shapes. He recorded the tool-marks. He recorded the age estimate based on erosion patterns and the geological context and the dimensions of the structure and every other piece of information the standard survey template asked for.

He did not record what the Force had told him.

The omission was deliberate. He knew that. He knew it before he finished the first data field, before he attached the images, before he keyed in Luke’s comm code on the transmission header. He was leaving something out. Choosing to leave it out.

He didn’t ask himself why. He just did it.

“Report going in?” Desh asked.

“Now.”

“You’re including the markings.”

“Yes.”

“You’re not including whatever made you jump.”

Ben’s thumb hovered over the send key. The wind had picked up again, sliding through the gaps in the walls, and the grit it carried stung the back of his neck. High overhead, a freighter’s contrail cut across the sky—someone moving supplies through the sector, unaware and uninterested in what was buried on this rock.

“No,” Ben said. “I’m not.”

Desh didn’t answer. He didn’t need to. The silence between them was the same silence they’d learned in narrow corridors years ago, wearing gray uniforms and following rules that only made sense if you didn’t stare too hard. The quiet of a man who had decided something and wasn’t going to explain it.

Ben pressed send.

The transmission went. The datapad chimed confirmation. Somewhere in the Temple on Coruscant, a server received a survey report that was technically accurate and structurally incomplete, and Luke Skywalker would read it and see the markings and the measurements and the careful catalog of an anomaly his son had found on a world that wasn’t supposed to have one.

He would not see the sensation behind it. He would not know that Ben had touched the stone and felt something that didn’t belong in any report.

The vibration stayed in Ben’s hand. He flexed his fingers, trying to shake it loose, and failed.

“Let’s finish the site,” he said. “We’ve got another two hours of daylight.”

“Ben.”

“Later, Desh.”

He started toward the next structure before Desh could answer. The wind followed him, dry and full of grit, and the sensation followed with it. Somewhere behind his ribs, the unease that had been building since the nav-computer first showed him the bearing settled into something harder. Not fear. Not yet.

Dread. The specific dread of a man who had just hidden something from his father and didn’t know what it meant about either of them.

## Scene 02.03

The second world was colder than the first, not because of season, but because of something older buried in the rock—something that didn’t care what the weather thought it was doing.

Ben crouched in the lee of a broken wall and ran his thumb over stone that should not have been here. Same markings. Interlocking circles. A jagged line slashing through them at the same angle, same depth, same slight overcut at the intersections where the carver had pressed too hard. The stone was different—granitic, not volcanic, darker, harder, wrong for the work—and the cuts were still clean, still sharp enough to snag the edge of his glove.

His chest tightened before he noticed it. He forced a breath in anyway.

“Same hand,” he said.

Desh stood a few meters back, scanner in hand, jacket zipped against a wind that had teeth. “You’re sure.”

“I’m sure.”

“Same tool?”

“Same hand.” Ben straightened. His knees ached from the cold seeping through the stone flags. “The tool I can’t speak to. The pressure, the angle, the way the cut deepens at the intersections—that’s a person, not a machine. Same person.”

Desh studied him for a long moment, then the markings, then him again. “One site was interesting.”

“I know.”

“Two sites is a pattern.”

“I know.”

“And a pattern means someone put these here on purpose.”

Ben didn’t answer. The tightness in his chest had climbed into his throat. The feeling here was stronger than on the first world—not a spike that jolted through his hand and left him shaking, but something steadier, a pressure that didn’t belong to the wind or the cold. It sat in the stone with no source he could point to, a vibration that seemed to live in the rock itself. The space between his body and the wall felt off, as if his eyes and his spine were giving him slightly different answers.

He didn’t touch the carvings this time. He didn’t need to.

“Record it,” he said. “Full imaging. I want the tool-mark analysis run against the first site before we break atmosphere.”

“Already started.” Desh tucked away the scanner and pulled out his datapad. “I grabbed the first site’s data while you were in the courtyard. The computer’s running the comparison now. Should have results by the time we’re back aboard.”

Ben turned from the wall. The ruins here were smaller than the first site—a single structure, maybe a waystation, built from the same dark stone and fitted with the same impossible precision. The roof had collapsed centuries ago. Wind and ice had chewed the remaining walls raw, but the carvings were sheltered in the deepest corner, protected by an overhang that had held through everything the climate could throw at it.

Deliberate placement. Someone had wanted these to last.

“Desh.”

“Yeah.”

“If you were building something you wanted found a thousand years later, how would you do it?”

Desh considered that. “I’d put it somewhere I knew someone would look.”

“That’s what I thought.”

They finished the site in forty minutes. Ben catalogued everything the survey template wanted—dimensions, materials, erosion patterns, geological context, mineral composition of the surrounding stone, atmospheric readings, local vegetation analysis. He recorded the markings from three angles and ran the scanner over them twice. He did not record the pressure in his chest or the vibration in the stone or the way the feeling had sharpened from a hum to something almost directional, a pull he could feel in his teeth.

Leaving that out was easier the second time.

---

The *Steady Returns* was warm. Recycled air, the hum of environmental systems, the faint smell of caf from the galley. Ben stood behind Desh’s chair in the cockpit and watched the nav console build its model.

Two systems. Two survey worlds. Two sets of ruins that shouldn’t have existed on worlds his father’s Order had marked empty for decades.

Desh overlaid them on the main display. The first world glowed blue. The second glowed amber. Between them stretched a line of empty space—no charted systems, no mapped routes, no survey markers, nothing. Just void.

“That’s the bearing,” Desh said.

“I can see that.”

“The bearing you’ve been tracking since we left Coruscant.”

“I can see that too.”

Desh extended the line. It cut through the empty sector and kept going, straight as a rule, into a region of the chart that was blank in a way that didn’t match the surrounding data. Stars on one side. Stars on the other. Nothing in between—not even the standard notation for uncharted space, the gray hash that meant they hadn’t looked there yet. This was deliberate absence. A hole in the map someone had cut out and thrown away.

“That’s not normal,” Desh said.

“No.”

“That’s fourteen systems that should be there and aren’t.”

Ben leaned over the console. The line Desh had drawn ran straight through the center of the void, no deviation, no curve. Whatever was out there, it sat exactly where the bearing said it would.

“Punch the coordinates,” he said.

Desh keyed them in. The nav-computer took a long moment to answer—longer than it should have, long enough that Ben felt the silence in the cockpit settle into something heavier. Then the display shifted. The void stayed void. The coordinates hung in the middle of it, attached to no system name, no survey designation, no navigational hazard marker.

Nothing there. According to every chart in the Order’s database, there was nothing there.

Ben stared at the empty sector. The feeling had a shape now. A direction. A destination. It wasn’t just something he felt anymore—it was somewhere he could go.

“Luke’s survey route,” he said.

“What about it?”

“It runs right along this bearing. Every world we’ve stopped at. Every world still on the schedule.” He kept his eyes on the display. “Coincidence or design.”

Desh was quiet for a moment. Then: “You don’t want to know which one it is.”

“I don’t want to know yet.”

“Ben.”

“I know.”

He didn’t explain. He didn’t need to. The silence between them was the same silence they’d learned years ago, wearing gray uniforms and following rules that only made sense if you didn’t stare too hard—the quiet of a man who had decided something and wasn’t going to defend it. Desh read him the way Desh always read him, and chose not to press.

The bearing pointed to a cluster of systems that did not appear on any navigation chart Ben had ever seen. Something was out there.

This one did.

Ben’s breath caught. He stared at the hyperspace tunnel beyond the viewport, the endless blue-white stream of a dimension with no geography and no direction except the one the nav-computer assigned it. But the disturbance had a direction now. It had a bearing. It was drawing him toward something specific—something waiting at the end of the survey route his father had given him, the route that ran straight along that bearing, the route Luke had chosen for the possibility that he wasn’t imagining it.

The pressure stopped being a problem.

It became a destination.

# Chapter 3

## Scene 03.01

The third world looked wrong before they broke atmosphere.

Ben felt it through the hull while Desh was still working the approach vector—a weight low in his chest, denser than the uneasy pull he’d been tracking for weeks. This wasn’t ambient. It was close, as if the planet itself had leaned in and set a thumb against his sternum to see how he reacted.

“Readings?” he asked.

Desh didn’t take his eyes off the console. “Atmosphere’s breathable. Gravity’s a little light—point nine three standard. Temperature’s cold, but nothing hostile.” He paused. “And there’s power.”

Ben looked up. “Power.”

“Yeah.” Desh’s tone stayed flat, the way it did when something refused to make sense. “Not much. Not a settlement. But something down there is drawing current.”

Ben studied the sensor display. The blip was small and steady, tucked in a valley between two ridges of dark rock. Their bearing ran straight through it.

“That’s our survey point,” he said.

“I know.”

“There shouldn’t be power at our survey point.”

“I know that, too.”

They set down a kilometer out, on a flat stretch of hardpan that crackled under the *Steady Returns*’ landing gear like old bone. Outside, the air was thin and dry, carrying a mineral bite—silica dust, maybe, or something ground finer than sand over a very long time. The sky hung overhead the color of a bruised wound on the mend.

From the ramp, the structure was already visible. It sat half-buried in the ridge face, dark stone cut into angles that didn’t belong to the surrounding geology. No collapsed walls. No weather-eaten edges. The lines were clean.

“That’s not ruins,” Desh said.

“No.”

“That’s a bunker.”

Ben didn’t answer. He was already moving, one hand near his blaster but not touching it, his Force-sense stretching ahead toward the dark stone and the hum buried beneath it. The weight in his chest tightened with each step. Not hostile. Not overtly. More like standing too close to something vast that hadn’t noticed him yet.

Desh came up beside him, scanner in hand, jacket zipped against the cold. “Power cells. Deep ones. The readings say this place has been running a long time.”

“How long?”

“Longer than it ought to.”

The entrance was a recessed slab set into the ridge face, seamless except for a hairline seam tracing its perimeter. No handle. No visible mechanism. Just dark stone, that fine line, and the vibration of power coming up through Ben’s boots.

He stopped three meters short of the door. The thing in his chest had become heavier, specific and patient, like a hand resting flat against his ribs.

“Ben,” Desh said quietly. “Your chrono.”

Ben glanced down. The readout on his wrist was flickering—not dead, just drifting, the seconds moving forward and then stuttering back, as if time itself couldn’t decide which way to run.

“Mine’s two seconds fast,” Desh said. “Has been since we hit atmosphere.”

Ben looked at the door. The seam was glowing now, a faint blue line around the perimeter, and the hum had risen into something he could feel in his teeth.

“It knows we’re here,” he said.

The blue light detached from the seam and reached for him.

It wasn’t a beam. It was a pulse, a cool wash that crawled over his body from shoulders to boots and back again, lingering at his chest, his temples, the base of his skull. Ben held still. The scan felt like a hand reading a text it hadn’t seen in centuries—checking each line, pausing where something didn’t quite match what it had expected, then moving on with something that felt almost like reluctance.

Four seconds passed. It felt longer.

Then the door split down the center and slid open, silent on tracks that hadn’t moved in longer than the Republic had existed.

Desh let out a slow breath. “What was that.”

“Force-recognition.” Ben’s voice came out rougher than he intended. “It was checking my signature.”

“Did you pass?”

“It let me in.” He stepped through the opening. “I don’t think it liked doing it.”

The interior stayed dark for exactly as long as it took Ben’s boot to touch the stone floor. Then the lights came on—not all at once, but in sequence, a wave of pale blue illumination rolling down the corridor like a held breath finally released. Displays flickered to life in the wall consoles. The power cells’ hum deepened, steadied, turned into a thrum he could feel through the floor plates.

The air smelled like dust and old metal and something else—ozone, faintly, the way a lightsaber smelled after a long sparring session.

Desh had his sidearm out before the light finished spreading. He swept the corridor from left to right in one smooth motion, checking angles the way Ben had seen him do a hundred times. “This is military.”

“I know.”

“Command-and-control. The layout, the console placement, the sight lines—” He gestured with the blaster. “Somebody built this to run a campaign from.”

Ben moved to the nearest console. The display cycled through characters he didn’t recognize—not Aurebesh, not anything the Order had taught him, something older and sharper, all hard angles where modern writing curved. The message had been running so long the pattern seemed worn into the screen like a scar.

“Can you read it?” Desh asked.

“No.”

“But you know what it is.”

Ben touched the console. The characters flickered under his fingertips, and the thrum in the floor shifted. It stopped being steady and started cycling, repeating, a message trying to force itself through a frequency nobody used anymore.

“It’s been broadcasting,” he said. “All this time. On channels nobody monitors.”

The characters stuttered. Changed. Settled again.

Ben watched them resolve, the angular strokes softening into letters he knew, archaic but readable, the Basic of an era long before the Order he served.

Two words.

SEAL FAILING.

The message cycled. SEAL FAILING. Cycled again. SEAL FAILING.

Ben’s breath caught. The weight on his chest turned into a pressure that pinned him where he stood, one hand still on the console, his eyes fixed on the display and the two words repeating over and over. He read them. He did not yet know what they were protecting against. The meaning hadn’t landed. Only the alarm had—the hard knowledge that something built before the Republic had been waiting all this time for someone to hear it.

“Ben,” Desh said. His voice had gone very quiet. “What does that mean?”

Ben didn’t answer. The message cycled again. SEAL FAILING.

The wrongness wasn’t ambient anymore. It was the sound of something breaking, and it had been breaking for centuries while no one listened.

## Scene 03.02

The data core wasn’t a room so much as a vault—circular, maybe eight meters across, ringed with consoles that had gone dark centuries before the facility’s power grid woke up and remembered its job. Emergency strips glowed pale blue along the floor seams, enough to work by, nowhere near enough to feel safe.

Desh had already found the access panel. It sat knee-high in the wall, its surface pocked with corrosion from air that hadn’t moved in a thousand years. He was down on one knee, working the edge with a flat tool Ben didn’t recognize, his face blank in the way it got when something resisted him and he’d already decided it was going to lose.

“Whoever built this didn’t want anyone pulling data out of it later,” Desh said. The tool scraped metal, found a gap, then bit. “Panel’s welded itself shut. The metal’s fused.”

“Can you get through it?”

“Give me a minute.”

Ben stood in the center of the vault and let the cold settle around him. The air was so dry his breath came out in white wisps that vanished fast in the blue light. The power cells hummed deeper here than in the corridor—not louder, just heavier, a vibration that climbed through his boots and lodged in his bones. Beneath it, the Force carried something else. A record-keeping residue. Not a threat. Not a warning. Just the sense that these walls had been paying attention for a very long time and hadn’t lost a thing.

The panel came free with a crack that rang off the stone. Desh set it aside and peered into the opening.

“Storage media,” he said. “Old. Not crystal—something earlier. Solid-state, maybe, or whatever they used before solid-state.” He reached into his jacket and brought out a data coupler, the universal adapter that had gotten him through half a dozen planetary customs offices and twice as many locked doors. “I’m going to have to guess at the interface. If I miss, we lose the data.”

“How badly can you miss?”

“Badly enough that nothing happens. Or badly enough that the storage bricks itself and we get nothing.” He was already fitting the coupler into the gap, his hands moving with practiced economy, the motions of someone who’d done this in worse places and under worse pressure. “Ready?”

“Do it.”

The connection sparked once—a brief flare of blue-white light that sharpened Desh’s face—and then again, a second arc that jumped from the coupler to the console housing and left a scorch mark on the stone. The hum in the floor stuttered. Came back. Held.

Desh exhaled. “That was the bad kind of spark.”

“Is it holding now?”

“Looks like it.” He rose, brushed dust from his knees, and studied the nearest console. “Connection’s stable. Data’s moving. Give it a second to—”

The displays came on.

Not one at a time. All at once, a ring of pale blue light washing the vault in old-star color. The central projector—a circular plate set into the floor that Ben hadn’t clocked as a projector until it lit—threw a holographic star map into the air above it. The map was incomplete, gaps going dark where the data had degraded, but the surviving sections were sharp enough to read.

Ben stepped closer. It didn’t match any chart he knew. The stars were where they should be—the coreward drift recognizable, the sweep of the Outer Rim there—but someone had drawn lines across them that didn’t belong to any modern navigation system. Routes. Connections. A network.

“That’s not a survey map,” Desh said.

“No.”

“That’s military.”

Ben didn’t answer. He was reading the geometry of it—the way the lines converged, the way the installations sat. Not randomly. Not even defensively. They formed a perimeter, nodes spaced at intervals that made no sense unless you were trying to hold something in. At the center of the ring, where every line met, was a single point. A fortress.

The pressure in his chest tightened. It had been there since they entered the facility, a weight behind everything, the sense of something huge holding still. Now it had edges. The lines on the map weren’t just routes. They were a fence. And the fence pointed at something.

“Command-and-control architecture,” Desh said. He’d moved to the console nearest the door and was reading its display with the flat, rapid focus of someone trained to pull tactical meaning out of unfamiliar systems. “The node placement, the routing, the way the data flows back to the center—somebody built this to run an operation. A big one. Multiple installations reporting to a central authority, all feeding the same decision point.” He glanced up. “This isn’t a listening post. It’s part of a network.”

“How many installations?”

“I count twelve nodes on the overlay. Nine are dark—no power readings, no active data feed. Three are still live.” He paused. “Including this one.”

Ben traced the perimeter with his eyes. The installations formed a ring around the central fortress, spaced evenly, each one a point on a circumference that stretched across a sector’s worth of empty space. The scale was wrong for anything the modern Order would build. This wasn’t a listening network. It wasn’t a research array. It was a cage.

He didn’t say that. The word wanted to surface—containment—but saying it would mean admitting he was already building a theory, and the theory would lead to questions he wasn’t ready to ask. He let the map stay a map. Geometry. Data. Facts that didn’t need interpretation yet.

“Can you pull the navigation data?” he asked.

“Already copying.” Desh’s fingers moved across the console, bringing up a secondary display. “Route coordinates, installation schematics, whatever’s left of the comm logs. Most of it’s degraded—the storage medium’s been living on residual power longer than the Republic has been around, and it’s losing bits every time it cycles. But the core nav data’s intact. Someone wanted the routes to survive.”

The central projector flickered. The map shifted, zooming in on the fortress, and a string of coordinates resolved in the air beneath it—a destination marker in a script Ben didn’t recognize, with a translation overlay the system had generated automatically. The coordinates pointed to a region of space that wasn’t on any modern chart.

“Uncharted,” Desh said. “No hyperspace lane. No Republic survey. Nothing.” He went quiet for a beat. “Somebody erased it.”

“Not erased.” Ben watched the coordinates cycle. “Hidden. There’s a difference.”

“Is there?”

“Erasing means you don’t want anyone to find it. Hiding means you don’t want anyone to look.”

Desh considered that, then reached into his jacket again and pulled out a datapad, syncing it to the console with the same casual efficiency he used for everything he wasn’t supposed to be doing in front of polite company. “I’m pulling everything. Coordinates, route data, whatever’s left of the comm logs. If there’s a signature on any of this, I want it.”

The comm logs were first to resolve. Most were too degraded to read—fragments of text in the same angular script, cycling too fast for the translation overlay to catch. But one entry held. The system had preserved it, or the operator who wrote it had flagged it for preservation, and the translation came through clean.

Authorization: Gavran. Status: active. Seal integrity nominal. Next scheduled inspection: —

The date field was blank. The log had been set to repeat, and it had been repeating for a thousand years without anyone updating it.

“Gavran,” Ben said. The name felt strange in his mouth—old, weighted, belonging to a history he’d never been taught.

“Commanding officer, maybe. Or the architect.” Desh was reading over his shoulder. “Whoever he was, he signed off on this network. And he’s been dead a very long time.”

Ben didn’t answer. He was looking at the map again—the ring of installations, the central fortress, the routes connecting them in a pattern that was starting to feel less like infrastructure and more like a sentence he couldn’t quite read. The pressure in his chest had sharpened into something else. Not pain. Not warning. Just the weight of a thing that had been waiting for someone to notice it.

“Coordinates are on the nav computer,” Desh said. “Route’s plotted. The *Steady Returns* can make the jump whenever you give the word.”

Ben copied the data to his own datapad. The transfer took three seconds. The destination marker blinked on the display—a point in deep uncharted space, surrounded by nothing, linked to the network by a single thin line the map had preserved for a thousand years.

The fortress was waiting.

And Ben was the one who’d found it.

## Scene 03.03

Ben had typed the report three times.

The first draft was clinical—survey notes, sensor logs, a dry account of the perimeter facility that read like every report he’d ever filed and meant absolutely nothing. He deleted it before the third paragraph. The second was honest—the seal, the warning, the thing he’d felt without finding a name for it—and it sat in the buffer for eleven minutes while he stared at the comm panel and felt the Force tug toward coordinates that weren’t on any chart. He deleted that one too.

The third version was still on the screen. A compromise. Enough detail to satisfy protocol, enough omission to leave him room to move. The cursor blinked at the end of a sentence about anomalous sensor readings. He hadn’t mentioned the Force. He hadn’t mentioned the seal.

He highlighted the draft. His finger hovered over the delete command.

“You’re not sending that.”

Desh’s voice came from the cockpit hatch, low and flat. Ben didn’t turn. The copilot’s chair creaked as Desh settled into it, and then came the silence that meant he was reading over Ben’s shoulder, the same way he’d once read threat assessments and mission briefs when they’d both worn gray and pretended they were old enough for the work.

“No,” Ben said. “Not yet.”

The words landed between them and changed the shape of the cockpit. The pressure that had been sitting in Ben’s chest—unremarked, easy to ignore while he was busy deleting drafts—tightened into something with edges.

Desh didn’t ask why. He didn’t ask what Ben was waiting for. He looked at the draft, then at Ben, his expression going still in that unreadable way it got when he recognized a pattern before anyone named it.

Then he reached across the console and brought up the nav computer.

The fortress coordinates were still in the buffer from the data-core download—a string of numbers pointing to a blank spot on every chart. Desh pulled them up without asking permission, his hands moving with the same practiced economy he’d used on the access panel in the vault. He paused with his fingers over the entry key.

“These?”

Ben looked at the coordinates. The Force pulled again, not as a haze at the back of his mind but as a hard, directional tug, like a current finding a gap in a bulkhead. The seal was failing on a clock no one else could hear. The report would take days to reach the Council, more days to work through the analysis queue, and more after that before anyone decided what to do with it. The seal didn’t have days.

“Yeah,” he said. “Those.”

Desh entered them. The nav computer shifted, probability matrices cycling as it tried to build a route through space that didn’t exist in its databases. Course corrections appeared and vanished while the processor worked, throwing out solutions that violated safety parameters, then trying again.

“Computer doesn’t like it,” Desh said.

“Can it do it?”

“It’s a nav computer. It’ll do what you tell it to do. Whether we come out the other side in one piece is a different question.” He watched the display cycle. “Route’s going to be rough. No hyperspace lane, no beacon network, no relay buoys. We’re flying blind into a hole in the map.”

Ben didn’t answer. The pull had turned heavy now, less like a tug than a weight pressing from ahead of them. Not the missing-handrail feeling he’d grown used to, but something spatial and immediate. The bulkheads seemed to lean in. Distance itself was refusing to behave.

Desh leaned back in the copilot’s chair and studied him. The silence stretched.

“You know what you’re doing,” Desh said. It wasn’t a question.

“I know the seal won’t wait for a committee.”

“That’s not what I asked.”

Ben met his eyes. Desh’s face stayed unreadable, but Ben knew him too well to mistake the stillness. He wasn’t objecting. He was making sure Ben understood the choice—and staying in the chair while Ben made it.

“You could tell me to send the report,” Ben said.

“Would you send it?”

The question hung there. Ben looked at the draft on his screen—the third version, the compromise, the honest-enough report he’d been about to delete anyway. He closed it without saving.

“That’s what I thought,” Desh said.

He turned back to the nav computer. The route had resolved into a thin line through unmapped space, the probability matrix showing a seventy-two percent confidence rating and declining. The hyperdrive was ready.

Ben pulled up the jump controls. His hand didn’t hesitate. The urgency of the seal’s degradation crowded the doubt aside before it could fully form. The coordinates waited. Desh stayed in his chair, hands resting on the console, the choice made and shared and locked in.

“Jumping,” Ben said.

The stars stretched.

Hyperspace swallowed them in the familiar rush—blue-white tunnel, brief disorientation, the ship settling into a new rhythm. Ben waited for the strain in the Force to ease. It always did in hyperspace. Distance usually thinned everything.

It didn’t.

It pressed harder. Not the directional pull of the coordinates, but a weight against the hull, against the bulkheads, against Ben’s own awareness. The seal’s failure wasn’t something they were flying away from; it was the medium they were flying through, and hyperspace wasn’t carrying them clear of it.

It was carrying them deeper.

Ben’s fingers tightened on the armrest. The blue-white tunnel streamed past the viewport, and the pressure climbed—spatial, directional, the distance ahead of them still refusing to make sense. It was as if the ship had hit weather in a place weather shouldn’t exist.

Desh was watching the sensor readouts. His face had gone still in a different way now—focus, not surprise, as he read data he didn’t like.

“That’s not normal,” he said.

“No.”

“The wrongness. It’s not fading.”

“I know.”

Desh didn’t ask what it meant. He didn’t need to. The nav computer was still painting its route through uncharted space, and the strain was pressing against the hull like something waiting for them to commit. Ben was alone with what he’d chosen—and Desh had chosen to stay alone with him.

The cost of that sat at the edge of Ben’s awareness, unexamined, as hyperspace carried them toward the source.

# Chapter 4

## Scene 04.01

The plateau felt wrong before Ben could have put a name to it.

Not the thin air burning in his lungs, or the wind dragging over the rock in a steady, hollow hiss. Those were just weather. Something deeper rode under them—a pressure moving through the mountain itself, directed and patient, like a breath held too long.

Desh walked three paces to Ben’s left, breath steaming white in the cold. He hadn’t said a word since they’d left the ship. That was his silence when he was working: not tension, but measurement. Ben had learned the difference during their year in gray uniforms, and the habit had stuck.

The fortress came out of the mountainside the way a ship dropped out of hyperspace: not gradually, but all at once, the angle of the light shifting to expose what had been there the whole time.

From the ridge it had looked like geology. Weathered stone, a shadow that might have been a crack in the mountain. Up close, the crack became a seam—stone on one side, metal alloy on the other, joined so cleanly that a thousand years of freeze-thaw had never pried it wider. The wall climbed fifty meters before disappearing into the slope’s natural face. No windows. No symbols. No decoration at all.

It hadn’t been built to be admired. It had been built to last.

Ben stopped ten meters out. The pressure here had tightened until it set his teeth on edge—not pain, exactly, but a hard weight behind his jaw, the same feeling he’d been learning to recognize over the past three months. The seal was somewhere inside this mountain. So was whatever it had been made to hold.

“No welcome mat,” Desh said.

Ben glanced over. In the sharp mountain light, Desh’s face was unreadable, but his stance had shifted: feet planted, weight ready, hands loose at his sides. A man who’d already counted the exits and didn’t like the number.

“That’s the idea,” Ben said.

“I know it’s the idea. I’m saying I noticed.”

The entry mechanism sat in the wall at chest height—a recessed panel of dark alloy, worn smooth by time, otherwise blank. No controls. No interface. Just a palm-sized depression in the metal, waiting.

Ben laid his hand on it.

The mechanism pushed back.

Not with muscle; his palm stayed flat against the alloy. The Force behind the stone resisted him the way a sealed hatch resisted pressure from the other side. Amber light bloomed in the recess, scanning, measuring. Ben reached for the technique he’d been trained in, the calm centered presence that was supposed to read as Jedi to any lock calibrated for the Order’s signature.

The amber deepened. Held.

Refused.

Ben’s jaw tightened. He tried again, pressing the modern Jedi signature harder, and the resistance sharpened into something close to repulsion. Not hostile. Selective. It knew what it wanted, and he wasn’t it.

“Problem?” Desh asked.

“It doesn’t like me.”

“First time for everything.”

Ben didn’t answer. His hand stayed on the stone, and he could feel the mechanism’s pulse under his palm—a steady beat that wasn’t his own, patient and utterly indifferent to his frustration. Possibilities ran through his head: force the lock, find another entry, go back to the ship and call for help that wouldn’t arrive in time. None of them sat right.

The thing in the wall wasn’t fighting him. It was waiting. That mattered, even if he couldn’t have said why.

He stopped trying to push.

His palm flattened, and he reached deeper—not into the training, but into the older current under it. The signature that had nothing to do with the Temple or the Council or the decades of practice separating a modern Jedi Knight from the era that had built this place. The thing his father carried. The thing his grandfather had carried before the dark took him.

The amber light flickered.

Ben felt a shift in the Force—not a surge, just recognition. Whatever lived in the wall acknowledged him. That didn’t make it comfortable. It made it worse, in a way, because the recognition was so specific. Like a lock deciding the key in front of it had been forged long before its owner knew what it was for.

The light went green.

“Huh,” Desh said. “It likes you now.”

“It doesn’t like me. It knows what I am.”

“Not the same thing?”

“No.”

The fortress door ground open.

The sound was low and dragging, stone on stone, the first movement in a thousand years. The mechanism didn’t so much turn as groan, a deep note Ben felt in his ribs before he heard it. Air spilled out through the opening—air sealed since the Jedi Lords had walked the Outer Rim, carrying a stale weight his lungs registered at once, and under it something else.

Something alive.

Ben pulled his hand away. The green light stayed steady, waiting.

Desh was already drawing a glow rod from his belt. The beam cut into the dark beyond the door, picking out a corridor of the same dark alloy, walls bare, floor level. No debris. No collapse. The fortress had been sealed, but it hadn’t been abandoned. Someone had closed it and expected it to stay closed.

“Defensible position,” Desh said. His voice had gone flat, the tone he used for threat assessments and mission briefs. “Kill-box design. Whoever built this expected trouble from outside.”

“And inside.”

Desh swept the glow rod again. “Yeah. That too.”

Ben reached into the Force ahead of them. Not pushing—he’d learned not to shove at whatever this was—just opening himself to the dark beyond the threshold. What came back wasn’t blank. It was shaped, directed, almost architectural. The thing in the mountain wasn’t a wound or a void. It was structured, channeled through rock like current through a conduit, and it was moving.

Something was alive in here. Something that had been waiting a very long time.

“We don’t have an extraction plan,” Desh said.

“I know.”

“If this goes sideways, we’re buried in a mountain on an uncharted world with no beacon and no backup.”

“I know that too.”

Desh looked at him. The glow rod held steady, but his expression changed—not into protest, but into the stillness that meant he’d made his choice and was waiting for Ben to make his.

“You still want to go in?”

Ben stared into the dark beyond the door. The pressure pressed at his awareness, concentrated and patient. The seal was coming apart on a clock nobody else could hear, and whatever waited inside this mountain was the reason they’d come.

“Yeah,” he said. “I do.”

Desh nodded once. “Then let’s go.”

## Scene 04.02

Level 1 was a command post, and it had been waiting for someone to wake it.

The corridor from the entry opened into a broad chamber cut out of the mountain’s heart, the ceiling vanishing into shadow above the reach of Ben’s glow rod. Banks of consoles lined the walls—dark alloy, recessed screens, interfaces built for hands that had been dust for a millennium. The air carried the same stale weight as the entry corridor, but beneath it ran a low thrumming, felt more than heard, like the mountain had a pulse.

Residual power. After a thousand years, something was still alive in the walls.

“The comm gear’s dead,” Desh said. He had moved to the nearest console cluster, his own glow rod sweeping across the interfaces. “Power couplings are cold. Whatever broadcast array they had, it’s been offline since before the Republic was a thing.”

Ben crossed to the central monitoring station. It was larger than the others, its surface angled toward a standing operator, and unlike the dead comm equipment it held a faint warmth. Not heat—presence. The Force stirred around it, sluggish and thin, the way current moved through a circuit that had nearly spent itself.

He laid his palm flat on the console.

The metal was cool, but not cold. Waiting.

“Careful,” Desh said.

“I’m not pushing. Just listening.”

The Force here was different from the pressure outside. That had been a weight, a held breath, a seal straining against what it contained. This was finer—a web of connections, most broken, a few still holding. Ben closed his eyes and let his awareness sink into the console’s residual field. It felt like touching a spiderweb in the dark: most of the strands snapped at the slightest contact, brittle with age, but three held. And one of them was screaming.

He opened his eyes and drew his hand back.

“What?” Desh asked.

“It’s still tracking something.”

Ben reached into the console with a targeted pulse—not a shove, just a nudge, the Force equivalent of tapping a stuck gauge. The surface flickered. Amber light bled through the dust on the display screen, resolving into a grid of coordinates and status indicators. The hum in the walls deepened a fraction, the residual power answering the first active contact in centuries.

The screen stabilized.

It showed a network map—twelve nodes scattered across a sector of the Outer Rim, each marked with a location code and a status indicator. Ben’s eyes moved over them before he thought to stop them, his tactical training slotting the data into place before his conscious mind caught up.

Nine nodes were dark. Dead. Their indicators sat flat and black, the coordinates grayed out.

Three nodes glowed amber—active, but degraded. One of them was the fortress itself; Ben recognized the location code from the approach vector Desh had plotted.

And one node was flashing.

White, urgent, cycling at half-second intervals. The location code beside it was tagged with a timestamp—sixty-three days ago, give or take the drift in the system chronometer. The status line read a single word in the same archaic script as the perimeter facility’s warning.

BREACH.

Ben’s breath caught. Not dramatically—just a hitch, a half-beat pause before his lungs resumed. His hand was still on the console, and through the metal he could feel the strain in the system, the way the network’s remaining field pulled toward the flashing node like a wound tugging at stitches.

The wonder he’d felt a moment ago—the satisfaction of making something ancient answer—evaporated. In its place was something colder.

The fortress wasn’t a tomb. It was a control center. And whatever it had been built to control was failing.

“Ben.”

Desh’s voice was flat. Not calm; he’d already looked at the display, taken it apart in his head, and moved on to practicalities. Ben turned.

Desh had his datapad out, the glow rod clamped between his teeth while he worked. He was photographing every console, every interface, every status indicator in the room. His movements were precise, methodical, the same rhythm Ben had watched him use in a dozen threat assessments during their GAG year. Cataloguing. Documenting. Preparing.

His jaw was tight.

“Exits,” Desh said around the glow rod. He pulled it free and pointed with it toward the far wall, where a broad archway opened onto a descending stairwell. “That’s the only way down. The entry corridor’s the only way back up. If that stairwell seals behind us, we’re digging through a mountain.”

“You’ve been counting exits since we walked in.”

“Since before we walked in. The approach plateau’s a kill-box. The entry corridor’s a choke point. This room’s a command post with one way in and one way out.” He tucked the glow rod under his arm and kept working the datapad. “Whoever built this place expected trouble from outside and inside. I’m just figuring out which one is more likely to find us first.”

Ben looked back at the display. The BREACH node kept flashing, steady and urgent, a distress call that had been running for two months without anyone hearing it.

“The one that got out,” he said.

“That’s my guess.”

Desh didn’t ask what the network was for. He didn’t ask what the nodes represented, or what BREACH meant, or whether the sixty-three-day timestamp was accurate or a sensor error. He was already calculating how to get out, because that was what Desh did—assess the threat, mark the exits, keep people alive. The questions could wait. The exits couldn’t.

Ben felt the weight of that silence. Desh’s fear wasn’t loud—it never was—but it was there in the set of his shoulders and the economy of his movements. He wanted to leave. He was staying because Ben was staying, and that was a debt Ben didn’t know how to pay back.

“We’re not leaving yet,” Ben said.

“I know.”

“The network’s failing. That node—”

“I saw it.”

“If the rest of the system goes the same way—”

“Ben.” Desh lowered the datapad. His expression was unreadable, but his voice changed, not into protest but into the tone he used when he was about to say something Ben wouldn’t like. “You don’t know what the system is. You don’t know what happens if it fails. You’re reading a map you can’t translate and heading toward the thing that’s flashing because it’s flashing.”

“I’m heading toward it because something’s alive down there.”

The words came out before Ben had fully shaped the thought. He’d felt it when he reached into the console—not just the web of broken connections, but something beyond them. Deeper. Warmer. The Force in the lower levels wasn’t deadened the way the upper fortress was. It was moving.

Desh studied him for a long moment. Then he tucked the datapad into his belt and pulled the glow rod free again.

“Alive how?”

“I don’t know yet.”

“That’s not comforting.”

“It’s not supposed to be.”

The stairwell waited at the far end of the chamber, beyond the archive door that had refused Ben’s first attempt. He’d tried the lock while Desh catalogued the consoles—the same amber-light rejection, the same selective indifference. The archive’s deeper material was sealed against a modern Jedi signature, and Ben didn’t have time to argue with it. The living things were below, not behind it.

He crossed the room. The archway opened onto a spiral stairwell cut from the same dark alloy as the walls, descending into blue-tinged emergency lighting that brightened as it dropped. The glow was cold and clinical, the kind of light meant to keep working when everything else had failed.

The air changed at the threshold.

Warmer. More charged. The stale weight of the upper levels gave way to something denser, almost humid, as if the mountain had been holding its breath for a thousand years and was finally starting to exhale. Ben felt it on his skin before he registered it in the Force—a prickle at the back of his neck, a faint pressure in his sinuses.

And underneath the physical sensation, something else.

The Force down there was alive.

Not the strained pull of the monitoring network or the deadened pressure of the seal. This was different. Three distinct rhythms pulsed in the blue-lit dark below, each one separate and clear, the way individual heartbeats cut through a stethoscope pressed to a chest.

One was disciplined. Steady, measured, the cadence of someone who had spent a lifetime in military drill and carried it with him now.

One was heavy. Slower, deeper, weighted with something Ben couldn’t name—fatigue, maybe, or a burden that had settled into the bones.

One was clipped. Tight, controlled, the Force equivalent of someone holding very still and waiting for the next blow.

Ben’s hand found the railing. The metal was cool, solid, grounding.

People. Living people, sealed below this mountain for a thousand years, and somehow still breathing.

“Three of them,” he said.

Desh had moved up beside him. He wasn’t looking at the stairwell—he was watching Ben’s face, reading the reaction Ben couldn’t quite hide. “Three what?”
