“Signatures. In the Force. They’re—” He stopped. *Alive* didn’t cover it. *Waiting* came closer, but it still missed the patience in those rhythms, the sense that whatever was down there had been holding position so long that waiting had become a way of being.

“They’re what?” Desh asked.

Ben didn’t answer. He was already starting down, the blue light climbing toward him, the three rhythms pulsing in the dark below like a summons he hadn’t known he was answering.

## Scene 04.03

The stairwell ended.

Ben stepped through the archway and stopped with one hand still curled around the cool alloy rail. The chamber opened around him in a ring—twelve recessed bays around a central shaft that dropped into blue-lit darkness. Emergency strips burned along floor and ceiling, steady and cold, the sort of light that could keep shining for a thousand years with nobody left to notice.

Most of the bays were dark.

He counted them before he meant to. Habit did it first; training did it second. Eight dark. Three with a faint blue flicker behind their transparisteel seals, the stasis fields cycling low. One bay stood open, its seal shattered outward, the interior scored with gouges that caught the emergency light in long silver slashes.

The three living signatures he’d felt from the stairwell pulsed in time with those flickers. Disciplined. Heavy. Clipped. Distinct, each one a person sealed behind a meter of transparisteel and a thousand years of silence.

The eight dark bays read as absence. Not empty—sealed shut, cut off. The Force around them was flat, ended, like a corridor that ran straight into a blast door. Whatever had been in those chambers was gone as far as he could feel.

“Twelve,” Ben said.

Desh moved past him, his glow rod sweeping over the nearest dark bay. He didn’t answer. He was already at work, already doing what he always did—photographing with his datapad, marking positions, building a tactical picture out of hard facts. His jaw had gone tight, the way it did when he was pushing through something he didn’t like.

Ben started around the ring. His footsteps rang off the curved walls and dropped into the central shaft. Every bay was the same—same size, same control panel set into the alloy beside the seal, same emergency indicators. The dark ones had been dark a long time. Dust lay on the transparisteel in a fine gray film, untouched.

The three lit bays were cleaner. The stasis fields kept the dust off, kept the seals clear, kept the occupants hanging in blue-tinged stillness. Ben couldn’t make out faces through the flicker—only humanoid shapes, motionless. The Force was clearer than his eyes. Disciplined. Heavy. Clipped. Three people who had been waiting a very long time.

He reached the open bay and stopped.

The transparisteel seal had been forced outward—not cracked, not split, but peeled back in long curls of alloy, the work of something patient and methodical. Deep parallel gouges scored the interior walls, radiating from the center of the bay toward the opening. Not random damage. Not panic. Something had worked at the seal from inside, weakening it layer by layer until the whole thing gave way.

The monitoring panel beside the bay was still live. Amber indicators blinked in the same urgent rhythm Ben had seen on Level 1. The breach timestamp sat in the corner of the screen, steady and damning.

Twenty-three standard days.

“Desh.”

“I see it.” Desh had come up beside him, datapad still raised. He lowered it and ran a gloved hand along one of the gouges in the bay wall. The touch was practical, measuring—like checking a blast pattern during their GAG year. “These run outward. Whatever did this started inside and worked free.”

“Twenty-three days ago.”

“Give or take the chronometer drift.” Desh straightened. His face stayed flat, but his shoulders had tightened. “Someone broke out. Recently.”

The words hit like a hatch sealing behind him. Ben looked from the three flickering bays to the open one and did the math. Twelve chambers. Eight dead. Three still sealed. One escapee already loose, already moving, with a three-week head start.

The seal hadn’t been failing. It had already failed. The only question was how far the damage had spread.

Ben’s hand found the monitoring panel. The interface answered his touch with the same grudging recognition the fortress’s other systems had shown—calibrated for another era, another kind of Jedi. The breach log scrolled across the screen in archaic script. Emergency containment failure. Structural integrity compromised. Occupant status: UNKNOWN.

Below that, a second set of indicators tracked the remaining three bays. All three glowed green—stable, sealed, the stasis fields holding at minimum power. The system had been built to favor the living chambers when power ran low. Someone had made that choice. Someone had decided three people alive mattered more than the lights.

Ben looked down into the central shaft. The blue emergency lighting fell away into shadow, but he could feel what waited below. Not a signature. Not a living presence. Something quieter. Settled. A note that had finished sounding.

“There’s another level,” he said.

Desh followed his gaze. “How far?”

“Not far.”

The spiral stairwell continued past the ring, tighter here, the steps narrower. Ben descended with one hand on the inner wall, the alloy cold and smooth under his palm. The air turned stiller as he dropped, the recycled tang of the upper levels giving way to something drier, older. The hum of the stasis fields faded behind him and another vibration took its place—the low thrum of conduits carrying power from somewhere below.

Level 4 was one chamber, smaller than the ring above, built around a central console that looked like the heart of the fortress’s monitoring network. Conduits ran from the walls into the base, thick cables bundled together and sealed with alloy clamps. The emergency lighting here glowed dimmer, amber instead of blue, as if the system had fed every spare watt to the chambers above.

Gavran sat at the console.

His hands rested on the control surfaces, fingers still curved around the interface edges. His posture was not peaceful; it was settled, the way a man looked when he had done what he could and then stopped moving. The same dry cold preserved him, skin drawn tight over bone, robes faded to gray. He had been old when he died. Old and alone, sealed inside a mountain on an uncharted world, holding a system together with will and whatever power the fortress had left.

The monitoring log still glowed on the console display. The last entry trailed off mid-sentence—a status update on the anchor network, the script growing less precise toward the end, as if the hand writing it had been losing strength. The final word ended in a line of static.

Ben stood there and looked at the body. The grief didn’t come as sorrow. It came as weight—a pressure in his chest that had nothing to do with the Force and everything to do with what one man had done. Gavran had stayed. He had held the seal, kept the three survivors alive, kept the system running while the anchors died one by one and the power bled away and the breach warning blinked unanswered on the panel above. He had held it until he couldn’t.

And now Ben was standing in his place.

“Ben.” Desh’s voice came from the stairwell. He hadn’t come all the way down; he stood on the steps with one hand on the railing, posture still locked in the controlled readiness he’d carried since they entered the fortress. “The three chambers up there. What do we do about them?”

Ben looked at the console. At Gavran’s hands. At the log that would never finish itself.

“I don’t know yet.”

He went back up to the ring. The three lit bays flickered in their steady rhythm, the stasis fields humming at the edge of hearing. Ben stopped at the nearest one—the disciplined signature, steady and measured, the cadence of someone who’d spent a lifetime in military drill. The control panel beside the seal was simple: a status display, a power indicator, and a single activation surface.

He touched it.

The stasis field answered with a low hum, the blue light brightening a fraction. The signature behind the transparisteel pulsed once—not movement, not waking, but acknowledgment. A held breath, aware that someone stood on the other side.

The other two signatures answered a moment later. Heavy. Clipped. Three people sealed for a thousand years, and they knew he was there.

Ben kept his hand on the control. The hum moved through his palm, into his wrist, up the bones of his arm. The weight of the choice settled into his shoulders—not the decision itself, not yet, but the knowledge that it belonged to him. Three people. Three lives. Three chances to learn what had happened here, or three more bodies to add to the eight already dark.

Behind him, the empty bay’s emergency indicators kept blinking. Twenty-three days. The thing that had broken out was already walking. Already acting. Already somewhere in the galaxy, doing whatever a thousand-year-old prisoner did with freedom.

Ben pulled his hand back. The stasis field dimmed to its normal pulse. The three signatures sank back into waiting.

The choice could wait. It had to. There was too much he didn’t know, too much he hadn’t seen. But waiting carried its own cost. Every hour spent figuring it out was an hour the escapee had to use.

He turned from the chambers and looked at Desh. “We need to go back up. I need the rest of the monitoring data.”

Desh nodded. He didn’t ask about the three survivors. He didn’t ask about Gavran. He was already turning toward the stairwell, already calculating the route back to the command post, already doing what he always did—assessing the threat, marking the exits, keeping people alive.

Ben took one last look at the ring. Twelve chambers. Eight dark. Three flickering. One empty.

Below, in the amber-lit silence of Level 4, Gavran’s body sat at the nexus of the conduit network. His hands were on the control surfaces. His last monitoring log entry trailed off mid-sentence.

He had held this together until he couldn’t.

# Chapter 5

## Scene 05.01

Ben’s hand closed around the release lever.

The metal was cold. He’d half expected warmth—the way the monitoring panels had warmed under his touch, the way the fortress systems had answered with grudging recognition. But the lever stayed cold in his palm, resisting for an instant before the mechanism gave. Then it moved, and the sound that followed was not mechanical.

It was breath.

The first chamber’s stasis field dissolved with a long, low hiss, like pressure finally giving after a thousand years. Blue light shimmered behind the transparisteel, flared once, and went out. The seal irised open. Cold air spilled into the ring, carrying a smell Ben couldn’t place: dry, faintly metallic, like old stone after rain.

The woman inside opened her eyes.

She didn’t blink. She didn’t hesitate. She came out of the chamber in one smooth motion, legs folding under her, weight dropping into a combat crouch. One hand cut toward her hip before her fingers closed on empty air. No sidearm. No rank tab. Her hand stayed there for a full second, as if she could make the missing weapon appear by will alone, before her gaze found Ben’s face.

Her presence hit him a moment later. Sharp. Disciplined. Not the smooth, settled imprint he’d felt from modern Jedi, but something harder, shaped by older training. The Force around her felt like a blade laid flat on a table: still, but ready to be snatched up. Under the discipline lay another tension, the weight of someone who had learned to obey orders she didn’t agree with.

She straightened with a soldier’s precision.

“Identify yourself.”

Her voice was rough from a millennium of silence, but the syntax was intact. Complete sentences. Formal address. She didn’t ask where she was or what had happened. She asked for identification and chain of command, because command language was the only solid ground she had left.

Ben released the lever. “Ben Skywalker. Jedi Knight.”

Her eyes narrowed, not at the name, but at the title. At the way he wore it. She was reading him the way she’d read a battlefield, measuring posture and equipment and the weight of his presence in the Force, and whatever she found didn’t fit what she expected.

“Jedi,” she said. The word carried both recognition and question. “Whose command?”

“The New Jedi Order.”

The second chamber’s field failed before she could answer.

The man who came out was taller than Ben had expected, broad through the shoulders, his robes the same faded gray as the fortress walls. He staggered on the threshold, one hand snapping to the doorframe, and for an instant his weight hung there the way a climber’s does at the end of a long ascent.

Then he looked past Ben. Past the woman. His eyes swept the ring, counting.

His presence reached Ben like heat from a furnace door. Warm. Heavy. It settled into the room the way a body settles into a chair after a long day—not relaxed, exactly, but fully present. There was something deep in it that Ben couldn’t name. Not dark. Not light. Just settled, like old stone. Like grief carried long enough to become part of the one carrying it.

“Eight,” the man said. His voice was steadier than the woman’s, a low baritone with an archaic courtesy in the vowels. “Eight dark. Three lit.” He straightened, still holding the frame. “And one open.”

The woman turned her head. Her eyes went to the empty bay—the peeled alloy, the silver gouges catching the emergency light—and her jaw tightened. She didn’t speak.

Behind him, Ben heard Desh shift, a small scrape of weight changing stance. Desh had taken up position near the stairwell, out of the immediate line of approach, and he hadn’t said a word since Ben pulled the lever. That was training. That was still the GAG year living in his bones: don’t spook the assets, don’t complicate the contact, let the point man work.

The woman’s eyes came back to Ben. Her assessment was done, and whatever she’d found hadn’t settled her.

“You’re Jedi-trained,” she said, “but not of my era. Your presence is…” She paused, searching. “Thinner. Quieter. The Force doesn’t move the same way around you.”

Ben didn’t answer. He was still feeling the man’s presence, still feeling how sharply it cut against the woman’s edge. Two signatures. Two completely different histories, two different kinds of training, and both of them had just woken a thousand years beyond everything they knew.

The woman took a step forward, her hand still at her hip where the absent sidearm should have been.

“What year is it?”

The question hit like a fist. Ben had known it was coming. He’d known it since he touched the release lever. That didn’t make the answer any easier to give.

He told her.

The number hung in the cold air. The woman took it in through her body first—her spine straightened a fraction, her shoulders squared, the way a soldier’s body received a casualty report. The man took it differently. His hand tightened on the frame, and his gaze went to the dark chambers. Something moved behind his face that Ben couldn’t read.

The third chamber’s field dissolved.

The man who stepped out was smaller than the others, lean, dark-haired, his robes cut closer to the body. He didn’t stagger. He didn’t speak. He found the wall with his shoulders and stopped, back against the alloy, eyes sweeping the ring in one efficient pass: chambers, exits, personnel, threats.

His presence was the quietest of the three. Clipped. Guarded. It didn’t project; it drew inward, the way a man drew his coat tight against cold. Ben felt the shape of it without being able to pin it down: a signature trained into efficiency by someone who had learned that visibility was dangerous, that being sensed was a liability, that Sith instructors noticed strong opinions and punished them.

The silence stretched.

The woman—she still hadn’t given her name—was holding the year in her posture, doing the private math of what a thousand years had cost her. The tall man had stopped counting chambers and was looking at Ben with an expression that wasn’t quite curiosity. The third man hadn’t moved from the wall.

Ben realized he was holding his breath. He let it out slowly.

The woman spoke first.

“My name is Sera Varik. I was field lieutenant under Gavran’s command at Veranthos.” The words came out formal, almost ritual, like a soldier reporting for duty. “These men are—”

“Torin Hal.” The tall man released the frame and straightened fully. There was an archaic courtesy in his tone, but something steadier beneath it. “Campaign commander. I knew Gavran. I knew what he was trying to do here.” His eyes flicked to the empty bay. “I didn’t know it would come to this.”

The third man said nothing.

Ben looked at him. The man looked back. Dark eyes, unreadable, and they didn’t drop. It wasn’t a challenge. It was waiting. The way someone waited who had been taught that speaking first was a mistake, that answers came when ordered, that survival depended on knowing when to stay invisible.

“Your name,” Ben said.

A pause. Then, quiet and flat: “Kael Drenn.”

Nothing else. No rank. No unit. No explanation for why a Sith-trained conscript was sealed in a Jedi containment ring. Just the name, delivered in a voice shaped by obedience drills and survival kata, and then silence.

Torin Hal looked at the eight dark chambers, then at the empty one, then at the emergency indicators blinking amber on the panels. His presence shifted—the warmth in it flickered, like a flame disturbed by an opening door, and steadied again.

“Who got out?” he said quietly.

The question was for the room—for Sera, who was still holding the year in her shoulders, and for Kael, who was still pressed to the wall, and for the eight dark bays that had stayed dark far too long.

Ben didn’t answer. He was looking at the empty chamber—the peeled alloy, the silver gouges, the blinking amber warning—and doing his own arithmetic. Twelve chambers. Eight dead. Three survivors. One escapee with a three-week head start.

Sera turned from the empty bay and looked at Ben. Her eyes were the color of old steel, and they held the same question Torin had asked, though she didn’t voice it. She was waiting for Ben to answer, because Ben was the one who had woken them, and that made him the one who owed them the truth.

Torin was still looking at the empty chamber. His presence had settled again, but something lay under it now. Pressure, maybe. The tension of a man who had just counted the dead and found one missing.

“Who got out?” he said again.

Ben met his eyes. The answer was heavier than the year, heavier than the eight dark bays, heavier than the weight of three people waking a millennium beyond everything they knew.

“We don’t know yet,” he said. “But we’re going to find out.”

## Scene 05.02

Sera crossed to the archive console without asking permission.

Her stride was shorter than Ben expected—compact, efficient, the gait of someone who had spent years threading ship corridors and command decks alike. She didn’t bother with the console’s markings. Her fingers found the activation points from memory: three presses in sequence across a panel that had been dead for a millennium, and the console hummed awake under her touch.

Ben’s chest tightened. The sound was too clean, too ready, as if the archive had been waiting for exactly those fingers. He’d spent an hour on Level 2 before waking the survivors, trying every access protocol his Order had taught him, and the console had given him nothing but amber standby lights and administrative lockouts in a register he couldn’t read. Now Sera was three keystrokes in, and the main display was already resolving into a command index.

Desh shifted near the doorway. Ben didn’t turn, but he felt it anyway: weight redistributed, the easy stillness of a man who had learned to disappear in rooms where something important was happening.

“Veranthos containment protocol,” Sera said. Her voice was flat, a field report forced through discipline. “Gavran’s seal architecture. The index is here.”

She didn’t elaborate. She didn’t have to. The words hit Ben like a door slamming shut—the abstract unease he’d been chasing since the survey run was suddenly concrete, archived, and named by someone who had lived inside it.

Torin stepped forward before Ben could answer. His hand moved over the display with the same thousand-year-old familiarity, pulling files into the holographic field, and Ben’s skin registered the strangeness before his mind did. Not the Force—there was still that, pressing at the edges of his awareness like a note held a hair flat. This was something else. A man moving through a room he’d helped build, touching controls he’d last touched when the Old Republic was still an experiment.

“Twelve chambers in the ring,” Torin said, his voice carrying the warm baritone Ben had heard in the stasis bay. “Gavran was specific. Twelve anchors, one central keystone—himself. Thirteen total, if you count the man who designed the lock.” He paused, something flickering behind his expression. “We called it containment. Gavran called it penance. The distinction mattered more to him than it did to the rest of us.”

Ben’s focus narrowed. The public timeline rose in his mind without effort—the Seventh Battle of Ruusan, the aftermath of the New Sith Wars, the Reformations that dissolved the Army of Light, ended the Jedi Lords, centralized training, and bound the Order more closely to Republic and Judicial oversight. He’d learned it from his father’s Order, from the records Luke had rebuilt after the Purge, from the history every Jedi Knight was supposed to carry. The Veranthos index predated all of it.

“The Reformations came after,” Ben said. The words felt slow in his mouth, a sentence assembling itself while his mind still reached for the edges of it. “After Ruusan. After the New Sith Wars. The public reforms—the Army of Light, the Jedi Lords, the training changes, the oversight—those were real. They happened.”

Sera kept her eyes on the display. “I don’t know what happened after Ruusan. I was sealed before the aftermath.” The words came out clipped and exact, a soldier reporting facts she had no room left to turn over. “I know what Gavran built at Veranthos. Containment. Protocol. Seals. Human cost. The rest is your history, not mine.”

Ben heard the line she’d drawn. Not evasion. Discipline. She was stopping at the edge of what she could testify to and refusing to pretend otherwise.

Kael moved.

It was the first voluntary thing Ben had seen him do since the stasis chamber opened. He reached past Sera—not quickly, not aggressively, just a lean figure in close-cut robes extending one hand toward the display—and tapped a designation on the Veranthos index. The text shifted. An archaic administrative term gave way to another.

“Keeper,” Kael said. His voice was quiet, spare, the same register he’d used when he gave his name in the ring. “Not architect. Gavran was the keeper. Custodian of the protocol. He didn’t design the seal. He engineered it from something older.”

He stepped back. No explanation. No glance at Torin or Sera. Just the retreat to the wall, arms loose at his sides, and the silence he left behind weighed more than the sentence.

Ben felt the correction land before he sorted out the meaning. Kael’s movement—the reach, the tap, the step back—read as a person coming into focus. The monk’s brevity landed harder than explanation would have. He had been waiting for something worth saying, and the distinction between architect and keeper was worth saying to him in a way nothing else had been.

Torin’s expression changed. Something passed across his face—surprise, maybe, or recognition of a detail he’d missed a thousand years ago—and then it was gone, replaced by that steady warmth. “He’s right,” Torin said quietly. “Gavran was the keeper. I’d forgotten he used that word.”

Sera finally turned from the console. Her eyes met Ben’s, and her composure stayed absolute—the discipline of someone who had learned to deliver hard facts without breaking stride.

“Private penance and containment,” she said. “That’s what the Veranthos protocol was. Not public reform. Not legislation. A concealed layer under what came after. Gavran buried it because he couldn’t live with what we’d done, and he couldn’t let anyone else know we’d done it.”

The sentence landed cleanly. No apology. No cushioning. Just the institutional fact, delivered in a voice shaped by command reports and battlefield triage, and Ben felt the connection strike before his intellect could keep up.

The public Ruusan Reformations were real. The Army of Light had dissolved. The Jedi Lords had ended. Training had centralized under Republic and Judicial oversight. All of that was true—his father’s Order had taught it, the archives confirmed it, the modern Jedi Order was built on it. But beneath that public history, carried in silence for a thousand years, was a concealed penance. A containment system built from twelve living anchors and one central keystone. A wound the Order had chosen to bury instead of name.

The drop through Ben’s center of gravity felt almost physical. His mind raced ahead: if the private protocol was penance, what was it penance for? What had Gavran done at Veranthos that required twelve people to be sealed alive? What had the old Order chosen to hide so completely that even the history his father had rebuilt after the Purge didn’t mention it?

He looked at the three survivors. Sera, still at the console with her shoulders squared and one hand at her hip where the absent sidearm should have been—the instrument of precision, used by a system that had weaponized her discipline. Torin, whose warmth carried responsibility without softness, the keeper of narrative texture who had been voluntary in a way the others hadn’t been. Kael, still near the wall, whose first contribution had been a correction—the quiet voice that had waited a thousand years to say: you’re naming him wrong.

The archive around them was no longer a room full of records. It was testimony. Three people who had been made into infrastructure, now standing in the room where their making had been documented, and the penance remained unnamed.

Ben did not know what to do with that yet. He only knew the history his Order had inherited was true but incomplete, and the missing piece was the one that mattered most.

## Scene 05.03

The monitoring station on Level 1 was a narrow slot carved into the fortress wall, lit by a single display that flickered amber with the exhaustion of systems that had outlived their makers by centuries. Ben stood close enough to feel the panel’s heat on his face, while the recycled air left the back of his neck cold.

The logs for Chamber Seven crawled across the screen in archaic administrative Basic, clipped and procedural, their timestamps pegged to a calendar that had been obsolete before the Republic had its name. He read by shape more than by word, letting the headings and spacing tell him what the glyphs refused to.

The readable portion ended twenty-three standard days ago.

Ben stopped the scroll. The cursor blinked at the break, and beyond it the display dissolved into scrambled blocks and warped timestamps, the visual equivalent of static. Not wear. Not the slow decay of dying storage. The break began cleanly at a precise point and ran on from there, as if something had reached into the record and yanked the thread free.

“Desh.”

Desh was already at the secondary terminal, his hands moving over a panel that had never been built for someone who couldn’t touch the Force. He had found the access paths anyway, the way he found most things—by treating a locked system as sequence, not permission.

“I see it,” Desh said. “Clean cut. Twenty-three days. The rest is slag.”

“Accident?”

“The sort that knows exactly where to stop being readable.” Desh tapped a line of text Ben couldn’t see from where he stood. “Terminal access logs for this chamber show activity after the break. Someone was still working the system after the monitoring went blind. Different access pattern—careful, repeat visits. They knew what they wanted.”

Ben felt the weight of that settle across his shoulders before the rest of him caught up. Not an escape. An operation. Someone had broken out of a stasis chamber built to hold Force-sensitives for millennia, and their first move had been to sit down at a terminal and start reading.

“What did they access?”

“Navigation records. Supply inventories. The fortress schematic.” Desh kept his tone flat, the way he did when things were bad enough to leave no room for emphasis. “They were planning a trip.”

The word hung in the recycled air. Trip. Desh had chosen it on purpose—a plain word for something that was anything but plain, the way an intelligence man used understatement to carry more weight than he wanted his voice to show.

Ben turned from the monitor. Sera had come into the chamber behind him without making a sound. Her attention was already on the walls.

The damage inside the empty chamber wasn’t frantic scoring from someone who had panicked and clawed their way out. It was orderly. Layers removed in repeating patterns, the work of someone who had spent centuries worrying at a prison one careful pass at a time, patient as water on stone.

Sera’s hand stopped an inch from the wall. She didn’t touch it. Her fingers hovered over a section where the erosion had bitten through to the structural alloy beneath the surface coating, and Ben saw her precision falter.

Only for a heartbeat. Her shoulders tightened, her jaw set, and then the discipline was back in place—a soldier’s control, hard-won and polished to a shine. She lowered her hand.

“Sera.”

She shook her head once. Not no. Not yet. Not here. The kind of gesture that said she needed one more look before she put the thing into words.

Ben didn’t push. The silence between them took on the weight her voice had refused to carry, and he let it stand.

Kael was farther back, near the doorway, his arms loose at his sides. He was looking at the same marks Sera had studied, but his expression had gone flatter, the look of someone who recognized another captive’s discipline and didn’t forgive what it had become. He said nothing. The silence around him changed texture anyway. Not ignorance. Recognition held back.

Ben filed that away.

Torin stood at the doorway. He hadn’t come inside; he leaned one shoulder against the frame, arms crossed, casual in a way that still managed to feel motionless. His gaze was on the erosion marks, and there was something warm in his expression.

“You have to admire the patience,” Torin said.

The words were easy, conversational, the kind of observation he’d been making since waking—the storyteller’s habit of finding the human angle in any room, of putting shape around a thing so everyone else could breathe.

But the inflection was off.

Ben caught it the way a musician catches a note a hair flat—not enough to stop the tune, not enough to name on the spot, but enough to make the back of his neck tighten. A warmth that sat one degree away from where it should have been. As if Torin were describing not a threat, but something he understood.

The dissonance passed before Ben could pin it down. Torin was still in the doorway, still wearing that easy half-smile, and the moment had already moved on. Ben didn’t try to explain it to himself. He filed it the way he filed sensor glitches that might matter later and might not mean anything at all.

He turned back to the display.

“Overlay the access timestamps on the fortress schematic.”

Desh worked the terminal. The map bloomed across the screen—Level 3, the stasis ring, the corridors running upward toward the landing bays. The timestamps gathered into a pattern that told the story without a name attached to it. Chamber Seven. Terminal access on Level 3. Supply stores on Level 2. The landing bay. Then nothing.

“Twenty-three days,” Ben said. The words came out steadier than he felt. “They’ve had twenty-three days.”

“Minimum,” Desh said. “If the logs went blind when they left, we don’t know what they’ve been doing since. Could be longer.”

The map sat there, and the pattern was methodical. The escapee hadn’t fled in a blind rush. They had prepared. Studied the fortress. Taken what they needed. Walked out with the calm of someone who knew exactly where they were going.

The direction was clear. The Corridor.

Ben’s breath evened out. His mind was already running ahead—the next step, the next move, the thing that had to happen now because the alternative was standing still while someone else got farther away.

“Pull every monitoring packet from the ring,” he said. “Every chamber, every timestamp, every access log you can reconstruct. I want to know when they started, what they touched, and whether there’s anything in the corrupted data we can recover.”

Desh nodded once. He didn’t ask when Ben wanted it. The answer was already in the room.

“First light,” Ben said.

The weight of what he knew, and what he still didn’t, settled across his shoulders. An escapee without a name. Twenty-three standard days of silence on the logs. Torin’s one inflection that he couldn’t quite place, sitting at the edge of his awareness like a note held a fraction too long.

He still didn’t have a name. He had a direction. And the direction was already ahead of them.

## Scene 05.04

The command table in the Steady Returns’ common area had been built for two people to review survey data over caf. Ben spread the fortress material across it anyway—Corridor maps overlapping at the corners, seal readings spilling onto the bench seat, the chamber file from Level 3 laid beside his left elbow with a name-shaped blank where the occupant designation should have been.

He arranged everything the way he’d been trained to arrange intelligence before a briefing: terrain first, then threat, then the thing nobody knew yet. The blank in the chamber file was that thing. His eyes kept snagging on it.

Sera and Torin had already taken over the bench along the starboard bulkhead without discussing it. The archive materials they’d hauled up from the fortress—data cards in formats the Steady Returns’ reader barely recognized, handwritten notes on flimsi that had outlasted the civilization that made it—moved between their hands in a rhythm that looked practiced.

It wasn’t practiced. It was muscle memory. Ben watched Torin pass Sera a stack of cards without looking up, watched her take them with her left hand while her right kept sorting, and understood he was watching two people who had done this exact work together a thousand years ago. The ease of it gave the compartment a warmer feel, which somehow made it smaller.

Sera’s sorting was exact, each item placed where it belonged in a system Ben could see but not yet read. Torin’s arrangement held the same information, but grouped by what followed what, the way a storyteller lined facts up so they would land in order. Ben let the difference sit. He didn’t comment.

He didn’t need to. The choice was already being made around him. These two were organizing for departure, not for archive. They were assuming he would follow.

His fingers tightened on the datapad.

Desh climbed up from the engineering crawlspace with grease on his knuckles and a data slate in his off hand. He looked at the spread across the command table, looked at Ben, and didn’t ask the question Ben could see forming behind his eyes. Instead he set the slate on the only clear corner of the table and tapped the display.

“The corrupted packets from Chamber Seven,” Desh said. “It isn’t random degradation. Somebody ran a selective wipe before they left—targeted their own designation, the access timestamps for the last six months, and the supply manifest for what they took. Everything else is intact or recoverable.”

Ben’s eyes went to the blank in the chamber file. “Can you pull it back?”

“Not here. The fortress terminals are too degraded for that much processing. But if I queue it through the Steady Returns’ response-quadrant architecture overnight, the packet structure should resolve by morning.” Desh paused. “The name will be there.”

Overnight. The word sat in the recycled air like a weight. Ben did the math without letting himself look at it too closely. Luke’s reply window on the survey report was open now. If he waited for the name, he’d be waiting past the point where his father would expect confirmation that the survey was still on schedule. If he lifted now, he could send a partial report before the window closed—enough to explain the delay, not enough to—

He cut the thought off before it finished.

“First light,” Ben said. “We lift at first light. The packets can run while we’re in transit.”

Desh nodded once. He didn’t ask about the report. The way he didn’t ask was information in itself—the calm of a man who recognized another man’s decision not to call home.

Kael had been standing near the cockpit access ladder for the last several minutes. Ben hadn’t seen him come in. He was braced against the bulkhead with his arms loose at his sides, watching the archive sorting with the flat expression he wore when he was cataloguing things he didn’t intend to discuss. His eyes moved from Sera’s hands to Torin’s, tracking the rhythm the way Ben had tracked it, and whatever he found there made him look away.

“Twenty-three days,” Kael said.

It wasn’t a question.

“Minimum,” Ben said.

Kael took that in. His face didn’t change, but something in his shoulders shifted—a tightening that read less like fear than recognition. He didn’t spell it out. Ben didn’t expect him to.

Sera finished sorting and stood. The movement was economical, no wasted motion, the way she did everything. She looked at the command table, at the Corridor maps overlapping at the corners, at the chamber file with its blank.

“You’re not waiting for the name,” she said.

“No.”

She held his gaze a beat longer than necessary. Her expression stayed unreadable—not hostile, not warm, the look of a soldier weighing a commander’s decision without volunteering approval. Then she nodded once, the same exact motion, and turned back to the archive materials.

Torin was already watching Ben. His expression was easier than Sera’s, the warmth still there, but something behind it was heavier than the moment required—attention that seemed to carry more than curiosity.

“You’re composing the report now,” Torin said.

It wasn’t a question either.

Ben didn’t answer. He picked up the datapad and walked to the cockpit.

The pilot’s seat was familiar in a way the rest of the compartment wasn’t. Ben sat down and brought up the comm interface. The standard survey update template filled the screen—location, status, findings, recommendations. He filled in the location. He filled in the status: delay, survey complication, continued investigation. He did not fill in the findings.

His thumb hovered over transmit.

The Force around him was still deadened, still spread through the wider sky like a pressure he couldn’t pin down. It gave him nothing. No warning, no confirmation, none of the subtle cues he’d learned to trust since he was old enough to tell when something was off. The disturbance was just there, present and spread and unreliable.

He sent the report before he could change his mind.

The confirmation chime was soft. Ben sat in the pilot’s seat for another thirty seconds, staring at the blank screen and pointedly not thinking about what he’d just done. Then he stood and went back to the common area.

Desh was already in the copilot’s seat when Ben returned to the cockpit an hour later. The fortress plateau stretched below the viewport in predawn gray, the mountain rising behind it like a tomb marker. Ben ran the preflight checks with his hands while Desh called out the numbers in a flat voice that didn’t invite conversation.

The engines caught. The Steady Returns shuddered—the extra weight of five passengers registering in the drive pitch—and then the ship climbed, the plateau shrinking, the fortress resolving into what it had always been: a lid over something still failing underneath.

Sera came forward as they cleared atmosphere. She stood behind the copilot’s seat with one hand resting on the headrest, not touching the controls, eyes on the sensor display. Her fingers hovered near the interface without making contact—close enough to read the data, restrained enough to acknowledge that the ship was not hers.

Torin stood in the cockpit doorway. He didn’t try to come forward. He leaned against the frame the way he’d leaned against the chamber doorway on Level 3, arms crossed, watching the starfield open ahead of them with an expression Ben couldn’t read.

Kael had taken the jump seat in the common area. Through the cockpit access Ben could see him sitting with his hands on his knees, deliberately still, like a man keeping himself from touching things he didn’t understand.

The Steady Returns leveled out. The Corridor stretched ahead on the nav display, a chain of anchor nodes through uncharted space. Somewhere along that chain, twenty-three standard days ahead of them, an escapee without a name was moving.

The monitoring packets were already queued. By morning they would resolve. By morning Ben would know who he was chasing.

He set the heading and engaged the hyperdrive.

# Chapter 6

## Scene 06.01

The common area smelled of caf gone stale an hour ago and the sharp ozone tang of a ship pushing its life support harder than it liked. Desh was already at the table when Ben ducked through the hatch, datapad in hand, the look on his face saying he'd been up before the shift alarm.

“We have a name,” Desh said.

Ben stopped halfway through the hatch. Across the table, Sera kept her eyes on the archive materials spread in front of her. Her hands continued their neat sorting—the same precise rhythm she’d had since the fortress—but her shoulders had gone rigid.

Desh set the datapad on the table and angled it so the display faced the room. The reconstructed monitoring file had cleaned itself into readable lines, the corrupted packets stitched back together. The first clear line in the header read: *Chamber Seven — Primary Occupant — Darth Veraine.*

“Darth,” Ben said. The word sat heavier than he expected.

“Brotherhood of Darkness,” Desh said. “Alchemist. Strategist. Gavran flagged her as high-risk.” He tapped the display. “She didn’t just break out. She ate through the chamber from the inside. Took weeks. The system logged the damage in real time, and nobody was there to watch it.”

Ben’s mind was already running—vectors, timing, threat angles, every assumption from the last two days shifting under his feet. The name changed the shape of the hunt. A Brotherhood alchemist with weeks of lead time and a thousand years of patience wasn’t just ahead of them. She was building something.

He pulled out the chair across from Desh and sat. His body had already moved into readiness without asking him—weight forward, hands flat on the table, the stance his mother had taught him before he was old enough to know why.

Sera still hadn’t spoken.

The silence stretched. Torin, standing near the galley with his arms crossed in that old-soldier way of his—close enough to help, never in the way—watched Sera with an expression Ben couldn’t read. Kael had folded himself into the corner by the nav display, hands loose at his sides, face blank enough to be a wall.

“Sera,” Ben said.

She looked up. Her composure held—the same blade-edged discipline she’d worn since the moment she woke—but she didn’t fill the gap between them. She left it there, and Ben understood she was waiting to see what he asked before deciding what she’d give.

“You know her,” Ben said. Not a question.

“I know of her.” Each word came out measured, set exactly where she wanted it. “She was captured at the Ryloth front. Gavran assessed her personally. He classified her as—” She stopped. Her jaw tightened, the smallest motion, gone almost before Ben caught it. “He classified her as a strategic asset.”

“Meaning what?”

Sera met his eyes. “Meaning he believed she was too valuable to execute and too dangerous to contain by ordinary means. So he made her an anchor instead.”

The word settled into the recycled air like a chunk of durasteel. Ben thought of the chamber on Level 3—the claw marks in the alloy, the peeled-back metal, the patient destruction of a prison built to last forever. Veraine had been awake in there. Partly aware, Desh had said. Feeling her Force presence drained away as structural support for a thousand years.

And then she’d decided to leave.

Kael moved. Only a shift of weight, his shoulders settling against the bulkhead, but Ben caught it the way a sensor caught an outlier. Kael’s face had flickered. Something had crossed it when Sera said the name, recognition there and gone, smoothed over into the studied blankness he wore like armor.

Ben went still. He didn’t need to analyze it. The fact was there, plain as a sensor reading: Kael Drenn knew Darth Veraine, and in the space of a heartbeat he’d chosen not to let anyone see that he knew.

Ben filed it away. He didn’t press. He didn’t forget.

Torin cleared his throat. The sound was warm and easy, like most things Torin did until they weren’t. “Well,” he said, “at least now we know who we’re chasing. That’s better than chasing a shape.”

“It’s better,” Desh said, “if you assume knowing the name tells you what she’ll do next.”

“Does it?” Ben asked Sera.

She didn’t answer. Her hands had stopped sorting. She was looking at the datapad, at the name in the header, and her silence answered for her—a warning that whatever Veraine could do, Sera wasn’t going to spell it out in front of people she’d known for less than three days.

Torin shifted his weight, filling the silence without breaking it. “The Brotherhood trained their strategists to think in campaigns, not battles. If Veraine’s moving along the Corridor, she isn’t running. She’s executing a plan she’s had a thousand years to refine.” He paused. “We should assume she knows we’re coming.”

“Why?” Desh asked.

“Because she’s a strategist. Strategists don’t assume an escape goes unnoticed.” Torin’s smile was thin, but real. “They assume pursuit and prepare for it. It’s what I’d do.”

The room took that in. Ben felt everyone’s attention shift—not fracture, exactly, but realign around the weight of the name the way a ship adjusted its load to a new vector. They were no longer chasing an escaped anchor. They were chasing Darth Veraine, Brotherhood alchemist, with weeks of lead time and a plan she’d been sharpening since before the Republic existed.

Sera stood. The movement was economical, all clean lines and no waste. She crossed to the cockpit access ladder without a word. Ben watched her go, tracking the tension in her shoulders, the way her hand reached for the rung and stopped.

She’d reached for a control that wasn’t there. Her fingers closed on blank hull plating where a console used to be—a gesture so automatic she seemed not to notice it. Her body remembered a ship that hadn’t flown in a thousand years.

Ben looked away before she could catch him staring. The motion was too private, too raw, and he understood all at once what they were asking these people to do. They’d woken in a galaxy that had moved on without them, aboard a ship built by a civilization they didn’t know, hunting an enemy who’d had weeks to prepare while they were still learning where the galley was.

Sera’s hand dropped from the hull. She turned back to the room, and her composure settled into place again—the blade-edge discipline, the soldier’s mask. But something in her posture had changed. She was looking at Ben with an expression he hadn’t seen before.

“The Corridor route,” she said. “You’re following the anchor nodes.”

“Yes.”

“Veraine will expect that. She knows the network better than we do—she’s had weeks to study it while we’ve been catching up.” Sera folded her arms. “What’s your alternative when she forces us off the expected path?”

It was a test. Ben recognized it the way he’d recognized his mother’s tests—the question that wasn’t really a question, the challenge tucked inside the words. Sera was asking him to prove he’d thought beyond the obvious answer.

“The anchor nodes are the only reliable navigation points through uncharted space,” Ben said. “If we leave the chain, we’re flying blind. So we don’t leave it. We use it differently.”

“How?”

“She’s expecting pursuit along the nodes in sequence. One, two, three. What she won’t expect is somebody who knows a modern hyperdrive can skip a node if the vector is clean enough.” Ben dragged the nav display toward him and rotated it so Sera could see. “The *Steady Returns* has a Class One hyperdrive and a nav computer that’s been mapping these anomalies since we entered the Corridor. If Veraine’s laying traps at the expected stops, we go around them. Hit the nodes she isn’t defending and work backward.”

Sera studied the display. Her expression didn’t change, but something in her stance eased—a half degree, maybe less.

“That could work,” she said.

It wasn’t praise. It wasn’t even approval. But it was the first time she’d looked at him like he might be worth following.

Torin found him in the corridor afterward. Ben had stepped out to check the sensor relay—or that was the excuse he gave himself. Really, he’d needed thirty seconds of quiet to sort through the way the room had rearranged itself around Veraine’s name, the way Kael had gone blank, the way Sera had tested him and found him not entirely useless.

“Your father’s name carries weight even in our time,” Torin said.

Ben turned. Torin leaned against the bulkhead, arms crossed, wearing the same easy posture he’d had since the fortress. But his eyes were sharper than the rest of him—measuring, the way a commander measured another commander’s work.

“Luke Skywalker,” Torin said. “Gavran spoke of him. Not by name—we didn’t have the name—but he knew the bloodline would continue. He believed someone would come.” He paused. “He was right.”

The compliment felt genuine. Torin’s warmth always did, which made it hard to read. But underneath it, faint as a sensor ghost, Ben caught something else—an appraisal that hadn’t been spoken, a judgment left hanging in the air.

“Thank you,” Ben said.

Torin clasped his shoulder. The grip was measured—a commander’s grip, the sort that carried approval and expectation in equal parts. Then he let go and headed back toward the common area, leaving Ben alone in the corridor with the hum of the hyperdrive and the weight of a name he was only beginning to understand.

## Scene 06.02

The cargo corridor was cold this early in the ship’s cycle, the life support still climbing out of its night setting. Ben had come aft to check a sensor relay that had flickered twice during the last watch—minor enough that Desh didn’t need waking, annoying enough to deserve thirty seconds of attention.

He stopped three paces from the corner.

Kael Drenn stood in the open space between the cargo lockers and the engineering crawlspace hatch, moving through a sequence Ben didn’t recognize. It wasn’t a combat form; the motions were too small, too contained, wrists and shoulders and spine doing the work instead of full-body extension. Kael’s eyes were open but unfocused, fixed on a point on the bulkhead that wasn’t there. His breathing ran in a pattern Ben couldn’t place: two short inhales, one long exhale, then a pause of exactly three heartbeats before it began again.

Then it ended. Kael’s hands dropped to his sides, his weight settled, and his face returned to the same studied blankness he wore in the common area. The whole thing had taken maybe forty seconds.

Ben stepped back before Kael could turn. The sensor relay could wait. Some things got broken if you acknowledged them wrong, and Ben had learned young—from his mother, from the GAG, from every hard lesson that had taught him to read a room—that private rituals people built to survive bad systems weren’t meant for witnesses.

He went back toward the common area with the image still hot behind his eyes: the precision of it, the economy, the way Kael had moved like someone who’d learned that doing one thing exactly right every morning was the only way to keep the rest of the day from killing you.

---

The common area smelled of field rations that tasted like nothing any of the survivors remembered. Desh had laid out the breakfast packets with his usual practical efficiency—no ceremony, just fuel arranged where people could reach it. Sera sat at the table with her back to the bulkhead, posture unchanged from the fortress: spine straight, hands visible, sightlines clear to both exits. Torin had taken the corner by the galley, nursing a cup of caf with the air of a man who’d been awake for hours and saw no reason to advertise it.

Kael arrived last, slipping into the corner by the nav display without a sound. His face gave nothing away. Ben didn’t look at him any longer than he looked at anyone else.

“The Reformations,” Sera said. She hadn’t touched her rations. “You said they were penance. I want to know what the galaxy was told they were for.”

Not a question. A demand, delivered in the clipped formal register she wore like armor. Ben pulled up the galactic history archive on his datapad—the public version, the one every student on Coruscant learned from—and slid it across the table to Sera.

“The official story,” he said. “The Republic centralized after Ruusan. The Jedi Order was restructured. The military was disbanded. The galaxy was told it was about preventing another Army of Light.”

Sera’s eyes moved down the screen. She read without reacting, the same blade-edged discipline she’d had since the moment she woke, but her hands had gone still on the table. Not attention. Holding very tight to something that wanted to move.

“The Republic didn’t centralize,” she said. “It conquered. The Outer Rim was annexed. The Jedi Lords were abolished and their territories absorbed. The military wasn’t disbanded—it was replaced with a Judicial Department that answered to the Core.” She looked up. “The galaxy was told a lie.”

“Yes.”

The word settled into the small space like a shut door. Sera didn’t flinch. She set the datapad down with the same precision she brought to everything, and then she went quiet in a way that made the silence feel like a third person at the table.

Torin broke it first. “A thousand years of peace built on a lie,” he said, his tone almost conversational. “That’s not the worst foundation I’ve seen. The Sith built empires on worse.”

Ben caught himself watching Torin’s ease the way he’d caught himself watching Kael’s discipline—the reflex to look for what was underneath it. Torin had taken the Reformations answer with the same warmth he brought to everything, and that warmth was starting to feel like a door Ben couldn’t see through.

Desh snorted and poured himself more caf. “The Sith also lost,” he said. “So maybe we don’t use them as the benchmark.”

Torin laughed—a real laugh, warm and easy—and Ben felt the room loosen a fraction. Desh had that effect. He’d had it in the GAG too, the ability to cut tension without cutting anyone down, and Ben was grateful for it in a way he didn’t know how to say.

Kael hadn’t moved from his corner. He still hadn’t spoken. He was watching the room the way Ben had watched him in the cargo corridor—tracking, filing, giving nothing back.

“What about the Sith worlds?” Kael asked.

The question came out flat, almost idle, the way Kael said everything. But Ben had seen the morning discipline. He knew what economy looked like on this man, and the question was too casual by half.

“Which ones?” Ben asked.

“The ones the Brotherhood took conscripts from.” Kael’s eyes met his. “The small ones. The ones nobody wrote histories about.”

Ben pulled up the database. The query took longer than it should have—the nav computer had to reach through three archival layers to find records that old, and the connection through the thin place was patchy. The screen flickered. Then it resolved.

Nothing.

He ran the search again. The parameters were right—the sector, the era, the Brotherhood’s known recruitment patterns. The database had records for the major systems, the fortress worlds, the battle sites that had made it into the histories. But the planet Kael was asking about wasn’t there. It had been too small, too rural, too insignificant to leave a mark on a thousand years of galactic record.

“There’s nothing,” Ben said. “The planet was too small for surviving records.”

Kael’s expression didn’t change. Not a flicker, not a tightening, not the tiny shift Ben had learned to read in the cargo corridor. But the line of his shoulders did something Ben couldn’t name—a settling, a small adjustment, the movement of a body absorbing weight it had expected and still wasn’t ready for.

The silence after that was different from Sera’s. Sera’s silence was a wall. Kael’s was a room with the door closed, and Ben could feel it taking up more space than Kael’s body did.

He didn’t speak. There was nothing to say. The database had answered, and the answer was erasure—a homeworld so completely gone that even its name had been lost, and Kael was the only one left in the galaxy who remembered it.

Desh set his caf down. He didn’t look at Kael—Desh was too good at reading rooms to stare at someone who’d just been gutted—but he nudged the ration packets half an inch closer to Kael’s side of the table. A small motion. Barely there. The sort of thing you did for someone you couldn’t help with words.

Sera’s stillness had changed. She was looking at Kael now, and her expression had shifted into something Ben hadn’t seen before—not pity, not quite, but recognition. She knew what it cost to lose a world. She’d lost hers too, even if it still had a name in the records.

Torin cleared his throat. “The Brotherhood took conscripts from a dozen worlds,” he said, and his voice was gentler than Ben had heard it. “Most of them were small. Agricultural. Places the Sith didn’t think mattered except as recruitment pools.” He paused. “I was stationed near one of them for six months. The people there didn’t care about the war. They just wanted to be left alone.”

It wasn’t an answer. It wasn’t comfort. But it was something—a shared history, a witness, a reminder that Kael’s world had existed even if the galaxy had forgotten it.

Kael didn’t respond. He reached for one of the ration packets, opened it with the same economical precision he’d brought to the morning discipline, and began to eat. The motion was so ordinary, so deliberately normal, that Ben felt his chest tighten.

The conversation didn’t resume. The common area had become smaller, the bulkheads closer, the recycled air thicker. But the silence was different now. It wasn’t five people avoiding each other. It was five people who had just registered what a thousand years had cost, who didn’t have words for it yet, and who were still sitting at the same table anyway.

Torin caught Ben’s eye across the common area. He nodded—a single measured motion, the nod of a commander acknowledging a subordinate who’d handled something difficult well. There was approval in it, or something close enough to pass for approval. There was also assessment, the look of a commander deciding where someone fit in a structure that hadn’t been built yet.

Ben nodded back. He wasn’t sure what he was agreeing to. He wasn’t sure whether he should be reassured that Torin was already thinking in those terms, or concerned that the group’s fragile empathy was already being threaded through a command structure none of them had asked for.

The hyperdrive hummed. The rations sat half-eaten. Kael’s silence still filled more of the room than his body did, but the others were sitting inside it now instead of around it, and that was something.

It wasn’t enough. Ben knew that. But it was what they had.

## Scene 06.03

The cockpit of the *Steady Returns* had never felt cramped. It did now, with all of them packed around the nav display and the blue glow of the Corridor route washing over faces Ben had just seen at their worst.

Desh had the display up in a clean, hard-edged hologram above the console. Fourteen nodes hung in the air, each marked with the same practical precision he brought to everything. Red for dead. Amber for degraded. Green for active. One node near the far end of the chain pulsed white, a steady flash that meant recent activity and nothing more useful than that.

“Three green,” Desh said, tapping the air beside the display. “The rest are dead or barely reading. The white one was visited in the last seventy-two hours. Someone’s moving.”

Ben’s attention narrowed to the map. The problem had a shape now. Fourteen points, one route, and a clear sign they weren’t the first ones through. Beneath the clean lines of the projection, unease tugged at him. This wasn’t just a damaged seal. Veraine had been here.

Sera leaned forward in the copilot’s seat, eyes tracking the node positions. She’d been quiet since the common area, still carrying the Reformations answer in the room like an object none of them had found a place for yet. The map drew her back into herself. Ben saw it in the straightening of her back, the shift from hurt to evaluation, the discipline clicking back into place like a latched blade.

“Fourteen nodes,” she said. “Spiral pattern, centered on Veranthos. The seal’s architecture is radial—each node pushes pressure outward from the center.” She traced the route with one finger, blue light spilling across her knuckles. “If we check each node in order, we can see the pattern of degradation before we commit to the center. We don’t run blind into a system that’s been failing for a thousand years.”

Torin, still in the doorway, shook his head. He had the same loose stance he’d worn in the common area, but the easy warmth in his voice had gone thin. “Systematic assessment takes time we don’t have. Veraine’s already hit one node in the last three days. She’s ahead of us, and every hour we spend examining dead relays is an hour she uses to get farther ahead.” He nodded at the flashing white node. “We go straight at Veranthos. The seal’s heart is where this ends. Everything else is delay.”

“Delay is information,” Sera said, not looking at him. “Charging straight at the center without understanding the pattern is how you walk into a trap.”

“It’s also how you catch someone before she finishes what she started.”

Their voices overlapped, Sera clipped and exact, Torin faster now, urgency gathering behind each word. Ben felt the cockpit tighten around them. Not the Force—not yet—just the human strain of two people who were both right, and who could not both get their way.

Kael spoke from the corner by the nav display.

“We don’t know why she’s moving.”

The words landed flat and clean, the way Kael’s often did. Not an argument. Not a vote. Just the missing piece nobody else had said aloud. Sera stopped mid-gesture. Torin’s jaw flexed.

Kael didn’t add anything else. He didn’t need to. Veraine had been visiting nodes, but none of them knew what she was doing there. Torin’s route assumed she was driving for the center. Sera’s route assumed there was a pattern to read first. Both were bets placed on a woman whose purpose they still couldn’t see.

Ben’s shoulders tightened. The display still glowed between them, the fourteen nodes waiting, and the three voices in the cockpit had stopped being ideas and started being a decision he had to make. Sera wanted caution. Torin wanted speed. Kael had pointed at the hole in both plans and left the rest to him, which was as close as Kael came to saying it.

Ben wanted the clean answer his father’s Jedi Order always seemed to find in the stories. But this wasn’t a story, and there wasn’t a single right line through it. Synthesis meant choosing one path and living with the fact that the other two had reasons to exist.

Desh glanced at him from the pilot’s seat. The look said he’d seen officers make harder calls with less to work on. No push. Just readiness.

Ben reached for the nav computer.

“Sera’s route,” he said.

Torin started to speak.

“Because we don’t know why she’s moving.”

Torin shut his mouth.

Ben keyed the coordinates in. The computer took them with a soft chime, and the route settled into the display—Sera’s methodical arc through the Corridor, node by node, building a picture before the final run at Veranthos. The white node gave one last pulse and folded into the sequence.

“We check the nodes,” Ben said. “We gather data. We read the pattern.” He looked at Torin. “If Veraine’s doing something we haven’t guessed, rushing past her work to get to the center won’t catch her. It just means we arrive ignorant.”

Torin held his gaze for a beat longer than comfortable. Then he nodded, measured and reluctant and still somehow steady—the same kind of nod he’d given Ben in the common area, approval and assessment packed into one movement.

“Your call,” Torin said. Some of the warmth had come back into his voice, but not all of it. “I hope you’re right.”

Sera didn’t say anything. She didn’t need to. The route was her plan, but Ben had named Kael’s reason out loud, and the two of them had become something neither had built alone. She was looking at him with an expression he couldn’t pin down. Not gratitude. Not quite respect. More like surprise, sharpened by calculation.

Kael had already turned back to the display, face unreadable. But his shoulders had eased a fraction.

The pressure in the cockpit eased with them. Not gone—Torin’s urgency was still there, Sera’s caution still there, and the agreement between them was held together by the reasoning Ben had spoken aloud. Still, they were moving. That mattered.

The course shifted. The *Steady Returns* angled toward the first node, the hyperdrive humming under Ben’s feet. He set his attention on the space ahead and reached for the Force, looking for whatever waited there.

The Force answered from a step farther off than it should.

It wasn’t pain. It wasn’t a voice. It was a gap, a place where continuity should have been and wasn’t. Like putting your weight on a stair that sat a fraction too far forward. The sense of it ahead was thin, like fabric worn through.

Ben held the feeling for a second, then let it sit where it was. No interpretation. No diagnosis. Just the fact of it. He would have time later, if there was a later, to decide what it meant.

For now, he had a route to live with.

And if it was wrong, the failure would be his.

# Chapter 7

## Scene 07.01

The *Steady Returns* settled onto the battlefield world with a low thrum that bled away into silence. Through the cockpit viewport, Ben saw ground shaped by war and then left to fade—trenches softened into shallow dips, artillery berms worn down into grassy swells. A line of corroded machinery jutted from the soil a hundred meters out, half-swallowed by wild grass, the barrel of something that had once hurled fire now aimed at nothing.

Ben unstrapped and rose. Behind him, the others were already moving—Sera’s clipped footsteps on the deck plates, Torin’s heavier tread, Kael’s near-silent presence at the rear. Desh caught Ben’s eye from the pilot’s seat and gave a fractional nod. Ship secure. Systems nominal. All the things Desh said without wasting breath.

The ramp lowered. Ben’s boots hit soil that still carried the shape of thousand-year-old trenches.

He reached for the Force.

It answered from a step farther off than it should have.

The sensation struck at once, all body and no mercy. Not pain. Not darkness. A gap where there should have been continuity—like putting weight on ground that arrived a fraction late to meet him. His balance corrected before his mind caught up, his body reading a tilt his eyes couldn’t see. The green hillside ahead looked soft, almost peaceful. The Force beneath it felt wrong.

Ben let the sensation settle without pushing deeper. Every instinct urged him to extend, to reach until the offset resolved into something he could name. He didn’t. The half-beat delay he’d felt in the cockpit had been a warning. This was what it had been warning him about.

He moved clear of the ramp and let the others follow.

The bunker sat two hundred meters east, a low durasteel structure half-buried in the hillside. Ancient military architecture—utility without ornament, built to be ignored until someone needed to survive inside it. The entrance was a recessed blast door, still sealed, the control panel beside it dark.

Sera stopped at his shoulder. Her shoulders were tight, jaw set hard enough to show in profile. She scanned the hillside the way she’d scanned Gavran’s fortress, measuring threat vectors, marking exits that weren’t there. The thin place was getting to her; Ben could see it in the stiffness of her movement. She felt it as danger. That made sense. Sera had spent her life reading the Force as a tactical tool, and this place made that tool lie.

“The door’s still sealed,” she said. “Whoever came here didn’t use the front.”

“Or didn’t need to,” Ben said.

He dropped to one knee beside the control panel. The casing was corroded, but the internal leads were intact—old Jedi Lords tech, built to outlast the people who installed it. He laid his palm against the access plate and let the Force move through him, feeling for the mechanism inside.

The lock didn’t recognize him.

That was wrong. The bio-locks at Gavran’s fortress had opened for his Skywalker signature, grudgingly but open. This one sat dead under his touch, as if it had forgotten what it was supposed to answer to. The thin place had seeped into the machinery, Ben thought. Or the machinery had died with its anchor centuries ago, and no one had noticed.

He drew his lightsaber. One clean cut through the manual release, and the blast door groaned open on seized tracks.

Inside, the air was cold and dry, preserved as if the bunker had been waiting for someone who never came. Ben’s boots left prints in dust that had settled for centuries and had never been touched.

The corridor ran straight for thirty meters before opening into a central chamber. Stasis pods lined the walls—twelve of them, identical to the ring at Gavran’s fortress, arranged in a semicircle around a central console. All of them dark. All of them empty.

Ben moved to the nearest pod and brushed dust from the access panel. The readings were dead, the occupant’s vitals flatlined so long ago the timestamp had gone to nonsense. He checked the next pod. Same. The next. Same. Twelve anchors, all gone silent centuries before the seal started its final collapse.

“The anchor here died first,” Sera said. She stood at the central console, fingers moving over the interface with brisk precision, as if the machine might still be persuaded to talk. “The node’s been running on residual charge for at least three hundred years. It wasn’t enough to hold the seal, but it kept the environmental systems alive.” She paused, then looked up. “The installation records show recent access.”

Ben looked up. “How recent?”

“Weeks. Maybe days.” She tapped the console again, and the display flickered with a pale wash of corrupted text.

Ben crossed to her side. The wrongness of the place faded to a background drag as he focused on the console, the dust, the timeline. His hand hovered over the panel. Weeks. Not centuries. Not ruins. Recent. “Open the chamber records.”

Sera’s mouth thinned. “Already trying.”

The chamber door stood open a handspan, its seam dark with dust. Ben slipped inside, and the air changed at once—same cold, same dryness, but thicker with old filtration and sealed time. The stasis chamber was empty. The cradle at its center stood open, lifeless, the anchor that should have been there gone so long ago the absence felt built in.

He swept a hand across the rim and down the access panel, pushing dust aside. Fresh tool marks caught the dim light—bright scrapes on top of gray film that had lain untouched for centuries. His fingers stilled.

Sera made a low sound behind him. “That wasn’t the only access.”

Ben glanced over his shoulder. Torin had moved farther into the bunker than the others, almost loose-limbed, as if the narrow corridors and dead air bothered him less than the open hillside. Kael stood near the chamber threshold, face blanked out, his usual sharpness pulled flat. Sera had gone rigid enough to hurt just looking at her. Ben took it in without naming any of it. Sera’s tension made sense. Kael’s shuttered expression read like withdrawal. Torin’s ease didn’t. Not here. Not in a place that made every step feel half a beat off.

Ben looked back down at the panel. Fresh lines scored the metal. Recent access. Recent hands. The trail had gone from archive to present tense.

Someone had been here weeks ago.

## Scene 07.02

The records room lay behind a door that hadn’t been opened in centuries. Sera stopped before it and set both palms flat on the metal.

Ben realized what she was doing a half-second before his training fully caught up. The door was Force-locked—ancient work, layered sealing the modern Jedi studied as history instead of craft. He started to tell her they should find another route.

Sera’s fingers flexed once.

The lock gave way.

Not cracked. Not bypassed. It simply came apart under her touch the way a household latch yielded to someone who knew exactly where the pressure belonged. One moment the door hummed with contained energy. The next it was dead metal, and Sera was already pushing it open with the heel of her palm.

Ben felt the ease of it before he understood it. The domestic familiarity. The thousand-year muscle memory that hadn’t lost a step. A faint vertigo opened in his chest—the distance between what he’d been trained to expect and what he was seeing.

Sera stepped through without looking back. “The archive will be in the central vault. They always put it at the core.”

Ben followed her into the dark.

The records room was a round chamber sunk into the bedrock beneath the bunker. Holographic projectors ringed the walls, their emitters dull with age but still intact. A central console sat in the middle of the floor, its interface panel dark. The air smelled of cold stone and old filtration, the same preserved deadness that had filled the stasis chamber outside.

Desh lingered at the doorway, blaster loose in his hand. “I’ll watch the corridor. You two do the history.”

Ben nodded without looking back. He was already moving toward the console.

The thin place made concentration harder. His attention kept tugging slightly off-center, as if something in the room wanted to be seen from the edge of his vision. He forced himself back to the console. Its interface was archaic, data architecture from an era before modern systems had settled into standard formats, but the power couplings were still live. He found the activation sequence on the third try.

The projectors woke with a low hum. Blue-white light filled the chamber, resolving into text and diagrams that hung in the air like frozen smoke.

Ben scrolled through the first layer of records. Military logistics. Supply routes. Troop movements across a sector whose name meant nothing to him. The language was archaic Basic, the sentence structure formal enough that his eyes wanted to skip over it.

“This is all operational,” he said. “Where’s the technical documentation?”

Sera came to stand beside him. Her gaze moved over the holographic text with the speed of someone who’d been trained to read this format before she’d been trained to fight. “Second layer. The Jedi Lords compartmentalized their archives—surface records for logistics, deeper vaults for Force-work. The lock I opened wasn’t the only security.”

She reached past him and set her fingers on the console’s edge. Her eyes half-closed. Ben felt the Force move through her—not broad and warm, like his father’s presence, but narrow and exact. A blade, not a hand.

The display flickered. The logistics records vanished. New text bloomed into place.

Ben read the first line and felt the floor shift under him.

*Classification: Restricted. Clearance: Anchor-Command. Subject: Force-Lock Discipline and Containment Architecture.*

Sera started translating before he could ask. Her voice stayed steady, clinical without going cold, the way she might have briefed Gavran a thousand years ago. “The Jedi Lords developed Force-lock techniques as a military discipline. Not meditation. Not philosophy. Engineering.” She paused on the last word, and Ben heard the weight she put on it—the same word Torin had used in the thin place, the same framing that had made Sera’s jaw tighten. “They trained specialists to build barriers, seals, and containment fields using structured applications of the Force. It was a formal school. Ranked. Credentialed.”

Ben scrolled deeper. The records opened into diagrams—Force-flow patterns mapped onto architectural schematics, the same interlocking geometry he’d seen in the fortress. “This is what Gavran used to build the seal.”

“Yes.” Sera’s voice had gone quieter. “And this is what the Brotherhood studied to learn how to break it.”

She touched a line of text near the bottom of the display. The holograph shivered beneath her fingertip.

“The technique has a name,” she said. “They called it unweaving.”

The word hit Ben’s body a beat after it left her mouth. Not his mind—his chest, his hands, the place behind his ribs where the Force moved when he reached for it. Unweaving. The shape of the procedure opened in him like something he’d always known and never been taught. A pull. A separation. Thread from thread, layer from layer, the structure of the Force coming apart under deliberate pressure.

His breath caught. Not fear. Recognition.

“Read it,” he said.

Sera’s eyes tracked across the text. “Unweaving is a counter-lock procedure derived from the Force-lock discipline itself. The practitioner identifies the structural nodes of a constructed barrier and applies localized pressure to each node in sequence. The barrier does not break. It comes apart.” She stopped. Her hand had gone still on the console. “The technique was classified after Veranthos.”

Ben’s focus sharpened. The thin place’s pull seemed to tighten, drawing his attention toward the name like a hand on his shoulder. “Pull up the Veranthos entry.”

Sera’s fingers moved across the interface. The display shifted again.

Coordinates bloomed in the air—a location in the Darrpa Sector, deep in the chart-void that had been wiped from modern navigation records. Beneath the coordinates, a purpose statement: *Primary containment anchor. Seal keystone. Wound origin site.* Beneath that, in text that glowed amber instead of blue-white, a warning notation: *Do not approach without Anchor-Command authorization. Local field instability Class 5. Active degradation ongoing.*

The wound was real. It had a location. And Veraine was practicing the specific discipline that had made it.

Ben’s body tightened before his mind finished catching up. Urgency settled in his chest—not panic, but the cold clarity of someone who understood the scope of the problem. Veraine wasn’t flailing. She wasn’t improvising. She was executing a named procedure from a formal training tree that had been classified for a thousand years because of what it had done to Veranthos.

He looked at Sera.
