She was still reading the display, her profile sharp in the blue-white light, and he saw her clearly now—not as a historical relic or a liability, not as a burden he had tolerated because the mission needed her knowledge. She was the only person in this bunker who could have done what she’d just done. The only person on the *Steady Returns*. The only person in the modern Order.

“We need to go,” he said. “Now.”

## Scene 07.03

Ben hunched over his datapad and deleted another paragraph.

The thin place’s aftereffects were easing out of him now—the half-beat lag in his reflexes smoothing over, the wrongness draining from muscle and nerve like a headache backing off after too many hours under bad light. He’d been at the report for twenty minutes. It should have taken five.

*Survey continues. Encountered navigational anomaly consistent with previous readings. No change in bearing. Proceeding to next checkpoint.*

True. All of it. None of it the thing he needed to tell his father.

His thumb hovered over the next line—the one about the technique having a name now, about unweaving and Veranthos and a classified discipline buried for a thousand years because of what it had done to the wound they were flying toward. He’d written it three different ways. Deleted it three different ways. The fourth version sat on the screen like a charge.

He deleted that too.

The datapad’s chrono ticked over. Luke’s comm frequency sat open in the corner of the display, patient and waiting, the way his father’s silences always were. Ben had learned to read those silences before he’d learned much else. This one he was writing himself.

“You’re editing.”

Torin’s voice came from the doorway. Not loud. Not sharp. Just there, the way Torin was there—warm, easy, and carrying something underneath that Ben had been refusing to name since the thin place.

Ben didn’t turn. “Reports need editing.”

“Mm.” Torin leaned in the frame, arms crossed, comfortable in his own skin in a way that reminded Ben of old Jedi Knights in Temple recordings—the ones who’d been fighting so long that stillness had become armor. “That’s not what I asked.”

The question settled on top of Ben’s keyboard. He felt it there, a weight he couldn’t type around.

Torin didn’t push. He just stayed in the doorway, watching Ben not answer, and the silence stretched until it stopped being an evasion and started being something else—something neither man moved to fill.

Ben’s fingers stayed on the datapad. The cursor blinked at the end of his last line, waiting for him to type something he wasn’t going to type.

“What will you tell them?” Torin asked.

Not *are you lying*. Not *why are you lying*. Just the question that would cost Ben the most to answer, delivered in the same warm register Torin used for stories and for making the crew feel like a team.

Ben looked at him then. Torin’s expression was open, unguarded, the face of a man who’d asked because he genuinely wanted to know—not because he was building a case, not because he was waiting to catch Ben out. The warmth was real. So was whatever lay under it.

“I’ll tell them what they need to know,” Ben said.

It wasn’t an answer. Torin took it as one anyway. He pushed off the frame, easy and unhurried, and the weight of him backed away by inches—still there, still patient, still carrying the question Ben hadn’t answered.

“Fair enough,” Torin said, and left the doorway empty behind him.

Ben stared at the datapad. The cursor blinked. The comm frequency waited.

He sent the report.

Across the common area, Kael’s voice came low and precise—a monk’s sentence with no spare words.

“Desh. The Kolven-era disciplinary logs. Can you pull them?”

Ben caught it from the corner of his eye before his mind caught up. Kael stood near Desh’s station, hands loose at his sides, not asking permission. Not explaining why. One sentence, delivered in the same dry register Kael used for everything, then silence while Desh took it in.

Desh didn’t ask either. He looked up from his console, held Kael’s gaze for a beat—Ben saw the quick appraisal, the familiar operational calculation of a man who’d spent years reading people for danger—and nodded.

“Yeah,” Desh said. “Give me a minute.”

His datapad turned in his hands as he queued the pull. The motion was casual, practiced, the same way he’d hand someone a hydrospanner or check a coolant valve. No commentary. No follow-up. Just easy acceptance.

Kael waited, still and patient, the way he always waited—like someone who’d learned that the best way to stay unnoticed was to keep still until the room forgot he was there. Ben had noticed anyway. He’d noticed the target of the request, Kolven—one of those Corridor-era settlement sites with disciplinary records, not general history, not context. Something specific. Something Kael had decided to chase on his own.

Ben didn’t move toward them. He kept it as texture—Kael’s low voice, Desh’s nod, the datapad turning in his hands—and let it sit at the edge of his attention without claiming it. A man who was used now choosing to learn. A request that asked nothing of Ben and required no reply.

The ship hummed around them. Recycled air carried the faint tang of the thin place’s residue, already thinning, already becoming just another thing they’d flown through.

Desh brought up the nav display. The next node’s readings bloomed across the screen—degradation stacking into an uglier baseline, the Force-ahead signal intermittent and broken. Where the previous stop had felt offset, a note played a little flat, this one came in pieces. Gaps between the pieces. Silence where structure ought to have been.

Ben felt it before he finished reading the display. The wrongness tightening. The bearing pulling harder.

“Energy signature’s up,” Desh said. His voice was flat, operational, the tone he used when the news was bad and there was no point pretending otherwise. “Same profile as the last stop, but stronger. Much stronger.”

Ben looked at the readings. The concentration pattern was unmistakable—not a pass-through, not a survey, not someone moving fast and leaving traces. Someone had stopped here. Worked here. Spent time dismantling the seal’s infrastructure with the same patience and precision that had clawed through a stasis chamber from the inside.

Renewed determination settled into Ben’s chest. Not hope. Not confidence. Just readiness, cold and clear, the thing that came after the choice had already been made.

The next node was on approach. The Force ahead came in pieces.

And someone had been there waiting.

# Chapter 8

## Scene 08.01

The Force dropped out from under him.

Ben’s hands hit the cockpit armrests before the sensation had fully registered—something less like the clean hush after hyperspace and more like a presence breaking into strips, gone, back, gone again. Like a comm channel losing signal. Like reaching for a handhold and closing on air.

His chest tightened. His first impulse was to do something about it—change course, check the systems, pull up the nav readouts and find the fault he could fix—but there was nothing to fix. The ship was fine. The displays were fine. What was failing was the thing they’d been flying through for days, and it clearly had no interest in whether he was ready for it.

Behind him, Sera went still. Not composed stillness. Braced-against-a-moving-wall stillness. Her Force presence drew in tight, all hard edges and discipline, like she was locking down every instinct she had and remembering older, uglier lessons while she did it.

Kael didn’t speak. He’d been quiet before the thinning started; now he was quiet in a different way, the silence of someone who knew how to make himself smaller in a room where the wrong glance could get him killed. His eyes tracked the cockpit without seeming to. Ben caught the direction anyway. Kael was watching Torin.

Torin had settled into his seat.

Not braced. Not tense. Settled. His shoulders had dropped a fraction, his head tilted as if he were listening through the static. He looked less like a man enduring the zone than one easing into a chair he’d occupied for years. His Force presence, that warm, jagged thing Ben had been counting as stability since the fortress, shifted. The heaviness in it eased. Something in Torin relaxed into the thin zone as if it belonged there.

Ben’s stomach dipped before his mind caught up.

He couldn’t have said what was wrong about it. It wasn’t a thought yet, just his body sounding an alarm before language could catch up. His eyes found Torin anyway, and what he saw was a man listening to the gaps between the Force’s fragments with an expression that wasn’t fear and wasn’t strain and wasn’t anything Ben wanted to name.

“Ankhural called it the natural state,” Torin murmured.

His voice was low, almost absent, like he was noting weather he’d seen before. No lecture. No performance. Just the tone of someone who’d spent a long time somewhere like this and hadn’t forgotten what it had cost to leave.

The cockpit seemed to hold the words for a beat too long.

Sera’s face emptied of expression. Not blank—emptied, like a room cleared before a fight. She’d heard exactly what Ben had heard. Filed it somewhere colder.

Kael’s stillness sharpened. His eyes hadn’t left Torin, and now his attention had the edge of assessment. Ben had seen that look before—on Desh, usually, when things were about to go bad and the only question left was how fast.

Desh himself was at the nav station, hands steady on the console, doing what Desh did when the Force-users started getting strange: flying the ship and keeping his mouth shut. The numbers on his display flickered, degradation stacking into a nastier baseline, the signal from the next node arriving in pieces with holes between them.

“Zone’s thinning out,” Desh said. His voice was flat, practical. “Another two minutes, maybe three.”

Ben nodded without turning. His grip on the armrest hadn’t loosened.

The two minutes dragged.

The Force came back in fragments—a pulse of presence, then nothing, then a thread of something that almost felt normal before it frayed again. Ben kept trying to compensate for a lag that wasn’t in his body. Every time he reached and got silence, his chest clenched harder. Every time the Force returned, the relief lasted less.

Torin didn’t tense at all. His head stayed tilted, his breathing even, his presence in the Force easing with each gap—as if the thinning were stripping away something he’d carried since the fortress. Since before the fortress. Since a thousand years ago, in a war Ben was only beginning to understand.

The sensation in Ben’s gut didn’t bother becoming words.

The zone cleared.

The Force settled back into its normal density—still wrong in the deeper places, still tugging toward the bearing, but whole again. The half-beat hitch in Ben’s reflexes smoothed out. His chest loosened by degrees.

Sera rose without a word and left the cockpit.

Ben waited until her steps faded into the corridor, then pushed out of the pilot’s seat. His legs held. His hands were less reliable. He flexed them once, twice, and followed her.

She caught him in the corridor.

Her hand closed around his arm above the elbow—hard, deliberate, enough to leave marks. Ben stopped. Sera’s face was still emptied of expression, but up close he could see the tension in her jaw, the fine lines around her eyes that hadn’t been there before the thin place.

“Watch him,” she said.

Her voice stayed low. Measured. The same tone she used for battlefield orders and tactical calls.

“In the thin places. Watch what happens to his face.”

She didn’t say Torin’s name. She didn’t need to.

Ben felt the grip as anchor and warning at once. Sera’s hand was steady—steadier than his arm under it—and the pressure of her fingers was the sort of thing you didn’t forget. A message in the only language she trusted him to hear.

He met her eyes. “I saw it.”

“I know.” She let go. The marks stayed. “That’s why I’m telling you.”

She moved past him toward the common area, her steps even and unhurried, the same disciplined cadence she’d had since waking up a thousand years out of time. Ben stayed in the corridor a moment longer.

The ship hummed around him. Recycled air. The faint tang of the thin place fading from the bulkheads. Through the wall, he could hear Desh running post-transit checks and Kael’s silence—a different silence now, one that had already put Torin somewhere Ben didn’t want to look.

Torin’s calm voice still echoed in his head. Ankhural called it the natural state. The tone more than the words. The ease more than either.

Sera’s grip still lived in the muscle memory of his arm.

Ben flexed his hand once and headed back to the cockpit. He didn’t look at Torin when he sat down. He didn’t need to. The warning was in his arm and in his stomach and in the empty space Sera had left behind in the corridor, a space that now felt heavier than the thin place had.

## Scene 08.02

The galley was too small for five people and an argument, but they crammed in both anyway.

Sera had been eating in silence since they’d cleared the thin place, her fork moving with the same disciplined economy she brought to everything. Ben had watched her work through half a ration bar before Torin spoke, and the moment he did, her fork stopped.

“The technique isn’t sacred or profane,” Torin said, reaching for the water bulb beside his plate. His voice stayed easy, almost conversational. “It’s engineering. You have a structure under strain, you reinforce it. You have a wound that won’t close, you stitch it. The stitching isn’t the wound.”

Sera set her fork down.

Not dropped. Not quite placed. Set down with a precision that made Ben’s hand stop halfway to his own plate. The tines lined up with the edge of her tray, the handle straight, the whole gesture a warning all by itself.

“It’s a lobotomy,” she said.

Torin spread his hands on the table. Open. Reasonable. “It’s a technique that deadens Force-sensitivity in a local area. Whether that’s a lobotomy or a tourniquet depends on what you’re trying to stop.”

“You’re describing the Weave like machinery.”

“I’m describing it as something that can be damaged and repaired.”

Ben’s first instinct was to cut in before the room locked down any further, but he stayed where he was. The space between Sera and Torin had gone too still.

“That isn’t a neutral description,” Sera said. Her diction sharpened on every word. “The Weave is the work of the Force. You talk about it like a construction job.”

“I’m talking about something twelve people were sealed into chambers to maintain.” Torin’s warmth was still there, but thinner now, the humor gone from it. “If the work is sacred, what does that make the anchors?”

Kael hadn’t looked up from his plate. He ate the way he did everything else—efficiently, without waste. But there was a stillness in his shoulders that said he was listening to every word.

Desh stood in the galley doorway with his arms crossed, watching the table with the flat, assessing look of someone reading a room because the room mattered. He caught Ben’s eye for half a second. Said nothing.

Sera touched the handle of her fork without lifting it. “The anchors were a crime. You’re proposing to do it again.”

“I’m proposing to understand the tool well enough to know whether that’s true.” Torin kept his hands where they were, voice calm, making the argument sound plain. “The stitching exists. Refusing to look at how it was used doesn’t make it vanish.”

Sera turned toward Kael.

“And you?” she said. “You’ve been quiet since the fortress. What does the Brotherhood’s conscript think of the stitching?”

Kael’s fork paused.

Torin’s hands moved before Kael answered. Barely a movement at all, just a small turn outward that pulled the line of the exchange back to himself.

“Kael wasn’t in the chamber with us when Gavran made that decision,” Torin said. “Neither were you, Sera. You were already sealed by then.”

The redirect slid into place so smoothly Ben almost missed it. Sera’s question had been aimed at Kael; somehow it landed on Torin instead. His tone never changed. His posture never changed. He had simply taken the heat, and by the time he finished, the opening for Sera to press Kael had closed.

Kael resumed eating.

Ben noticed and filed it away without knowing what to do with it.

“The problem,” Sera said, and her voice had gone cold now, “is that you think the stitching is separate from what it does. That you can use the technique and stay clean. You can’t.”

Torin left his hands on the table. “And your position is that the Weave must be left untouched even where it’s already been used as a weapon.”

“It does not become less sacred because someone abused it.”

“No. But abuse doesn’t stop being real because the thing abused matters to you.”

For a second nobody spoke. The galley hummed around them, recycled air and the steady vibration under the deck plates.

Then the ship gave a hard little shudder.

It was enough to rattle utensils against trays and send a thin splash of water over the rim of Ben’s bulb onto his knuckles. Desh’s head snapped toward the corridor on reflex. From somewhere forward came the brief complaining whine of a panel taking the vibration badly, then settling.

Nobody got up. But the interruption cut across the argument just long enough to remind Ben they were still on a ship held together by parts, stress, and habit.

Desh pushed off the doorway, listened for another sound, then stayed where he was when none came.

Kael set down his fork.

The click against the tray was small, but it cut straight through the hum. He didn’t look at Sera or Torin right away. When he did, his expression hadn’t changed.

“You’re all describing the same cage.”

His voice was flat. Not heated, not weary, nothing anyone could get a grip on.

“Sera calls the cloth sacred and says anyone who touches it is a vandal. Torin calls it stitching and says anyone who won’t touch it is afraid. The Brotherhood called it a weapon and said anyone who wouldn’t use it was prey.” He took a sip from his water bulb and set it down. “Three ways of naming the bars. Same cage.”

Silence dropped over the table.

Sera’s hand stayed beside her fork. Torin’s were still spread on the table, but the reasonableness had gone out of the gesture, leaving only the shape. Desh looked from Kael to Ben and said nothing.

Ben didn’t have anything either.

The ship hummed. Food cooled on plates. Somewhere forward, the panel gave one last thin buzz and fell quiet.

Nobody answered, because nobody had a good response.

## Scene 08.03

Ben heard the exchange before he saw it.

Not the words—the crawlspace sat too far down the corridor for that, and the ship’s steady mechanical hum swallowed anything below a certain volume—but the rhythm. Two voices trading short, unhurried sentences. Not an argument. Not a briefing. Just two people talking about something concrete enough that neither had to raise a voice.

He slowed without meaning to. The corridor outside engineering was narrow enough that slowing meant stopping, his shoulder grazing the bulkhead while the hyperdrive throbbed through the deck plates under his boots. The crawlspace door stood half-open. Through the gap he could see Desh’s legs extending from the access panel, boots braced against the grating, the rest of him buried in the ship’s innards.

Kael stood in the doorway. Not hovering—he’d folded himself against the frame with the easy patience of someone who had been there for a while, arms crossed, head tipped slightly as he watched Desh work.

“—draw pattern,” Kael was saying, his voice carrying the same dry, economical register he’d used in the galley. “The power-cycle draw. It’s been fluctuating on a three-second interval since the last transit. Not enough to trip the warning system, but it’s consistent.”

Desh’s reply came from inside the crawlspace, muffled by machinery. “Three seconds exactly?”

“Three point two.”

A pause. The clink of a tool being set down on metal. Then Desh’s legs shifted, and he slid out of the access panel far enough to look up at Kael. His face was streaked with something dark—lubricant or coolant, Ben couldn’t tell from the corridor—and his expression held the same flat, measuring look he’d worn in the galley doorway.

“Show me,” Desh said.

Kael unfolded from the frame and stepped inside.

Ben stayed where he was. The corridor around him had gone quiet; the rest of the ship was empty, Sera and Torin having retreated to their own corners after the argument, the galley’s tension still bleeding away like heat from a cooling plate. He should keep moving. The cockpit was waiting. The nav-computer needed updating before they reached the next Corridor node.

He didn’t move.

Through the half-open door he watched Kael crouch beside the access panel, his hands moving over the diagnostic readout to indicate what he’d found. Desh listened without interrupting, head angled, eyes tracking whatever Kael showed him. The exchange was quick—a few sentences, a nod from Desh, and then Desh was sliding back into the crawlspace while Kael reached for the toolkit without being asked.

Ben felt the shift the way he’d learned to feel tactical changes in a firefight: not as a thought, but as a nudge at the back of his awareness that said something had changed. Kael had approached Desh. Not the other way around. Not because Desh had asked, not because Ben had arranged it, not because anyone had told Kael where he belonged or what he should contribute.

He’d just gone.

The crawlspace had been built for one person. Desh filled most of it—his shoulders, his toolkit, the sprawl of cabling and conduit housing that made up the ship’s power-distribution system. Kael folded himself into the remaining space with the economy of someone used to making room where there wasn’t any, his body fitting the gaps without hesitation, his hands already sorting through the open toolkit on the grating.

Ben had seen Kael fight. He’d seen him move through the fortress on WR-7741 with a predator’s efficiency, every motion measured, every step placed. This was different. The same precision, but without the tension that had sat under it since the moment he’d woken from stasis. His shoulders had dropped half an inch. His jaw had loosened. He was handing Desh tools before Desh asked, his hands moving with the rhythm of work he knew too well to think about.

“Hydrospanner,” Desh said.

Kael’s hand closed around the tool. Then stopped.

Not for long. Just a beat. The smallest hesitation, deliberate enough that Ben saw it even from the corridor, even through the half-open door. Kael’s fingers wrapped around the hydrospanner’s grip, and for a moment he didn’t move. His eyes stayed on the tool. His breathing stayed even. The ship hummed around them, the hyperdrive pulsed through the deck, and Kael held the hydrospanner like he was deciding something.

Then he passed it.

Desh took it without comment. His hand closed around the grip—the metal still warm from Kael’s palm—and reached back into the access panel to keep working.

Ben let out a breath he hadn’t realized he’d been holding.

The hydrospanner. That was the moment. Not the observation about the power-cycle draw, not the ease with which Kael had folded himself into the crawlspace—the tool. Desh had asked for it without thinking, the way he’d ask anyone working beside him, the way he’d asked Ben a hundred times on a hundred survey runs. And Kael had held it for that extra beat, that deliberate stillness, because no one had asked him for something that wasn’t about the Force since he’d woken from stasis.

No one had asked him for anything at all.

Ben pushed away from the bulkhead. The cockpit was waiting. The nav-computer needed updating. Whatever was happening in the crawlspace didn’t need him standing in the corridor tallying it like a mission report he’d never file.

He took one step toward the cockpit. Stopped.

Kael’s voice came through the door again, quieter this time, almost casual. “Does it bother you?”

Desh’s answer was muffled by the access panel. “Does what bother me?”

“The Force. The way they talk about it. The way they assume everyone in the room is having the same conversation.”

A pause. The sound of the hydrospanner turning, metal on metal. Then Desh’s voice, flat and dry: “The Force doesn’t fix a bad conduit. I don’t need it to.”

Kael made a sound that wasn’t quite a laugh—more an exhale, short and sharp, the noise someone made when they’d expected a different answer and didn’t get one.

Ben looked back through the doorway. Kael’s face was half-lit in the crawlspace’s dim glow, his mouth still shaped around that sound. Not a smile. Just the faintest lift at one corner, gone almost as soon as it appeared.

Almost.

Ben turned and walked toward the cockpit. The nav-computer was waiting. The next Corridor node was waiting. Whatever Kael and Desh were building in the crawlspace didn’t need him to name it.

The hydrospanner had done that already.

# Chapter 9

## Scene 09.01

Sera had carried the Codex case from the fortress herself. Ben had watched her lift it from the archive chamber, hands steady, face giving away nothing. She’d kept it sealed since then—through the debate in the galley, through the thin place, through every conversation that circled the thing inside without naming it.

Now she set it on the common-area table.

The case was plain enough: dark metal, worn at the edges, no markings Ben recognized. It could have held medical gear or navigation crystals. Around it, the Steady Returns’ common area stayed quiet—four acceleration couches around a table bolted to the deck, the hyperdrive’s low hum threading through the bulkheads until Ben had mostly stopped hearing it.

Desh stood near the corridor to the cockpit, arms crossed, wearing the same easy readiness he used when he’d decided a situation didn’t need his input yet. Kael had folded into the corner couch with his back to the bulkhead, watching Sera’s hands. Torin sat across from Ben, forearms on the table, his Force presence carrying that warm, jagged edge Ben had learned meant anticipation.

Sera didn’t speak. She put her thumb to the seal.

The lock gave with a soft hiss, like a breath released.

The air shifted.

Ben felt it before he understood it—a drop in the room’s balance, the way the air changed just ahead of a storm rolling in off the coast. His ears didn’t pop. Something deeper did. The Force around the table, which a moment before had been four distinct presences held in easy balance, went taut and still.

Sera lifted the fragment from the case.

It was smaller than Ben had expected. A slab of dark stone, about the length of his hand, its surface cut with characters he didn’t know. The lines were dense and precise, deep enough to hold shadow. Inside the case it had glimmered faintly, a residual glow that died the instant Sera’s fingers left it.

She set it on the table between them.

No one spoke. The hyperdrive’s hum seemed to pull back, or maybe the silence around the fragment had simply grown louder. Ben’s attention narrowed until the room’s edges felt far away—the couch under him, the recycled-air tang, the faint vibration in the deck plates all pushed behind something sharper and nearer.

The fragment was not inert. He could feel that now, the way he could feel a live circuit before he touched it. Not a presence. Not even a signature. Just a held breath that made the skin on his arms tighten.

“Ankhural’s Codex,” Sera said at last. Her voice stayed quiet, formal, the same tone she’d used in the fortress archive. “The section on vergence architecture. What he believed the Jedi Lords had done to the local structure around Veranthos.”

Torin leaned forward. His Force presence tightened, that warm hum narrowing to a point. “You’ve read it.”

“I read the sealed summary in Gavran’s records. This is the primary text.” Sera kept her eyes on the fragment. “He argued that what we feel at vergences isn’t inherent. That it was imposed. Built into the site by the practitioners who first shaped it, until the shaping felt natural because no one alive remembered it.”

Kael gave a quiet sound that wasn’t quite a snort and wasn’t quite a laugh. “That would be heresy in any tradition I trained in.”

“It was heresy in ours,” Sera said.

Ben heard the exchange, but it stayed a layer away. His attention had locked on the fragment. The waiting quality had sharpened, or maybe he had just stopped screening it out. The Force around the stone felt off in a way he couldn’t quite name—not damaged, not dark, just missing in one direction. Like a room with one wall taken away and the space beyond not empty, but unmade.

He reached out.

The motion wasn’t entirely conscious. His hand moved toward the fragment the way it might move toward a door he hadn’t decided to open yet, and he was aware of Desh shifting near the corridor, aware of Torin’s eyes following his fingers, aware of Sera going still. None of it stopped him.

His fingertips touched the stone.

The Force was there.

And then it wasn’t.

No—it was there, but stripped bare. The sensation hit him as a sudden emptying, so abrupt his body reacted before his mind caught up. Weight without direction. Sound without body. The Force around him, through him, was simply present with nothing in it—no texture, no pull, no moral weight to tell him which way to lean. The compass he’d trusted since he was old enough to feel the Force at all had stopped pointing.

His hand jerked back.

The recoil slammed his elbow against the couch arm. Pain flashed up his forearm, sharp and welcome and real. He was breathing hard. His pulse hammered. The fragment sat where it had been, unchanged, unlit, and the Force around it was already easing back into the weave he knew.

The whole thing had lasted three seconds.

“Ben.” Desh’s voice came from the corridor, low and level. “What was that?”

Ben shook his head. He didn’t have words yet. His fingertips still tingled, and the place where he’d touched the stone held a ghost-cold that had nothing to do with temperature. The afterimage sat in his chest like a breath he couldn’t quite let go.

“The imposed structure,” Torin said quietly. “Ankhural was right. You felt it. The absence of the shaping—that’s what the Force is without the architecture we’ve put into it.”

Sera’s jaw tightened. “You don’t know what he felt.”

“I know what I felt when the case opened. We all did.” Torin looked around the table. His warmth was still there, still genuine, but something under it had gone sharp. “The Codex lays out a way to read the local weave. To tell what’s inherent from what’s been imposed. If Ankhural was right about vergences—”

“Then the Jedi Lords built a cage and called it sacred ground,” Kael said. His voice stayed dry, nearly lazy, but his eyes were sharp. “Which is exactly what the Sith would have done if they’d thought of it first.”

“That’s not—” Sera began.

“I’m not taking a side. I’m noting a symmetry.” Kael shifted against the bulkhead, settling his shoulders. “Every tradition I’ve been forced into believed its structure was natural. The Brotherhood. The Jedi Lords. Your modern Order, probably. The only difference is whose name is on the architecture.”

Ben heard them. Sera’s clipped precision, Torin’s gathering certainty, Kael’s flat refusal to commit. Four Force-sensitives holding the same thing and landing on four different readings in real time. He should say something. He should push back, ask the right question, keep the conversation from hardening into positions they couldn’t walk back.

He couldn’t. The afterimage was still too loud. His fingertips were still cold. For a handful of seconds the Force had been there and unshaped—no direction, no texture, no moral register—and he had yanked his hand away before his mind arrived, because his body had known faster than thought that whatever he was touching was wrong in a way he didn’t have a name for.

Not dangerous. Not safe either. Safe was the word that had failed. The Force without that familiar register wasn’t hostile. It was just absent in a direction he’d never had to navigate without.

The ship alarm screamed.

The sound cut through the common area like a blade—three sharp tones, then a sustained pulse that shivered through the deck plates. Desh was moving before the second tone ended, pushing off the corridor frame and heading for the cockpit in one smooth motion.

“Breach alarm,” he called over his shoulder. His voice went flat and professional. “The Brotherhood Outpost. Energy readings are spiking hard. Someone’s there.”

Ben was on his feet. The vertigo still turned in his chest, unresolved, but his body had already snapped into tactical focus. Sera was closing the Codex case. Torin’s warmth had gone tight and ready. Kael had unfolded from the corner couch without a sound.

“How recent?” Ben asked.

Desh’s reply came from the cockpit, clipped and certain. “Recent enough the readings haven’t dropped off. Someone is there, or was there very recently.”

Ben braced one hand on the doorframe. The fragment’s afterimage was still there—a cold spot in his memory, a direction he couldn’t feel. He set it aside. Not answered. Just shelved.

The Brotherhood Outpost had just gotten a lot closer.

## Scene 09.02

Torin lifted the fragment without ceremony.

His hand closed on the dark stone and raised it from the case Sera had opened on the table. Ben’s breath tightened. Everyone else had flinched from the thing’s stripped-bare emptiness, from the way the Force went directionless and cold around it. Torin held it like a tool.

His presence shifted with it. The familiar warm, jagged confidence narrowed and smoothed into something lighter, cleaner, more dangerous. The air around the table seemed to thin.

“The records are intact,” Torin said. “Ankhural was documenting what he found at Veranthos.”

Sera stood opposite him, arms folded, jaw hard. “Read it,” she said. “Don’t improve it.”

Torin glanced at her, then back to the fragment. “The wound at Veranthos isn’t damage that heals. It’s absence. The local structure was taken away and never re-formed.”

The understanding landed in Ben’s body first: a drop in his center, throat tight, stomach hollow. Not a scar. A hole.

Torin went on, quieter now. “Gavran’s answer was containment. Twelve Force-sensitives placed around the perimeter, sustaining the seal. Not as guards. As the seal. Living anchors holding the wound closed.”

Ben stared at the stone. Twelve people. Not an outer field, not some machine left humming in a vault. People, year after year, bled into shape and function until there was nothing left but the work they were forced to do.

The ship’s recycled air tasted metallic. Beneath his boots the Steady Returns’ engines kept their steady hum, absurdly ordinary.

Kael said nothing from the couch, but one look at his face was enough: there it was again, all of them building higher walls inside the same trap.

Torin turned the fragment in his hand. “And Ankhural’s heresy is simple. The void wasn’t created by the technique. It was exposed by it. The weave everyone treated as natural at Veranthos wasn’t natural at all. It had been imposed there, shaped and fixed in place. The procedure stripped that architecture away.”

Sera’s mouth tightened. “That’s the part you like.”

“It’s the part the text supports.” Torin set the fragment down with care. “If he was right, then this wasn’t violation in the way we’ve been taught to think of it. It was correction. What Gavran sealed wasn’t a wound trying to heal. It was a state he couldn’t accept. The anchors weren’t punishment. They were a structural answer to the fact that once the site was opened, no one knew how to let it stand.”

Beautiful. That was the danger of it. The pieces fit too well. Nihilus, Malachor, every warning Ben had ever heard about wounds in the Force—they all sat inside the argument instead of breaking it. If the thing at Veranthos would not heal on its own, if the twelve had truly been the only reason it stayed bounded, then horror and necessity locked together until you could barely tell them apart.

And if Ankhural was right—

Ben felt his head start to dip.

Sera moved first.

She stepped to the table and laid her hand flat between Torin and the fragment. Not hard. Not dramatic. A line drawn in plain sight.

“No,” she said.

Torin looked up. “Sera—”

“No.” Her voice stayed low, but the word had edges. “You don’t get to call a man chained into a wall a solution and make it clean.”

For a beat nobody spoke. Ben caught the motion of his own nod and stopped it cold. The shock of that was worse than the argument. He had almost gone with Torin all the way to the end of it.

Sera’s eyes flicked to him, saw that he knew it, and held there for a fraction of a second.

Then she stepped back from the table. “Ben. Corridor.”

It wasn’t a request.

He followed her out. The door slid shut behind them, muting the common area to a dull mechanical hum. The corridor lights were low; the air was cooler here. Sera stopped with her back to the door and faced him.

For a moment she said nothing. Her jaw worked once.

Then, very quietly, “I’ve heard that argument before.”

Ben didn’t answer.

“Same shape,” she said. “Same calm. Same way it takes something monstrous and turns it one degree until it looks necessary.” Her gaze stayed on his. “I followed a man like that into a war.”

The words hit clean and hard.

“He was wrong,” she said. “I knew it too late.”

She didn’t say the name. She didn’t need to. Ben heard it anyway, in the space she left between one sentence and the next.

## Scene 09.03

The alarm slammed through the common area like a blade.

Ben was on his feet before the klaxon finished its first pulse. His body moved through the door and down the corridor, reaching the cockpit doorway before his mind fully caught up. Proximity alert. Desh had set it to trip on energy signatures matching the Corridor’s profile.

Desh was already in the pilot’s seat. Three seconds. Ben had counted them without meaning to. The man’s hands flew across the controls with the same professional calm he’d brought to every sensor sweep since Terminus Station, pulling up data streams and tactical overlays while the klaxon thinned to a steady warning chime.

“We have a problem,” Desh said.

Ben caught the doorframe and braced. The starfield ahead was only the usual smear of hyperspace, but the scanner to Desh’s left was lit with readings that did not belong there. Energy signatures. Clustered. Pointing straight at the next stop on their route.

“The Brotherhood Outpost,” Desh said without looking up. “Someone beat us there.”

Behind Ben, the corridor filled with bodies. Sera first—she’d been closest to the cockpit, and she moved like someone who’d spent a lifetime answering alarms. Her hand closed on the back of the copilot’s chair and stayed there, knuckles white against the worn fabric. Kael came next, silent as ever, folding himself into the corner where the bulkhead met the corridor wall. Torin’s heavier footsteps followed, and Ben felt him settle in behind his right shoulder.

“How bad?” Ben asked.

Desh’s fingers moved over the sensor controls. “Energy spikes. Concentrated. I’m reading at least four distinct signatures—power signatures, not weapons fire. Someone was running equipment down there.” He paused, eyes narrowing at the data. “Days old. Whatever happened, it’s done.”

The words landed in Ben’s chest without bringing relief. Days old. Not an ambush waiting. Veraine had been here, and she’d moved on.

The alarm’s bite shifted in him, from raw startle to something colder. Tactical assessment. The question wasn’t whether they were flying into a fight. The question was what she’d been doing here for days, and what she wanted them to find.

“Show me,” Ben said.

Desh brought up the signatures on the scanner. Four clusters of energy discharge, spread across the outpost’s layout as if someone had worked methodically through the facility. The timestamps told the story: the earliest spike was seventy-two hours old, the most recent almost a day. Veraine hadn’t just passed through. She’d spent time here. Enough time to do something deliberate.

“She knew we were coming,” Sera said quietly. Her voice was flat and controlled. “She left this for us.”

Ben didn’t answer. He was already reaching for the Force, letting his awareness stretch ahead of the ship toward the outpost below. The thin place was waiting.

It was worse than the last sites. Moderate now, not mild—the way a crack widened across a floor, the way oil seeped through and spread. Dark-side residue bled through the weave’s thinning places, thick and cold, pressing at Ben’s awareness like something trying to force its way through a seam that had already started to give. He felt it in his teeth first, then in the back of his throat.

And under it, something else. A pattern. Deliberate. Someone had shaped what was left here, arranged it so anyone who came looking would find exactly what she wanted them to find.

A message.

“How long until we’re in range?”

Torin’s voice cut in from the corridor. Eager. Ready. Ben heard the edge under it, though—the same edge that had been there when Torin had held the Codex fragment and talked about the void as revelation rather than damage.

Desh didn’t answer right away. His hands kept moving across the controls, running a secondary scan, cross-checking the energy signatures against the outpost’s layout. The hesitation was professional, but it opened a space in the cockpit that hadn’t been there a moment earlier. A gap between Torin’s eagerness and Desh’s caution.

“I’d like to finish the scan before we commit to an approach vector,” Desh said. His voice stayed even. “If she left surprises, I want to know about them before we’re in range of whatever she left behind.”

“We’re already behind her by days,” Torin said. “Every minute we spend scanning is another minute she uses to get farther ahead.”

“She’s not running. She’s staging.” Desh turned in his seat and met Torin’s gaze with the flat calm of someone who’d learned patience in rooms where impatience got people killed. “If she wanted to be gone, she wouldn’t have left signatures this clean. She’s inviting us in. I want to know what we’re walking into before we accept.”

The friction was there. Not a fight—not yet—but the shape of one. Torin wanted speed. Desh wanted certainty. And Ben stood in the cockpit doorway with the Codex argument still unresolved behind him, its weight pressing against his shoulders along with the dark-side residue bleeding through the thin place ahead.

He didn’t reach for the argument. The alarm had burned that thought out. What remained was the tactical question: which instinct did he trust?

Ben felt the rest of the group waiting on his answer. Sera’s stillness. Kael’s silence. Torin’s coiled eagerness. Desh’s steady hands on the controls, still running the scan because no one had told him to stop.

“Finish the scan,” Ben said. “I want to know what we’re flying into.”

Torin said nothing. The silence was answer enough.

Desh gave one sharp nod and turned back to his console. The data kept scrolling—energy signatures, layout overlays, the slow build of information that would tell them what Veraine had done here and what she’d left behind. The cockpit settled into the quiet of professionals working under pressure.

Ben stayed braced against the doorframe, eyes on the starfield ahead, and felt the thin place waiting for them. Oil seeping through a cracked floor. Dark-side residue leaking into a space that should have been clean. And beneath it, that deliberate pattern—the shape of a message Veraine had spent days arranging.

His breathing steadied. Uncertainty gave way to focus. Whatever waited at the Brotherhood Outpost, Veraine had left it for them to find.

The Steady Returns carried them toward it.

# Chapter 10

## Scene 10.01

The outpost door slid open on tracks that hadn’t been serviced in a thousand years, and Ben stepped through into something worse than a temple.

The corridor stretched ahead in hard prefabricated lines—durasteel panels, dead light strips inset overhead, modular construction thrown up in days and meant to outlast the people who built it. Brotherhood of Darkness engineering. No statuary. No altar. No holovid grandeur. Just a hallway wide enough for cargo, with junction boxes at regular intervals and the stale chemical tang of old insulation.

A workplace.

Ben stopped two paces in and let his eyes adjust to the emergency lighting Desh had brought up from the ship. The beam swept across a control panel, a bank of dead monitors, the corner of a workstation chair still tucked under its desk. Someone had left for the day and never come back.

“Sith architecture,” Sera said behind him. Her voice had the flatness she used when something bothered her more than she wanted to show.

“No,” Ben said. “Sith prefab. There’s a difference.”

Desh moved past him, scanner raised, reading the space with the same unhurried professionalism he’d used at every stop along the Corridor. “Power’s dead. Atmospherics are stable, but I wouldn’t trust the scrubbers long-term. Whatever she ran here, she ran off her own supply.”

Veraine.

The energy signatures Desh had picked up from orbit were still bleeding residual heat into the walls. Ben felt it through the Force before his skin caught up. Equipment had been running here. Days ago, not weeks. She’d spent time in this place, and she’d left it arranged.

The corridor opened into a larger room, and the team spread out by instinct. Sera took the left wall, checking power couplings and data ports with the efficiency of someone who’d cleared hostile structures before. Torin moved right, his heavier footsteps the only sound beyond the hum of Desh’s scanner. Kael drifted toward the center, quiet, his attention settling on a workstation that still had a data tablet docked in its cradle.

Ben watched him a beat too long. Kael’s movements had changed since the thin place. Not slower—more deliberate. The way a man moved when he was thinking about something he hadn’t said.

“Anything?” Ben asked.

“Equipment’s been pulled,” Desh said from across the room. “Not scavenged—curated. She took specific components and left the rest. Power regulators, field stabilizers, a whole rack of signal boosters.” He paused, running the scanner over an empty shelf. “Whatever she’s building, she’s building it in pieces. This was a supply stop.”

“A supply stop with a message,” Sera said.

Ben felt it before he understood it. The dark-side residue bleeding through the Weave’s thinning places wasn’t just in the air—it clung to the room in layers. Heat in a chamber where a fight had gone on too long. Anger with nowhere left to live. Cruelty with no living mind behind it anymore, only the stain it left. The Force tasted wrong there, sharp at the back of his throat.

Sera’s jaw tightened. She’d felt it too—Ben saw the strain in her shoulders, the way her hand stayed near her lightsaber without closing on it. Discipline holding against pressure.

“Each room we clear,” she said quietly, “it gets worse.”

Ben didn’t answer. He was tracking the residue through the Force, trying to separate the contamination from his own reactions. The cruelty wasn’t his. The malice wasn’t his. But feeling them meant carrying some fraction of their weight, and he hated that part of the job. His discipline tightened in response—the old Skywalker reflex, control as armor.

He moved closer to Sera without deciding to. Protective positioning. She noticed and said nothing.

Torin had stopped at a bank of dead monitors, his hand resting on the console. “She was systematic. Look at the cable routing—she pulled the data cores but left the housings intact. Someone who knew exactly what she wanted and exactly how long she had to take it.”

“How long did she have?” Ben asked.

“Three days. Maybe four.” Desh straightened from the empty shelf, scanner still running. “Long enough to strip the place of everything she needed. Not long enough to hide what she was doing.”

Kael had reached the workstation.

Ben saw him pick up the data tablet—an ancient unit, Sith-manufactured, its screen still dark. Kael’s thumb found the activation plate, and the display flickered to life in pale amber script. Sith-tradition notation. The characters were sharp and angular, arranged in a format Ben couldn’t read but recognized from the Codex fragments Sera had translated.

Kael read.

His face didn’t change.

Ben had watched Kael through enough tense moments to know the tells that usually gave him away—the slight tension at the corner of his jaw when he was calculating odds, the way his eyes moved when he was reading a room. None of that showed now. His expression was the controlled blankness of someone who had learned to hide reactions in places where showing them got people killed.

But his hands. When Kael set the tablet down, he did it with the careful precision of someone handling ordnance that might still be live. Slow. Exact. Silent. The tablet met the workstation surface without a click.

Then Kael walked out of the room.

He didn’t look at Ben. He didn’t look at Sera or Torin or Desh. He crossed the doorway and vanished into the dark corridor beyond, and his footsteps faded into the outpost’s silence without a word of explanation.

Ben stared at the tablet. The amber script still glowed, still unreadable, still waiting for someone who could understand what Veraine had written.

She’d left it for Kael. Not for the group, not for the Jedi who’d come looking for her—for Kael specifically. She knew who he was. She knew what he represented. And she’d written him a message in a notation only he could read, at a workstation in a Brotherhood outpost she’d spent three days stripping for parts.

The pursuit had just tipped in Ben’s hands. Veraine wasn’t running from them. She was moving toward something, and she wanted them to follow. Wanted Kael to follow, at least, and Kael had just walked out of the room with whatever she’d told him locked behind his eyes.

The dark-side residue pressed against Ben’s awareness. Cruelty without a source. Malice without a target. And now this—a message without a reader, except the one who’d already read it and chosen silence.

Sera was staring at the doorway Kael had disappeared through. Torin’s hand had gone still on the console. Desh had stopped scanning, his attention fixed on the tablet with the flat assessment of someone who’d just watched a variable enter the equation and started recalculating the odds.

Ben didn’t move. The tablet glowed amber in the dark.

Kael knew something. And he wasn’t telling.

## Scene 10.02

Desh had been at it for the better part of an hour. The operations table no longer looked like a table. He’d turned it into a grid, every square holding something pulled from Veraine’s trail.

Equipment manifests lay in neat stacks, cross-referenced against the outpost’s original inventory. Disassembly photos sat beside them, each stamped with a time and tagged with the component’s designation. A tactical map occupied the center, spread flat and marked in Desh’s small, precise handwriting—the script of a man who wrote reports people would read looking for mistakes.

The room smelled faintly of cold dust, old insulation, and the metallic tang that clung to Brotherhood machinery after years of closed-cycle air. Ben stood at the edge of the table, close enough to see the ink strokes without leaning. The dark-side weight in the room sat on him like a pack he’d gotten used to carrying, and pushing through it made his thoughts narrow and sharp. He wanted to move. The evidence was laid out. The pattern was waiting. Standing still felt like wasting time.

Desh didn’t hurry. He set the last photo beside its manifest entry and straightened, scanner still in his off hand.

“Power regulators,” he said. “Field stabilizers. A full rack of signal boosters.” He tapped the manifest. “She left the redundant power couplings. Left the backup atmospheric processors. Took the things that let you build something that talks to something else.”

Sera stood on the far side of the table, both hands flat on the surface. She’d kept them there since Desh started laying out the photos, and Ben had noticed without saying anything. The weight in the outpost was heavier than it had been in the corridor—thicker, closer, enough to make him want a bulkhead or a wall at his back. Sera was bracing against it.

“She’s not scavenging,” she said. “A scavenger takes what’s valuable. She’s selecting.”

“Yeah,” Desh said. “She is.”

He reached across the table and dragged the tactical map toward the center. The Corridor stretched across it in a line of annotated stops—the outpost, the relay station they’d passed two days ago, the survey cache Veraine had hit before they arrived. Each site was marked with what she’d taken and what she’d left.

Desh traced the route with one finger.

“She hit this outpost first. Took the signal boosters and half the field stabilizers. Then she doubled back to the relay station—picked up a set of frequency modulators she’d left behind on the first pass, plus a power converter that wasn’t on the local inventory.” His finger moved again. “The survey cache was last. She took geological sensors and a deep-core mapping unit. Left the biological sampling kit and the atmospheric probes.”

Ben leaned over the map. The pattern was there, just at the edge of clarity, the way a ship’s silhouette came out of hyperspace—first shape, then class, then name.

“She knew what was at each stop before she got there,” he said.

“She had the original deployment manifests,” Sera said. Her voice had tightened. “The Brotherhood built these outposts. She would’ve known the standard loadouts.”

Desh shook his head once. “More than that.”

He slid one of the older sheets free from beneath a photo stack. The paper was a copy, not original—faded columns, old Brotherhood coding, annotations in a hand that wasn’t his.

“Archived transfer records,” he said. “Pulled from the outpost cache. Not complete, but enough. She wasn’t guessing standard loadouts. She was reading the revisions. What got moved, what got swapped, what got stored off-manifest after the Corridor started going bad.”

His finger tapped three separate entries in quick succession.

“She knew this relay station had modulators because they were rerouted here six years ago and never logged into central inventory. She knew the survey cache still had the deep-core unit because the recovery order got drafted and never executed. She knew this outpost kept spare boosters in secondary storage because the Brotherhood flagged the room in an internal maintenance note.”

Sera’s hands pressed harder against the table.

“She had archive access.”

“She had time,” Desh said. “And help.”

He said it without emphasis, the way he’d laid out everything else. Ben looked up.

Desh drew a short line on the map between two marked sites. “We were still pulling survivors out of the wreckage and trying to figure out what the Codex was telling us. During that window, she didn’t need to do all of this herself. She only needed the list.”

He touched the first stop. “A crew here, already in place to start stripping the relay as soon as they got her signal.”

Second stop. “Another team on the cache.”

Then the outpost.

“She comes to ground where she needs to be seen, checks what they missed, takes the pieces that matter, leaves the pieces that make the pattern visible, and moves.”

Ben felt his posture change, shoulders drawing tighter as the route on the table stopped looking like a trail and became timing.

“The Ash-Tithe Compact,” Sera said.

Desh nodded. “Split tasks. Split transport. Split risk. She doesn’t have to be physically present at every site to stay ahead of us. She just has to have curated the work before we even started moving.”

He tapped the archived sheet again. “And the manifests tell her where to send them.”

The pressure in the room seemed to settle lower, not lifting, just changing shape. Ben looked down at the evidence and saw the blank spaces differently. Not delays. Not lucky guesses. Prep.

“She staged it before we knew there was a route,” he said.

“Before we had one,” Desh said. “We were reading archives and triaging survivors. She was reading archive inventories and assigning pickups.”

The words hit cleanly because there was nothing mystical in them. No aura, no feeling in the Force, no hidden meaning waiting to be translated. Freight. Timing. Labor. Ben felt the correction land in the room.

Desh traced the route again, slower this time.

“She’s not running,” he said.

No drama. No pause to soften it. Desh said it the way he’d deliver a threat assessment in a briefing room—the conclusion first, the evidence after, and no time wasted on how anyone felt about it.

“She’s curating. Every stop along this Corridor, she’s taking specific components and leaving specific things behind. The things she leaves aren’t junk—they’re working equipment she could’ve used. She’s leaving them because she wants us to know what she took.”

Sera’s jaw set hard enough to show. Ben saw the tension run up her forearms and stop at her shoulders, held there by discipline.

“She’s leading us,” Ben said.

It wasn’t a question. Desh nodded anyway.

“We’ve been reading this as a pursuit. She got ahead because she escaped first, and we’ve been following her trail.” He gestured at the map. “That isn’t what this is. She’s not ahead because she’s fleeing. She’s ahead because she pre-positioned people and started stripping sites while we were still trying to understand what had happened. Then she left us the route she wanted us to see.”

The room went quiet. The hum of Desh’s scanner was the only sound until he switched it off.

Ben looked at the map again. The stops along the Corridor made a line, but not a straight one. It bent around empty sectors and doubled back at the relay station before continuing outward. A flight path would’ve been direct. A retreat would’ve been fast. This was neither.

This was a route. Prepared. Marked.

Veraine had been in the outpost for three days. She’d had time to strip it clean or wreck what she left behind. She’d done neither. She’d taken exactly what she needed and left exactly what she wanted them to find. Even the order of the stops made sense now: not nearest first, but whichever pickups her crews could complete while she moved them forward.

The question they’d been asking was where she was going. That was the wrong question.

The question was what she was building.

Ben’s hands stayed steady on the table’s edge. His breathing had gone shallow—not from fear, but from the effort of holding the pattern in his head without trying to finish it. He could feel the shape of it, the way the components Desh had listed would fit together if you knew what they were meant to power. He didn’t know. The answer wasn’t there yet.

He pulled back from the table and straightened.

“If she wants us to follow,” he said, “then pursuing her along the Corridor is either an opportunity or a trap.”

“Yeah,” Desh said.

“Either way,” Ben said, looking at the line Veraine had written across the map with freight, crews, and lead time, “we stop treating this like we’re catching up. We’re on the route she made for us. We need to understand what she’s building before the next stop makes that choice for us.”

Sera lifted her hands off the table. She flexed them once, then let them fall to her sides. “We don’t have enough to tell which.”

“No,” Ben said. “We don’t.”

The weight in the room sat there like a third presence. Ben felt it against his skin, at the back of his throat, in the place where the Force should have been clear and wasn’t. Desh had done what the Force-users couldn’t—read Veraine’s intent through freight manifests and disassembly photos and the patient work of cataloguing what she’d left behind. The realization settled into Ben’s chest without fanfare. It was simply true.

He looked at the map one more time. The route curved away from the outpost and kept going, marked in Desh’s small, precise handwriting, each stop annotated with what Veraine had taken and what she’d chosen to leave.

She wasn’t just ahead of them.

She had written the chase.

## Scene 10.03

Kael was outside.

Ben found him on the outpost’s exterior steps, a long shelf of stone cut into the mountainside and dropping into blackness after the first few feet. The cold bit hard. It climbed through the stone, through Ben’s boots, and into his hands before he’d taken three steps from the inner door.

Kael sat near the edge of the platform with his legs drawn up and his arms resting on his knees. His head was tipped back. He was looking at the sky.

Ben paused at the door and let it hiss shut behind him. The sound carried in the thin air, and Kael must have heard it, but he didn’t look over.

The constellations were wrong. Ben had known that since their first night on the surface. These stars were not the ones above Coruscant or Yavin or any Core world he’d ever stood under. Still, he hadn’t really looked at them before. There had been the outpost, the operations table, Desh’s map, the dark side pressing at his temples like a headache he couldn’t shake.

Out here, the weight eased a little. It was still there—the whole mountainside held it—but distance and open air thinned it. Ben crossed toward the steps. His boots scraped stone. Kael still didn’t turn.

He sat beside Kael and left a deliberate arm’s length between them. Not crowding. Not putting on distance, either. Just enough room for Kael to close if he wanted, or leave alone if he didn’t.

The stone was cold enough to soak through Ben’s trousers at once. He ignored it.

Kael’s Force presence had been a knot ever since the stasis chamber. Guarded. Not hostile—Ben knew hostile, and this wasn’t that—but drawn tight, like a man holding himself still for the next blow. Tonight the knot had loosened a fraction. Not open. Just less clenched.

Ben said nothing. He let the silence stretch.

Overhead, the sky spilled unfamiliar stars. No constellations he could name. No landmarks. Kael had slept for a thousand years while the galaxy moved under him, and when he woke, the sky belonged to somebody else.

“It’s strange,” Kael said at last. His voice was low, roughened by the cold. “I knew it would be different. Precession. Stellar drift. I worked out the numbers once, in the chamber, when there wasn’t anything else to do.” He paused. “The numbers were right. It’s still strange.”

Ben glanced up. The stars were sharp in the thin air, bright enough to sting. He tried to find a pattern and failed.

“What were they?” he asked.

Kael lifted one hand without looking. “There. The Hook. It was a marker for the inner Corridor—traders used it to find the relay station. The Hook and the Eye.” His hand fell. “The Eye’s gone. Drifted off. The Hook is still there, but the shape is wrong.”

Ben found the cluster Kael had pointed out. Five stars in a curve that might have been a hook if you were being generous. Or might have been nothing at all.

The cold settled deeper. Ben’s breath clouded and vanished.

He could feel the moment where he could start talking—where the machinery of apology and explanation could begin turning, where he could say something about the Order’s responsibility, about the Reformations, about the thousand years of silence that had buried what Gavran had done. The words were there. They would be true. They would also be a way around the thing he’d come out here to say.

He didn’t take that path.

“What was done to you,” Ben said, “was indefensible.”

No qualification. No context. No lecture about how the Order had changed, or what the Reformations meant, or how the Jedi of today differed from the Jedi Lords who had sealed twelve people into living infrastructure and called it containment.

Just that.

Kael didn’t move. His head stayed tipped toward the sky. His breathing stayed even.

Then his hand went into his pocket.

Ben watched him draw out the data tablet—Veraine’s tablet, the one he’d found in the outpost, the one he hadn’t shown anyone. Kael turned it over in his hands once. Then again. The motion was slow and controlled, the way someone turns a stone over when he isn’t sure he wants to see what’s underneath.

Ben felt the moment sharpen.

He had said the thing that couldn’t be unsaid. Now Kael was deciding what to do with it.

The tablet turned again. Kael’s thumb traced its edge.

Then he slipped it back into his pocket.

“I know,” Kael said.

The silence came back, but it had changed. Less like waiting. More like the pause after a door closes.

Ben didn’t press. He sat with the cold and the strange stars and the loosened knot of Kael’s presence, and he waited.

“She’ll come for me,” Kael said after a while. “At some point. She’ll try to recruit me.”

Ben kept his hands still on his knees. The ground didn’t move, but something underneath it did—a shift he felt before he could name it.

“I’m not refusing what you said,” Kael went on. His voice was still quiet, still rough, but there was something in it now that hadn’t been there before. Not warmth. Not trust. Honesty, stripped bare. “You’re the first Jedi who’s treated me like a person instead of a problem. I’m not refusing that.”

He stopped. The cold filled the space between them.

“She might have a point.”

He didn’t say which point. The moral argument about the seal. The strategic argument for breaking it. The personal one—the parallel between them, the conscript and the alchemist, both used as infrastructure, both sealed away, both waking to a galaxy that had moved on without them.

Ben couldn’t tell. Kael wasn’t going to tell him.

The uncertainty settled in, cold and certain as the stone beneath them. Kael had been treated like a person, and the cost of that recognition sat here now in the dark under stars neither of them could navigate by—Kael caught between gratitude and the pull of someone who shared his wound, and Ben left with a choice he couldn’t control and couldn’t ignore.

The constellations turned overhead, silent and indifferent. The Hook held. The Eye was gone.

# Chapter 11

## Scene 11.01

Desh set his datapad on the galley table with the screen angled outward so everyone could see the tally column. It had grown long enough to need scrolling.

“That’s fourteen,” he said.

Sera broke off mid-sentence. Her hand stayed on the caf dispenser, which had been the center of the argument for the last four minutes. “Fourteen what?”

“Arguments since we broke atmo on WR-7741.” Desh tapped the screen. “I’m counting the one about the nav-computer’s accent as half. That was mostly agreement.”

Torin leaned back against the bulkhead, arms folded, the corner of his mouth lifting. “You’re keeping a ledger of our disagreements.”

“Someone should. For the record.”

“The record of what?” Sera’s voice had the edge of someone who had been right about the galley rotation and knew it. “The record of a ship built for a pilot and a passenger carrying five people and their opinions?”

“Six,” Kael said from the corner.

Everyone looked at him.

He didn’t look up from the ration bar he was breaking into pieces. “Six people. You counted the pilot and the passenger separately. That’s two. Then you said five people and their opinions. That’s seven. Unless the opinions are people.”

Desh snorted. “Fourteen and a half.”

Ben stood near the cockpit door with one shoulder against the frame, a half-finished cup of caf cooling in his hand. The air had gone stale three days ago and wasn’t getting any better. The scrubbers were rated for two occupants. They’d been running hard since Fondor, and the smell sat in the back of his throat every time he breathed in.

It made him restless. He wanted to move, to do something, anything, before the friction caught. He’d spent the morning running diagnostics that didn’t need running, checking the nav-computer’s course projection three times, then standing in the cockpit doorway because there wasn’t room to stand anywhere else.

Sera set the caf dispenser down with a click. “The rotation was established on departure. I took the second watch. It is Torin’s turn.”

“I made caf this morning,” Torin said.

“That is not the rotation.”

“It’s caf, Sera. The water’s recycled. The grounds are the same grounds we’ve been using for four days. The rotation is theoretical at this point.”

“The rotation is the principle.”

Desh made another mark on the datapad.

Ben pushed off the doorframe and crossed to the table. The motion came on instinct, the need to get between them before the argument sharpened. “Torin, take the watch. Sera, the caf’s yours tomorrow. Desh, stop keeping score.”

“Fifteen,” Desh said.

“That wasn’t an argument.”

“You sounded argumentative.”

Kael laughed.

It was short. One rough exhale that caught in his throat and got out before he could stop it, genuine and almost instantly gone. His head stayed down. His hands stayed on the ration bar. The sound had already done its work.

Ben felt the room change before he could name it. The air didn’t get any fresher — the scrubbers still labored, the stale smell still clung to everything — but the mood shifted. It was as if a hatch had cracked and light had slipped through before anyone decided to move toward it.

No one commented. No one turned. But every conversation on the ship had stopped, and none of them started again right away.

Kael finished breaking the ration bar and ate a piece.

Desh set the datapad down facedown. The motion was casual enough to pass, but Ben saw it. The tally was done for now. Kael’s laugh had done what the count couldn’t — reset the room without making anyone lose.

Torin straightened from the bulkhead. “There was a pilot in the NSW,” he said, and the story came on its beat the way his stories always did, warm and easy, filling the silence before it could open into a gap. “Flew a rusted-out Aurek that should have been decommissioned before the war started. She kept a log of every argument her crew had. Called it the Grievance Index.”

“That’s a better name,” Desh said.

“She said it was the only way to settle disputes on a long haul. Whoever had the higher index score at the end of the run bought the first round.”

Sera turned from the dispenser. “Did it work?”

“Until the run where they all had the same score. They spent three hours arguing about who owed who and missed their departure window.” Torin’s smile landed right where it was supposed to. “The squadron commander made her delete the file.”

The galley eased. Kael had finished the ration bar and was wiping his fingers on his trousers, still not looking at anyone, but some of the tightness had gone out of his shoulders. Ben noticed because he was watching for it — not thinking about it, just filing it away with the smell of the ship, the whine of the scrubbers, and the half-beat lag in the Force that never quite went away.

The conversation drifted. The technique came up — Sera said something about the seal’s architecture, and Desh asked a question that was half practical and half needling, and the room opened around the old argument the way it always did. Ben stayed by the table, listening with half his attention while the other half tracked Kael in the corner.

An opening surfaced.

Torin was mid-sentence, talking about the lattice and the way the technique had felt when Gavran first demonstrated it — not the wound, but the correction, the moment before the damage set in. The argument had bent toward the institutional question: whether the technique was wrong in itself or wrong in its use, whether the Jedi Lords’ mistake lay in the tool or in the hand that used it. Kael’s head had come up. Ben could see the shift in him, the slight stillness that said he was listening closely, the way he treated arguments about Jedi authority like a sabacc player watching a dealer’s hands.

Torin could have pulled him in. One question. One invitation to answer. The cost would have been almost nothing — Kael was already half-engaged, already leaning toward the critique, and Torin’s warmth was the right key for that door.

Torin didn’t take it.

He finished his sentence and turned to Sera instead, redirecting the conversation with a question about the seal’s original anchor configuration. The opening shut. The room moved with him, smooth and clean and just a little too perfect, the way a musician lands a note that is correct and somehow late.

Kael’s eyes flicked to Torin. Once. Then back to Desh.

Ben took it in and kept going. The eye-flick and the pivot and the timing of Torin’s warmth landing a touch too neatly all went into the file he was keeping, the one that still didn’t have a name. Torin had passed up a free pitch. Ben didn’t know why. He didn’t try to solve it. The ambiguity stayed where it belonged: noted, not examined, something he’d turn over later when the ship wasn’t pressing in on him and the scrubbers weren’t whining and Kael’s laugh wasn’t still hanging in the back of his mind.

He pushed away from the table and headed for the cockpit. The caf in his hand had gone cold.

The cockpit was quiet. Hyperspace streamed past the viewport in its usual blue-white smear, steady and indifferent. Ben dropped into the pilot’s seat and left the door open behind him. The nav-computer’s course projection glowed on the console. No deviations. Fourteen hours to Maetha.

He heard Desh’s footsteps before the door shut.

Desh dropped into the copilot’s seat with the same easy looseness of a man waiting for the room to clear. He didn’t look at Ben. He looked at the console, then the viewport, then his hands.

The cockpit door closed behind him.

Ben waited. The silence stretched. Desh’s hands stayed on his knees, still in a way that wasn’t like him — Desh usually had to be doing something, checking a setting, tapping a panel, keeping his fingers busy the way operatives did when they were thinking.

“Can we talk about something that isn’t the Force?”

His voice was too casual. The words came light, but the weight was underneath them, and Ben recognized it. Desh had been building up to this. The tally on the datapad, the joke about the Grievance Index, the careful way he’d turned the screen facedown after Kael laughed — all of it had been marking time until the cockpit door closed.

Ben turned in his seat to face him. “Yeah,” he said. “We can do that.”

## Scene 11.02

Desh didn’t start with Jacen.

He started with a raid they both remembered — a day with too much shouting, one arrest gone bad, and a face Ben still saw when sleep slipped sideways. Desh kept both hands on the flight controls while he talked, steady as always, nudging trim he didn’t need to nudge.

“The apartment block on Level 87,” Desh said. “The one with the blue stairwell doors. You remember those?”

Ben did. He remembered the doors because he’d spent forty minutes staring at one while the breach team stacked up, waiting for a signal that never came. The paint had been peeling around the handle. Someone had scratched a name into it — he couldn’t remember the name, only that his eyes had kept snagging on it while his heart hammered too fast and his blaster stayed cold in his hand.

“Yeah,” Ben said. “I remember.”

“The arrest was supposed to be clean. Intel said the target would be alone. Intel was wrong.” Desh’s voice stayed level, not cold — just flat, the way he might have read a mission summary into a recorder. “There was a family in the back room. The target’s sister and her kid. Maybe four years old.”

Ben’s jaw tightened. He knew where this was going. He’d replayed that day often enough to know each beat before Desh named it — the sister screaming, the kid crying, the target reaching for something that turned out not to be a weapon, the shot that went off anyway because the breach team had been nineteen and scared and told the man was dangerous.

“I know what happened,” Ben said. “I was there.”

“I know you were there. That’s why I’m telling you.”

Ben’s first instinct was motion. Stand up. Pace the narrow strip of cockpit floor. Do anything besides sit here and let the words settle. The cockpit was too small for pacing. He stayed put and let the tension drive down into his shoulders instead.

Desh glanced over, then back to the console. “The kid’s face. You still see it.”

It wasn’t a question.

“Sometimes,” Ben said. The word came out flatter than he meant. “Less than I used to.”

“I see it every time someone tells me a story that’s too clean.” Desh’s hands made a small adjustment on the controls, unnecessary but precise. “Every time someone lands a beat exactly where it’s supposed to land, like a good version of a bad thing.”

The cockpit went quiet.

Ben felt the shift before his mind caught up. His stomach dropped — that sick, hollow lurch when the floor gives way under a step that should have been there. Desh hadn’t said the name yet. He didn’t have to. The shape of the sentence was enough.

“Torin,” Ben said.

“Yeah.”

“You’re saying Torin sounds like—”

“The good version of him.” Desh kept his voice even, but careful had crept into it. “The version who tells stories instead of giving orders. The version who makes people want to follow because he’s warm, because he sees them, because he knows exactly what to say and when to say it.”

Ben’s fingers tightened on the armrest. He felt the pressure of his grip and couldn’t make himself ease off. The name was in the cockpit now. Jacen. It had been there since Fondor — since before Fondor, since the first time Desh had answered a comm call he shouldn’t have and agreed to a mission he hadn’t asked enough questions about. They’d carried it in the spaces between words, in the shorthand that let them talk around what they’d both done at sixteen. Now it had a shape.

“You’ve been thinking this for a while,” Ben said.

“Since the galley argument. The story about the Grievance Index.” Desh shook his head once. “It landed too well. Everybody laughed, everybody relaxed, and I thought — I’ve heard this before. Not the story. The timing.”

Ben wanted to push back. The answer rose hot and immediate — that Torin wasn’t Jacen, that the comparison was unfair, that Desh was reaching. But his body had already taken the verdict. His stomach was still down around his boots. His fingers still dug into the armrest. He’d been filing Torin’s warmth-on-a-beat for days without admitting what the file contained.

He didn’t argue.

Desh let the silence sit. He turned his attention to the console, checking the nav-computer’s course projection, the fuel readouts, the comm panel. His hands moved with the same practiced economy he brought to everything, a man who kept his fingers busy while his mind worked through something painful.

The datapad on the console beside him blinked.

Desh looked at it. His hands stopped.

“The reconstruction finished,” he said.

Ben glanced at the datapad. The screen glowed at low brightness, text small and dense, but the shape of the display was plain enough from the copilot’s seat. Desh had been piecing together fragments for days — archive references, Sera’s testimony, the scraps of Brotherhood doctrine Kael had been willing to share. The reconstruction had been running in the background, cross-checking every scrap against the framework they’d built.

It had resolved.

Desh turned the datapad so Ben could see. “The Brotherhood wasn’t trying to destroy the vergence. They were trying to flip its polarity. Corrupt it into a permanent dark-side well — a wound that would feed their adepts and starve everyone else.”
