Not a question. The name landed with the weight of recognition, and not the welcome kind. Ben caught her face in the reflection of the screen: the slight pull at the corners of her mouth, the pause that said she was choosing not to say more.

“You know the name,” Ben said.

“Everyone in Gavran’s command knew the name.” Sera straightened, but she kept looking at the display. “He was a heretic. The Codex was his life’s work. Gavran read it because Gavran read everything, but he never cited Ankhural in his orders. He knew better.”

Ben looked back at the marginalia. The sentence hadn’t changed. Gavran’s handwriting still pressed hard into the flimsi, the ink still dark with the urgency of a man who’d written it knowing he might not get another chance. *We lacked the will or the trust.* The most powerful Jedi Lord of his era, admitting he couldn’t do what the solution demanded.

Ben’s finger hovered over the bookmark function.

The hesitation made no sense, which meant it made perfect sense. The note was exactly the kind of thing he’d been searching for—a theoretical route around the array, buried in the archive of the man who’d built the seal in the first place. It should have been an instant flag, a priority file, something to drag Sera and the others into the room over.

But the requirement was trust. Trust between traditions that had spent a thousand years defining themselves against each other. And Ben’s group had only partly built that. Sera and Kael still circled each other like people who hadn’t agreed on the rules, Torin’s warmth had thinned in ways Ben couldn’t quite name, and Ben himself was still filing reports that left out everything that mattered.

*We lacked the will or the trust.*

Gavran had known what the solution required and admitted he couldn’t make it happen. Ben wasn’t sure he could, either.

He bookmarked the note.

The motion was quick—a tap of his finger, the blink of the confirmation icon. He filed it under *return later*, the same label he’d used on Coruscant for things that had turned out to matter more than he’d expected. Then he moved on, the next file loading into place, and the pressure of the other work shoved him forward.

Sera was still behind him. He could feel her frown without turning.

“You’re not going to read it again?” she asked.

“I bookmarked it.”

“That’s not the same thing.”

Ben kept scrolling. “It’s a theory. A thousand years old. And the man who wrote it down said he couldn’t make it work.” He paused, his hand still on the control. “Right now I need something I can use before we reach Veranthos.”

Sera said nothing. After a moment, her stylus resumed its short, precise strokes behind him—the sound of someone deciding the argument wasn’t worth having yet.

Ben moved on to the next file. The amplification array still glowed amber above the nav station. The intercept vector was still the right call. Veranthos was still four days out.

But the note sat in his datapad under *return later*, and the weight of it stayed with him—the feel of a door left ajar, of a sentence that had landed before he fully understood it.

He would come back to it.

# Chapter 15

## Scene 15.01

The comm station aboard the *Steady Returns* was barely bigger than a storage locker, and Ben had been packed into it for forty minutes.

He cycled frequencies with the easy motion of someone who had spent too much of his adolescence on ships like this one, running survey routes with his father while the galaxy rebuilt itself around them. The encryption protocols were standard Jedi Order: half the channels open on outpost relays, the rest sealed tight for Temple eyes only. Ben had clearance for both. He had been using it.

The first fragment caught him in mid-scroll.

*—anomalous Force disturbance at the Dantooine nexus. Repeat, anomalous disturbance. Requesting guidance from—*

He stopped the feed. The message was stamped six hours ago, routed through the Ord Mantell relay. Half-decrypted, the voice at the other end belonged to a young Knight Ben didn’t recognize. Static gnawed away the rest, but the word *anomalous* landed hard, like a rock dropped into still water.

He kept cycling.

The second report came from Telos. The third from a listening post near the Hapes Cluster, a Jedi observer who had been watching gravimetric shifts in the Transitory Mists and found something else instead. Something that made her voice tighten in a way the encryption couldn’t hide.

Ben’s hands moved on the controls before his mind caught up. The trouble had been a Corridor problem. A Veranthos problem. Something he could fit into the space between his ship and the seal.

It wasn’t anymore.

He pulled the decrypted fragments into one file and sent them to the main display in the common area. Then he stood, the ache across his shoulders only now making itself known. He’d been bent over the console long enough for the muscles to knot, and he hadn’t noticed.

Desh was already at the nav station when Ben came out, dragging a galactic map onto the main holodisplay. Sera stood near the bulkhead, arms crossed, her expression flat in the way that meant she was storing information for later. Torin sat at the table with a cup of something that had gone cold an hour ago.

Ben skipped preamble. “We’ve got reports from three outposts. Dantooine, Telos, and a Hapes listening post. All of them are seeing anomalous Force activity at nexuses.”

Desh glanced up from the display. “The same anomalies we’ve been tracking?”

“Same language. Different coordinates.” Ben brought up the file on his datapad and read the timestamps aloud. “Dantooine six hours ago. Telos four. Hapes is still live—the observer there says the Mists are acting wrong. Not gravimetric. Something else.”

“Something else,” Desh repeated. “Very helpful.”

“She’s a Jedi observer, not a physicist. She’s describing what she feels.”

Desh made a noise that wasn’t agreement and turned back to the holodisplay. His hands moved with the same economy Ben had learned to read years ago—the quick, precise motions of a man used to making sense of information that didn’t want to be understood.

The map shifted. Three points flared to life: Dantooine in the Outer Rim, Telos in the border regions, the Hapes Cluster hanging near the Coreward edge. Desh added the Corridor—the stretch of space they had been following since the first survey world, the bearing that had pulled Ben across half the Outer Rim.

Then he added the Valley of the Jedi.

The shape was immediate. The disturbances weren’t random. They spread outward from the Corridor in a rough spiral—not a straight line, not a clean circle, but a pattern all the same. Like a pressure front moving through the galaxy’s weather, the leading edge of something that had begun at Veranthos and hadn’t stopped.

Sera stepped closer to the display. “The Valley of the Jedi.”

Ben nodded. He’d seen it the same instant she had.

“The Reformations began there,” Sera said. “The first Council met on Ruusan. The decrees that buried Gavran’s work were signed in that valley.”

“And now it’s answering the seal’s failure.” Torin’s voice came from the table, steady and warm in a way Ben had learned to distrust. “The damage is running backward through the history that tried to bury it.”

Ben didn’t answer. The weight in his chest had changed—not alarm anymore, not quite. Something heavier. The trouble had been something he could feel in his bones, a constant pressure like the air before a storm that never broke. Now it had edges. Now it had a map.

Desh added two more points—listening posts farther out, stations that hadn’t reported anything yet but sat right in the pattern’s path. “If this keeps spreading at the current rate, those stations are going to start screaming within twelve hours.”

“Can we warn them?” Sera asked.

“Warn them of what?” Desh turned from the display. “We don’t have a sentence that fits in a standard comm burst. ‘The Force is coming apart at a vergence nobody remembers’ isn’t going to get through relay filters.”

“Then we give them something that will.”

Ben was only half listening. The datapad in his hand had chimed—a soft, brief tone, the one he’d assigned to his personal frequency. A frequency only a handful of people in the galaxy had.

He looked down.

The message was short. Coded, but readable—the sort of encryption Luke used when he wanted privacy without the ceremony of formal security protocols. Three lines, in the voice of a Jedi Master who had felt something wrong in the Force and was asking his nephew for an accounting.

*Sensing disturbances at multiple nexuses. Report status.*

*—Luke*

The cursor blinked in the reply field.

Ben stared at it.

The hum of the ship filled the quiet. Desh was still talking—something about relay protocols and whether the Hapes observer could be reached directly. Sera had a question about the Valley’s current status. Torin said nothing, but Ben could feel his attention, steady and direct.

The cursor blinked.

Ben’s hands rested on the datapad. He didn’t move them.

The reply field stayed empty. Not because he didn’t know what to say—he knew exactly what he should say, and he knew exactly what it would mean. A full report would pull the Order into the Corridor. It would bring Jedi resources, Jedi oversight, Jedi questions he couldn’t answer without explaining why he’d been filing partial reports for weeks. It would hand the mission to someone else, and someone else didn’t know what Ben knew. Someone else hadn’t been inside Gavran’s fortress. Someone else hadn’t felt the seal failing from the inside.

A partial report was worse. A partial report was the thing he’d been doing since the first survey world, and it had already cost him more than he wanted to count.

The truth was impossible. The lie was worse.

The cursor blinked.

Ben closed the message. He didn’t delete it. He didn’t file it away. He let it sit in his inbox, unread and unanswered, the way he’d let too many things sit over the last three weeks.

The pressure in his chest didn’t lift. It sank deeper, like a migraine settling behind the eyes and refusing to break—not pain yet, but the promise of it. The front was still moving through. The storm hadn’t arrived.

But it would.

Desh’s voice cut through the quiet. “Ben. You still with us?”

Ben looked up. The holodisplay still glowed, the disturbance points burning amber against the starfield. Sera was watching him with that sharp, unreadable focus she used when she’d noticed something and had decided not to name it. Torin’s expression gave nothing away.

“Still here,” Ben said.

His voice came out steady. The datapad was dark in his hand.

He didn’t look at it again.

## Scene 15.02

Torin waited until Ben had finished half his plate before he brought it up. He always waited long enough for the food to dull the edge, long enough for the silence to stop feeling like a pause and start feeling like dinner. Then he set his fork down with a small, deliberate click against the ceramic.

“The Valley’s destabilizing.”

Ben kept his eyes on his plate. “I saw the reports.”

“Then you saw the pattern.” Torin’s voice stayed warm and easy, the same tone he’d used three nights ago telling a story about a Coruscanti tapcaf that had made Sera laugh despite herself. “The disturbances aren’t random. They’re spreading outward from the Corridor, and the leading edge hit Ruusan six hours ago. The Valley of the Jedi is answering the seal’s failure.”

Sera sat at the end of the table, her own plate barely touched. She had been quiet since the comm station, and Ben knew that silence. It had weight. It meant she was filing things away for later. This time she was listening.

“The seal was always temporary,” Torin said. “Gavran knew it. The Council knew it. They built a containment system out of living anchors and called it mercy, but they knew it would fail eventually. The only question was when.”

Ben set down his fork. The food had gone lukewarm—some protein-and-grain mix Desh had pulled from the ship’s stores, edible in the way emergency rations were edible. “The seal held for a thousand years. That’s not temporary.”

“It’s temporary if it ends.” Torin pushed his plate aside and drew the tactical display between them. The holomap flared to life: disturbance points Desh had plotted, the Corridor stretching across the Outer Rim, the Valley glowing amber at the edge of the pattern. “Look at the rate of deterioration. The breach at Darrpa was sixty-three days ago. The anchor deaths are accelerating. The cascade is already moving, and nothing we’ve done has slowed it.”

“We’ve been running recovery, not intervention.” Ben leaned forward, the urge to meet him point for point already pulling him in. He wanted to find the flaw, to put pressure on the weak seam in Torin’s logic. “We haven’t tried to reinforce the seal yet.”

“Because reinforcing it won’t work.” Torin’s finger traced the Corridor’s line. “The seal was built to fail. Twelve anchors, one keystone, all of them mortal. Gavran built a dam out of bodies and hoped it would hold forever. It didn’t. It won’t.” He looked up from the display, and his eyes met Ben’s with the same warmth they’d held since the fortress. “The only permanent answer is someone who understands the technique becoming the new seal.”

The words settled between them and stayed there.

Ben heard himself say, “No.”

“You haven’t thought about it.”

“I don’t need to think about it. We’re not trading one person for a stopgap.”

“It’s not a stopgap if it holds.” Torin still didn’t raise his voice. He was explaining, the way he’d explained the tactical situation on the ridge, the way he’d walked Kael through modern comm protocols with patient good humor. “The technique can be applied in reverse. Instead of tearing it apart, you weave yourself into it. You become the structure. No anchors, no chambers, no slow decay. One person, one seal, permanent.”

“One person dead,” Ben said.

“One person choosing.” Torin smiled, and it was real—not the polished warmth Ben had started to watch for, but the thing that made people trust him. “I’m not proposing a sacrifice, Ben. I’m offering to pay a price I’ve already accepted. I used the technique once. I know what it costs. I’m the only person in this room who can make that choice with full knowledge of what it means.”

The display glowed between them, the disturbance points pulsing amber. Ben could feel the shape of Torin’s argument—clean, narrow, impossible to ignore. No more racing Veraine to failing nodes. No more watching the seal sag by inches. One decision, one action, one answer that would not give way tomorrow.

It was the same shape as every choice Ben had made since the first survey world.

“You’re asking me to let you die.”

“I’m asking you to let me finish what I started.” Torin’s hand rested on the edge of the display, steady as durasteel. “The technique damaged the Force. I damaged the Force. A thousand years of stasis didn’t change that—it just postponed the accounting. This is the accounting.”

Sera still hadn’t moved. Her silence had changed, too—not the listening stillness of someone storing information, but the heavy stillness of someone who had heard this argument before. Ben glanced at her, and she gave him nothing back. She was watching him, not Torin.

“You’ve been withholding information,” Torin said.

Ben’s attention snapped back.

“Since the first survey world. You’ve been filing partial reports, controlling what the Order knows, managing the narrative to keep the mission in your hands.” Torin’s tone held no accusation. The warmth remained, and that made it hit harder. “You’re curating reality to manage the outcome you want. You know who else did that?”

The sentence stopped there.

The galley’s recycled air hummed through the vents. Ben’s hands found his knees under the table. He gripped them hard, the pressure a way to stay anchored while he had the strange, sick feeling of being seen—not watched, not measured, but seen clean through. His breath caught. The impulse to answer died in his throat, because answering meant either lying or admitting Torin was right.

Torin didn’t finish the sentence. He didn’t have to.

The display still glowed. The disturbance points still pulsed. The argument still hung there, all clean lines and brutal simplicity, the permanent answer that would cost one life and end the crisis. Ben could feel the pull of it, the temptation to treat Torin’s sacrifice as the solution. It was the same pull he felt every time he closed a report without sending it, every time he let his father’s message sit unanswered.

Sera’s silence settled around them. She wasn’t neutral; her stillness was witness. She knew where this conversation led. She’d known before it started.

Ben kept his hands locked on his knees. The sentence Torin hadn’t finished stayed in the air between them, and Ben did not complete it. He didn’t name the pattern. He didn’t say the parallel out loud. He just sat there while the recognition sank into him, heavy and cold and impossible to shake.

The question was whether recognizing the pattern was enough to break it.

He didn’t know yet.

## Scene 15.03

The companionway was shadowed enough that Ben saw the two figures before he made out their faces. Kael had his back to him, shoulders set in the way that meant he was listening to something he had not committed to yet. Torin faced outward, one hand braced on a coolant conduit at chest height, his head angled slightly toward Kael.

They were standing close. Not the closeness of two people who had paused in the same passage by accident. The closeness of a conversation that had been going on for a while.

Ben’s boot rasped against the deck plate.

Kael turned first. His expression didn’t shift—that same watchful economy he wore everywhere—but he stepped back from Torin in one clean motion and walked away down the companionway without looking at Ben. Not avoidance. Something closer to a door sealing.

Torin watched him go. His gaze tracked Kael’s retreat with an expression Ben couldn’t pin down, a blend of assessment and recognition, and then he turned back and met Ben’s eyes.

Neither of them said anything.

Ben’s first impulse was to do something about it—ask a question, lift an eyebrow, acknowledge that he’d walked in on something and noticed it. He swallowed it down. The restraint felt like a small, deliberate wrong turn, a muscle he was learning to use.

He kept walking.

The sleeping nook was barely larger than the fold-down bunk it held, the same stripped-down slot the *Steady Returns* offered every passenger who wasn’t its pilot. Ben sealed the panel behind him and stood in the quiet for a moment, listening to the ship’s ventilators cycle through the night pattern.

His lightsaber hung at his belt. He unclipped it and sat on the edge of the bunk.

The components came apart in the order they always did. Emitter housing. Focusing lens. Power cell. Cycling field energizer. He laid each piece on the fold-down shelf with the care of a ritual he had done a thousand times, his hands moving through the familiar sequence while his mind went to the harder work.

The report. The one he should have sent three days ago.

He set down the last component—the diatium power cell, still warm from the saber’s standby draw—and pulled his datapad from the locker. The screen came alive with the message queue he had been avoiding. Luke’s coded inquiry sat at the top, timestamped fourteen hours earlier, still unopened.

Ben opened a blank report template.

The cursor blinked.

He typed.

*Survey team located Jedi Lords-era containment facility in the Darrpa Sector. Facility functioned as central node in a network of twelve anchor sites distributed across the Outer Rim. Nine anchors deceased. Three survived in stasis. One escaped approximately sixty-three days before our arrival. Survivors identified as New Sith Wars-era Jedi Knights Sera Varik, Torin Hal, and conscript Kael Drenn. Escapee identified as Darth Veraine, Brotherhood of Darkness alchemist and strategist.*

*The containment network was designed to seal a Force wound caused by a forbidden technique deployed during the New Sith Wars. The technique damaged the local structure of the Force at Veranthos. The seal is failing. Veraine is moving along the Corridor ahead of us, and based on the rate of deterioration, she will reach Veranthos with enough momentum to attempt a full breach.*

*I am pursuing with the survivors. They have operational knowledge of the seal’s architecture that the Order does not possess. I am withholding exact coordinates pending further assessment of the tactical situation.*

He read it through twice.

The paragraphs were honest. They named the fortress, the survivors, the damage, Veraine, the seal’s failure, the stakes. They were also complete enough that sending them would force the Order’s hand. A Jedi strike team would be dispatched within hours. The survivors would be debriefed by analysts who had never felt the strain in the air, who would treat the seal like an engineering problem and the anchors like museum pieces. Ben would be relieved of operational command and assigned a liaison role while someone with more seniority made decisions about people he had woken from a thousand years of stasis.

The part of him that trusted the Order—that believed in hierarchy and protocol and the chain of command his father had spent decades rebuilding—wanted to hit send. That part wasn’t small. It was the part that had filed every report on time as a junior Knight, that had never lied in a mission briefing, that still believed the institution could be trusted to do the right thing if it had all the facts.

The other part had watched the Order move too slowly too many times. Had seen what happened when a crisis hit committees and subcommittees and the careful consensus-building that passed for decisive action. Had learned, at seventeen, that the people who were supposed to protect you could become the people you needed protection from.

Both choices felt like betrayal.

Ben’s thumb hovered over the send command.

He deleted the report with a single keystroke.

The screen went blank. The cursor blinked once and stopped.

He set the datapad aside and reached for his lightsaber components. His hands weren’t quite steady. He fit the focusing lens back into the emitter housing, then the power cell, then the cycling field energizer, each piece sliding home with the same precision he’d used to take it apart. The motions were practiced, mechanical, a performance of steadiness his body could offer while the rest of him caught up to what he’d just done.

The wrongness settled into place. Not the Force wrongness—that was still there, a constant pressure at the edge of everything, like weather moving in from the horizon. This was smaller and more personal. The weight of a choice that could not be unmade.

He finished reassembling the lightsaber and set it on the shelf beside the datapad. The file index was still open. The ghost of the deleted report sat in memory cache, its entry grayed out but intact, recoverable by anyone who knew where to look.

Ben stared at it.

He did not clear the ghost.

The part of him that knew he would need proof later—that he had tried, that he had written the truth before choosing to bury it—wasn’t yet the loudest part. But it was there. It had been there since the first survey world, since the first partial report, since the first moment he had looked at his father’s message and decided not to answer. It was a passenger now. A permanent one.

He closed the file index and lay back on the bunk. The ventilators cycled. The pressure at the edge of his senses held steady, patient and familiar, weather rather than event.

He did not sleep.

# Chapter 16

## Scene 16.01

The comm panel lit with an incoming signal that didn’t match any civilian or Jedi protocol Ben recognized. On Desh’s secondary display, the encryption handshake scrolled by in characters from an older conversation.

Kael straightened in the jump seat. His attention had been on the starfield, the nav overlay, the silence he seemed to carry everywhere, but the protocol’s era signature hit him before it reached anyone else. His jaw tightened once, then eased.

“That’s hers,” he said.

Ben’s hand had already started toward the accept key. He stopped a finger’s breadth short and forced himself to wait. The urge to answer was physical, a lean forward in his chest that wanted to close the distance before distance could be used against him. He knew that feeling. He didn’t give in to it.

“Full encryption,” Desh said. His fingers were already moving on his datapad, breaking the signal into pieces with the same quick, precise motions Ben had seen him use on a dozen threat assessments. “Sith-era protocol. Brotherhood variant, if I’m reading the handshake right. She’s not hiding who she is.”

“She is announcing it,” Sera said. Under pressure, her voice went formal, each word trimmed down to its essentials. “The protocol itself is the signature. She wants us to know the signal is authentic.”

“Or she wants us to think it is.” Desh never looked up. “Could be a lure. Somebody who knows enough about Brotherhood comm protocols to fake a handshake.”

“Who else would know enough?” Kael’s voice stayed quiet, the question flat and exact. He wasn’t arguing. He was stating what he’d already accepted and waiting for the rest of them to catch up.

The comm panel chimed again. Incoming audio.

Ben keyed the channel open.

The voice that filled the cockpit was calm, precise, and complete in a way that made every sentence feel measured twice. “I would prefer to speak rather than be spoken about. I suspect you would as well.”

Silence followed, the sort that happened when five people each had something to say and none of them wanted to start.

Desh broke it first. “She’s good. One sentence and she’s already set the terms.”

“She has not set anything,” Sera said. “She has asked. The difference is the trap.”

“The difference is she used Kael’s name in the handshake.” Desh turned his datapad so Ben could see the decrypted header. “She’s not broadcasting to the Steady Returns. She’s hailing Kael Drenn through a protocol he recognizes and asking to talk. That’s not a trap. That’s an invitation with a very specific guest list.”

Torin shifted against the cockpit’s aft bulkhead. He’d been quiet since the signal came through, watching the exchange with the same patient attention he brought to everything. When he spoke, his voice carried the warmth it always did, the storyteller’s cadence that made people lean in instead of square up.

“We should hear her.”

Sera turned toward him. “You would walk into a meeting with a Sith alchemist who has spent a thousand years planning her next move, on the strength of a polite invitation?”

“I would hear what she has to say before I decided whether to walk anywhere.” Torin’s tone never sharpened. “She reached out. That’s information. Refusing to receive it does not make us safer. It makes us less informed.”

“She reached out because she wants something,” Desh said. “The question is whether what she wants is something we want to give her.”

Ben listened as the argument moved around the cockpit and felt each position settle into place. Desh’s tactical caution was the read he trusted, the one that had kept them alive through operations where trust was the first thing to get someone killed. Sera’s archival memory was the one he listened to; she knew Veraine’s era in ways the rest of them didn’t, and her instinct to treat the invitation as a trap was earned, not reflexive.

Torin’s warmth was something else. Ben registered it without naming it, the way he had in the bunker on the first survey world, the way he had when Torin described the thin place as a relief instead of a wound. It was half of what made Torin effective and half of what made the skin on Ben’s arms go cool. He didn’t know why he was noticing it now. He only knew that he was.

Kael still hadn’t spoken since those first two words. His silence was an answer too.

“She’s going to keep broadcasting until we answer,” Ben said. “That protocol is built for persistence. She’ll cycle the handshake every ninety seconds until she gets a response or the signal degrades beyond recovery.”

Desh looked at him. “You’re considering it.”

“I’m considering the fact that she’s been invisible for a long time and she just stepped into the open. That isn’t an accident. She wants something, and whatever it is, I’d rather learn it in a room where I can see her hands than over a comm channel, where I can’t.”

“The room is the trap,” Sera said. “You cannot see her hands if her hands are the room.”

“Then we don’t meet in her room.” Ben brought up the nav display on the main screen. The Veranthos course overlay was still there beneath the argument, the bearing they’d been following since the first survey world. “Neutral ground. Midpoint between our position and Veranthos. She can’t have had time to prepare it.”

“She has had a thousand years to prepare everything,” Sera said. “You are assuming her preparation began when we arrived. It did not.”

Ben met her eyes. “I’m not assuming anything. I’m choosing to face her now instead of later, when she’s had more time to shape the encounter. That’s not trust. That’s tactics.”

The debate was still open. Desh had another objection forming behind his eyes; Sera’s jaw was working on a sentence that hadn’t escaped yet. Torin was watching Ben with that same patient warmth, and Kael was still silent, and the comm panel was going to chime again in forty seconds with another handshake cycle.

Ben didn’t wait.

His hand moved to the comm controls. He keyed the acceptance sequence—coordinates, timestamp, acknowledgment of the protocol—and transmitted before the argument could reopen. The confirmation packet left the Steady Returns with a soft chime that already sounded like a decision made.

The cockpit went quiet.

“Coordinates sent,” Ben said. “She’ll have them in twelve minutes. We’ll have her response in twenty-four.”

Desh looked at the transmit log, then at Ben. His expression didn’t change; it was the same operational stillness he wore through a hundred decisions he hadn’t agreed with. “You just overrode all of us.”

“I know.”

“Without answering Sera’s objection. Or mine.”

“I know.” Ben met his eyes and held them. “I couldn’t answer them. That’s not the same as not hearing them.”

Sera’s voice came out quieter now, the formality still there but the edge gone. “You have committed us to a meeting with a woman who has killed more Jedi than anyone in this cockpit has ever met, on ground she will have time to assess before we arrive, with no guarantee she will honor the truce.”

“Yes.”

“And you believe this is the correct course.”

Ben let the question sit for a beat. The unease was still there at the edge of his senses, familiar and patient as weather. Veraine was somewhere ahead of them inside it, moving along the Corridor with a plan he couldn’t see and a timeline he couldn’t afford to let her control.

“I believe I can’t fight an abstraction,” he said. “And she’s been an abstraction since the moment we found her empty chamber. I need to see her hands. I need to hear her voice in a room instead of over a speaker. I need to know what she wants badly enough to stop running and start talking.”

He looked at each of them in turn—Desh, Sera, Torin, Kael. “That’s not an answer to your objection. It’s why I made the call anyway.”

Torin nodded once. The warmth was still there, and Ben still didn’t know what to do with it. “Then we prepare.”

Desh was already bringing up the sensor logs, his disagreement filed away in the same place he put everything that wasn’t immediately useful. “She’s going to be early. Somebody with her patience doesn’t arrive on time. She’ll be in position before we get there, and she’ll use the wait to study the ground.”

“Then we don’t give her much ground to study.” Ben pulled the nav display back up. The meeting point was a flat stretch of terrain on a world too insignificant to have a name on their charts, equidistant between the Steady Returns’ current position and the Veranthos bearing. “Minimal approach. We land, we talk, we leave. She doesn’t get a tour.”

“She will not need one,” Sera said. She had turned back to her own console, but her shoulders still held the rigid line that meant she was keeping something in. “She has been studying this Corridor for a thousand years. She knows the ground better than we do.”

“Then we’ll learn it from her.” Ben locked the coordinates into the nav computer. “That’s what this meeting is. Whatever else she wants from it, we’re going to get something too.”

The nav display shifted as the computer accepted the new course. The overlay marked the meeting point, and beyond it, a single contact already in position.

Veraine’s ship.

One vessel. Minimal crew. No support craft, no escort, no visible weapons signature. Either she was supremely confident or supremely prepared.

Probably both.

Ben studied the contact for a long moment. The unease pressed at the edge of his senses, patient and familiar, and somewhere ahead of them in it was a woman who had spent a thousand years planning her next move.

He’d just agreed to walk into a room with her.

“Twelve minutes to confirmation,” Desh said. “Then we’ll know if she’s serious about the truce or just wanted to see if we’d answer.”

“She’s serious,” Kael said.

Everyone turned. Kael hadn’t moved from the jump seat, his silence broken only by those two words. He met Ben’s eyes with the same watchful economy he’d worn since the moment they’d woken him.

“She used my name,” he said. “She does not waste names.”

The comm panel chimed. Incoming confirmation.

Veraine had answered in eight minutes instead of twelve.

## Scene 16.02

The clearing was flat and bare, a stretch of anonymous ground chosen for meetings nobody wanted on their own turf. Veraine’s ship sat at the far edge with its gangway down, one vessel and little else, while the Ash-Tithe Compact held position in ash-gray vacuum coats and mixed armor harnesses—unarmed in posture, not in fact. One of them, a woman with a manifest slate and false transponder tags, tracked the Steady Returns as it approached without even fingering a weapon. Another stood by a maintenance crate that looked too square to be accidental. A third cradled an archive cylinder as if it were a sacred relic. The fourth watched Kael with the focused attention of someone who had taught fear to stand at attention.

Ben took all of it in during the walk from the landing zone to the clearing’s center, his senses open, his hand hovering near his lightsaber. Something sat at the edge of his awareness—patient, familiar, the same off-key weight he had been following since the first survey world. Veraine was inside it somewhere, and in a moment she would be in front of him. His body wanted motion, the clean answer of action, the same pull it always got when the quarry finally stopped running.

Then she stepped out from the shadow of her ship’s gangway.

Calm. Composed. Military bearing beneath academic refinement, like a blade carried by someone who also wrote treatises. Her hands were empty. Her posture was straight without being rigid. She extended her right hand in greeting with the easy formality of a colleague, and the gesture was so ordinary, so stripped of theater, that Ben felt his threat assessment wobble.

He had wanted a monster. Monsters were easier.

“Ben Skywalker,” she said. No contraction. Clean, complete grammar wrapped around the name. “I have wanted to meet the Jedi who could read the seal’s failure before it became visible.”

Ben did not take her hand. He stopped two paces short and let the distance answer for him. “You have been leaving us a trail. That is not the same as wanting to meet.”

“The trail was an invitation. You accepted.” She withdrew her hand without the slightest sign of irritation, which somehow made her harder to read. “I am Darth Veraine. The title is accurate, not a provocation. I trust you will not waste time pretending otherwise.”

Behind Ben, Sera had gone still in the way that meant she was cataloguing threats. Torin had drawn inward, his warmth tightening around him. Desh stood at Ben’s left shoulder with the same operational quiet he carried into every tactical problem, his eyes moving across the Compact in a pattern Ben recognized from a dozen operations. Kael had stopped three steps back and had not moved since.

Veraine’s gaze moved across them and settled on Ben. “You have questions. I have questions. I propose we exchange them rather than fire.”

“You are the one who broke the seal,” Ben said. “That puts the burden of explanation on you.”

“Does it?” The question carried no heat, no challenge—only that slight lift of genuine inquiry. “You have seen the anchor system. You have seen what your predecessors built. Do you believe the seal was a structure that deserved to hold?”

Ben felt the shape of the trap before he could put words to it. She was not asking him to defend the seal. She was asking whether he had already decided it was indefensible, and she knew the answer he had been carrying since the moment he understood what Gavran had done to twelve living beings.

He did not give it to her. “The seal was keeping a wound closed. Whatever else it was, it was doing that.”

“It was keeping a wound closed by turning Force-sensitives into structural rebar,” Veraine said. “Twelve anchors. One keystone. A thousand years of half-aware imprisonment while their life-force was drained to maintain containment. You have met three of the survivors. You have seen what the system cost them. Do you believe the cost was acceptable?”

The question settled into the clearing’s quiet. Ben could feel Sera’s stillness sharpen behind him—she had been one of those anchors, and Veraine knew it, and Veraine had not once looked at her. The omission was deliberate. The question was aimed at Ben because Ben was the one meant to feel the weight of answering.

“I believe the people who built it thought it was,” Ben said.

“Yes.” Veraine gave a single precise nod. “They thought many things. They thought the Weave was natural and the breach was an aberration. They thought the binary was eternal and the war was inevitable. They thought imprisoning their enemies as living infrastructure was mercy because they did not execute them. A thousand years of war, Skywalker, and at the end of it they built a prison and called it peace. Did it work?”

Ben opened his mouth, then shut it again as the ground shifted under the question. She was not asking whether the seal had held. She was asking whether the thing they had built had produced anything worth the cost.

“It held for a thousand years,” he said.

“It held because twelve people were made into a wall. When they died, the wall weakened. When I escaped, the wall began to fail. The seal was not a solution. It was a postponement. You are now living in the moment the postponement ended.” She let that sit for a beat. The silence after her sentences had a pressure of its own—not the silence of someone waiting to be challenged, but the silence of someone who had already run every objection to ground and was leaving him room to arrive there himself. “The breach is real. The seal is a prison built from fear. Release is not destruction. It is the end of an arrangement that should never have been made.”

“Release kills people,” Ben said. “You know that. You have run the numbers.”

“I have. I counted the transition dead before I came here. Did you?”

The question landed like a blade laid flat. Ben felt Sera shift behind him and knew without looking that her jaw had tightened. Torin had gone still. Desh’s attention had stopped roaming the Compact and fixed on Veraine with the flat, measuring stare of someone who had just reclassified a threat.

“You are asking me to accept casualties you do not have the right to decide,” Ben said.

“I am asking you to acknowledge that the current arrangement also produces casualties. The seal is failing regardless of what either of us chooses. The question is not whether people will die. The question is whether they will die preserving a prison or because someone finally had the courage to end one.” She held his gaze. Her Force presence was analytical and precise, not a surge of dark-side malice but something stranger and worse—certainty without heat. “You are a Jedi. You have been taught that the dark side is corruption and the light is purity. Has that teaching prepared you for what you found in Gavran’s fortress? Has it given you a framework for understanding what your predecessors did?”

Ben did not answer. He could not—not without walking deeper into the logic she was building, and every step into it made the next question harder to refuse.

Veraine waited. When he stayed silent, she turned.

The movement was small and deliberate, a shift of attention that reoriented the entire clearing. She turned toward Kael the way a scholar might turn to a text she had been waiting to examine, and her voice changed—still precise, still complete, but with something personal under the academic cadence.

“Kael Drenn,” she said. “You were conscripted by the Brotherhood because your Force-sensitivity made you useful. You were captured by Gavran’s forces and repurposed because your Force-sensitivity made you useful. You have spent a thousand years as infrastructure because someone else’s institutional framework required your compliance. Why would you defend the frameworks that used you as building material?”

Kael stood motionless. His face was a mask, and it cost him; Ben could see the strain in the set of his jaw, the stillness around his eyes, the careful control of his breathing. He did not speak.

The quiet stretched.

Ben felt the question hit his own chest. It was not rhetorical. It contained a real answer Kael might give, and Veraine’s refusal to oversell it—no gesture, no lean, no theatrical pause—made it worse, not better. She had asked because she genuinely wanted to know what Kael would say, and that made the question more dangerous than any speech.

“You and I are the same,” Veraine said, softer now, the precision still there but the volume lowered to something almost intimate. “We were both made into tools by institutions that claimed the right to use us. The difference is not that I am wrong and you are right. The difference is that I have stopped waiting for permission to stop being a tool.”

Kael’s silence held. Ben could feel the effort in it—the way Kael was holding himself still against the question’s weight, the way his answer was assembling somewhere behind the mask and was not ready yet. Ben wanted to speak. He wanted to interrupt, to deflect, to do anything that would break the pressure before it found its target.

He could not. The question was not his to answer.

Veraine studied Kael’s face for a long moment. Then she asked, quieter still, “What did you conclude during the centuries in the wall? When you had nothing to do but conclude.”

She did not wait for an answer. She turned and walked back toward her ship, her people falling into formation around her without a word, and the question remained behind in the clearing like something she had set down rather than spoken.

Kael still had not moved.

Ben stood ready for the handoff, his worldview tested by a coherent argument he could not dismiss, delivered in a register that denied him the target a monologue would have given. The cost sat in his chest like a weight he could not shift. Kael’s silence was not refusal. It was the specific silence of someone whose answer was still assembling, and Veraine had known exactly where to put the question so it would sit where Kael stored answers.

The gangway retracted. The ship’s engines cycled up, a low hum that vibrated through the packed earth. Ben watched it lift and did not reach for his lightsaber.

He had gotten what he came for. He had seen her hands. He had heard her voice in a room instead of over a speaker. He knew what she wanted badly enough to stop running and start talking.

He just wasn’t sure he knew how to fight it.

## Scene 16.03

Sera stepped forward.

It was a small, deliberate movement, one pace that took her past Ben’s shoulder and into the open ground between the group and Veraine. She stopped at arm’s length from the other woman. Close enough for Ben to see the tightness at the corner of her jaw, the way her hands hung at her sides in the stillness of someone who had learned composure as survival and worn it so long it had gone into her bones.

Veraine turned to face her. The movement was unhurried, almost academic—the same measured attention she had given Kael, the same attention she had given Ben. Her hands stayed empty. Her expression did not change.

Sera spoke.

“You have described the seal as a prison.” Her voice was formal, the archaic syntax giving each word the weight of a blade set down on stone. “You have described release as mercy. You have counted the dead from the transition and called the arithmetic acceptable.”

She paused. The tremor was in her voice now, controlled but there.

“I was inside the seal for a thousand years. I know what it cost. I know what Gavran did. I know the weight of every year and every hour and every moment I spent as structural rebar in a machine I did not consent to power.”

Her gaze never left Veraine’s face.

“And I am telling you that when you open the breach, what comes out will not be mercy. It will be nothing. I have felt the nothing. I have lived beside it. I have spent a millennium listening to it press against the seal like a hand against glass, and there is nothing on the other side of that glass that deserves to be let through.”

She stopped.

Ben felt the silence after that as if someone had set a weight across his chest. Sera had not raised her voice. She had not gestured. She had delivered it once, short and lived, and now stood motionless with her hands still at her sides, her breathing controlled, a thousand years hanging in the air between her and the woman who had asked for it.

The Force around them carried no combat texture. No surge of dark-side pressure, no gathering of defensive energy. Only the stillness of a room where something had been said that could not be unsaid.

Veraine’s composure sharpened.

The change was small—a recalibration of attention, a narrowing of the intelligence behind her eyes. Her expression did not warm. Her posture did not shift. But something in her face tightened into focus, and when she spoke, her voice was quieter than before.

“You have described what the breach feels like from inside the seal,” she said. “You have described the nothing. You have described the pressure. You have described a thousand years of proximity to a thing you believe should never be released.”

She held Sera’s gaze.

“You have not described what the place felt like before the breach was made.”

The question landed without flourish—no lean, no gesture, no theatrical pause. Veraine gave it the way she gave everything else: precise, complete, already certain of the answer she was not asking for.

“You were an anchor, Knight Varik. You were placed at the seal’s perimeter. You felt the breach’s pressure for a thousand years. But you also felt the Weave as it existed before the seal was built. You felt the structure Gavran’s technique damaged. You felt what the Jedi Lords chose to preserve.”

She tilted her head a fraction.

“Was it natural? Or was it already a prison you had been taught to call home?”

Sera did not answer.

Ben watched her face and saw the cost of testimony land—not in movement, not in any crack of composure, but in the absolute stillness that followed the question. Sera’s jaw tightened. Her hands did not move. Her breathing did not change. But something behind her eyes went very quiet, and Ben understood with cold clarity that Veraine had not refuted her.

She had asked a question Sera could not answer without unmaking the ground under her feet.

The silence stretched. Veraine held it for three heartbeats, four—long enough for the question to settle into the clearing like something placed rather than spoken—and then she turned.

The movement was unhurried. She walked back toward her ship with measured steps, her boots pressing into the packed earth with the same even rhythm she had brought into the meeting. She did not look back.

The Ash-Tithe Compact fell in around her without being ordered. The woman with the manifest slate—Vess, Ben’s mind supplied, cataloguing without permission—checked the sky before the ramp, a reflex that smelled of combat landings and contested extractions. The one by the maintenance crate touched it once, a quick verification, then followed. The man with the archive cylinder bowed his head to it instead of to Veraine, cradling it against his chest as he climbed the gangway. The fourth—Mira, the one who had watched Kael—moved only when Veraine’s silence allowed it, stepping into the gap behind her with the easy economy of someone who had done this a thousand times and would do it a thousand more.

Ben caught himself cataloguing. The ship’s approach vector—low and fast, a combat descent that had flared at the last moment, not the cautious spiral of a diplomatic arrival. The two tells in the crew’s discipline: Vess’s sky-check and the way the maintenance crate had been placed to give cover if the meeting broke into fire. The three archive fragments Veraine had referenced as if everyone in the clearing had read them—Karness Muur’s taxonomy of Force-wounds, the Veranthos observational corpus, and something Ben did not recognize, a citation delivered in the same level tone as the others but without the anchoring reference that would have placed it in any archive he knew.

He did not comment. He did not stop doing it.

The gangway retracted. The ship’s engines spun up, a low hum that vibrated through the packed earth and rose to a quiet, uncontentious frequency—civilian-grade, or made to sound civilian-grade. The ship lifted, turned, and receded against the cleared sky.

No Force-shift arrived. No easing, no release of the thing sitting at the edge of Ben’s awareness since the first survey world. The meeting was over, and it was not over.

Ben stood in the clearing with the group. His body stayed tense. His mind kept at its catalogue—archive-fragment register, crew tells, approach vector, the specific stillness in Veraine’s face when she had asked Sera the question that could not be answered—but the list did not clarify anything. It only made the load heavier.

Sera had not moved. Kael stood where he had been since Veraine turned away from him, his face unreadable. Torin’s warmth had drawn inward until it was almost gone. Desh was watching the sky where the ship had been, his expression wearing the same operational flatness he used after every tactical problem that did not resolve cleanly.

Ben still had no language for what he was carrying out of the meeting. Only the certainty that Sera’s testimony and Veraine’s counterquestion were both true, and the meeting had not settled them, and something in the air had been more than talk.

## Scene 16.04

The ramp sealed with a hard, final thunk.

Ben climbed to the cockpit deck behind the others, pale clearing dust marking their path across the plating. The Steady Returns smelled of recycled air, old caf, and the faint machine grease that always lived somewhere under Desh’s maintenance work. Familiar enough that the wrongness stood out harder.

Nobody sat where they usually sat.

Sera took the space by the nav station with her back against the bulkhead, arms crossed, posture held so tightly it looked built rather than chosen. Torin had dropped into the jump seat beside the engineering console instead of anywhere that belonged to him, leaning forward with his elbows on his knees, trying to keep warmth in the room by sheer decision. Kael stayed near the galley hatch in the corner, hands visible on his thighs, expression shut down but not absent. Desh was already at the pilot’s station, not settling in, just working through passive departure checks with the kind of silence that meant his thoughts were several moves ahead and none of them pleased him.

Ben stopped at the threshold a moment too long. Some part of him kept waiting for the shipboard rhythm to reassert itself—for straps to click, for Desh to mutter at a balky display, for Torin to ask whether anyone wanted caf as if they had only come back from another hard stop and not from a conversation that had left something open in all of them. The room would not give him that.

“Everyone in one piece?” Desh asked without looking up.

No one answered.

Desh exhaled once through his nose. “I’ll log that as yes.”

Ben crossed to the copilot’s station and sat. The seat still held residual warmth. Desh had been up and moving since before they boarded, then. Ben let that file itself away with everything else.

On the passive display, Veraine’s ship rode the edge of the board as a narrowing blip, vector clean, speed controlled, no flare of panic, no sign of haste. It looked like what it wanted to look like: meeting concluded, parties withdrawing.

Ben’s mind kept refusing the larger shape and catching instead on fragments.

Veraine’s voice, measured enough to make every question sound like record rather than challenge. Sera answering anyway, not because Veraine deserved it but because the dead did. Kael’s silence after Veraine turned to him, no refusal announced, no surrender either. And around all of it, the Ash-Tithe Compact not standing like witnesses or attendants but occupying ground by function.

Vess by the ramp, checking sky and perimeter with reflex too old to be performance. Pira’s crate set down too square, too useful, in a spot that would matter if the truce collapsed—or if the truce was never the only business being done there. Serran with the archive cylinder tucked close and guarded like cargo already assigned. Mira still as a lesson learned under fear, moving only when Veraine’s pause released her. It kept needling at him because it did not fit the shape of a leader bringing people merely to stand behind her while she talked.

“The meeting did not change her,” Sera said.

Her voice came out level, but the control cost her. Ben could hear that much.

Torin looked across at her. “No.”

He let the word sit before adding, “But knowing her shape still matters.”

Sera kept her eyes on the forward viewport. “Her shape was already clear.”

“Clear in principle,” Torin said softly. “Not always in practice.”

That almost got a laugh out of nobody. The room stayed where it was.

“She asked questions she had already answered for herself,” Sera said. “She counted losses as tolerable before I spoke. She heard what happened and used it to refine the next cut.” Her jaw set. “Knowing that does not make her smaller.”

“No,” Torin said again, gentler still. “It makes her more exact.”

Sera did not reply. It was not an argument, not really. They were too tired for one, and too close to the thing itself.

Ben turned his head slightly toward Kael. Since boarding, Kael had said nothing. Ben had seen him carry silence before: as discipline, as retreat, as refusal to feed other people what they wanted from him. This was none of those cleanly. There was strain in it now, not visible until you looked twice.

“Kael,” Ben said.

Kael’s eyes shifted to him. Slow. Deliberate.

“I don’t have a useful report,” he said.

The dryness in his voice would have passed for indifference to anyone who did not know him. Ben knew better.

“She asked you something,” Ben said.

“Yes.”

Ben waited. Kael did not give him more at first, and then, after a breath, “I am still deciding what belongs to me and what belongs to the question.”

Torin glanced toward him, concern plain and carefully unobtrusive. Sera’s posture changed by less than an inch. Ben nodded and left it there. Pressing would only make Kael retreat into the old blankness, and this was not blank. It only looked that way from a distance.

Desh straightened a little over the board. “I’m running back-sweeps across the meeting window.”

Ben looked over. “Back-sweeps?”

“Passive only.” Desh tapped one display, then another, building overlays from approach logs and local traffic drift instead of touching the active systems. “I want our own tracks, their tracks, the clearing perimeter, and every piece of movement we recorded while we were busy having an honest philosophical exchange.”

Torin’s mouth tightened at that, not in disagreement.

Sera pushed off the bulkhead half a step. “You think she brought us there to distract us.”

“I think,” Desh said, “the Compact moved like a crew with split jobs.”

That pulled everyone’s attention to him.

He kept his eyes on the display as he spoke. “Not bodyguard work. Not ceremonial placement. Vess watched the perimeter before and after. Pira didn’t set that crate down at random. Serran had the archive cylinder in hand like he was already on assignment, not like he was carrying a prop for the conversation. Mira’s placement changed with Veraine’s pauses, not with the argument.” His fingers moved, drawing time marks across the meeting window. “And Veraine has had too much in motion for too long to be doing all of it herself in sequence.”

Ben heard the point under the procedure. Arrays. Archive visits. The truce itself. Veranthos ahead of them by weeks if their worst read was right. That only worked if she had been laying rails before they ever saw her, using what the old Brotherhood records gave her and sending different hands to different pieces while everyone else was still recovering survivors and trying to understand what they had already lost.

Desh did not say all of that aloud. He did not need to. The shape of it was there.

“Could be nothing,” he said after a beat. “Could be me disliking how tidy they were.”

“No,” Ben said, before he meant to.

Desh glanced at him once.

Ben looked back to the fading passive track. “Not nothing.”

Because that was the part he could not shake. Veraine had spoken sincerely in the clearing. Ben was sure of it. She had meant every word. That made it worse, not better. People who lied for cover could be read eventually. People who told the truth while something else moved under the truth were harder.

The display washed pale light across Desh’s hands as he pulled old sensor slices and departure vectors into alignment. No alarms. No visible anomaly. Just a pilot refusing to let a bad pattern pass untested.

Sera’s arms loosened, though she did not uncross them. “If there was another layer,” she said, “we should have seen it.”

“Maybe,” Torin said.

Kael spoke without looking up. “Not if the meeting was meant to hold our eyes.”

The quiet after that landed differently.

Ben turned back to the viewport. Veraine’s ship had dwindled to almost nothing, a point easy to lose against the starfield if you looked away even once. He kept watching until the last passive trace vanished.

Desh started the back-sweeps. Ben watched Veraine’s ship vanish from the last passive track and knew the meeting was not finished with them.

# Chapter 17

## Scene 17.01

The wrongness lurched.

Not a jolt. A settling. The floor of the Force seemed to drop another meter, and Ben felt it in his body before his mind caught up: a vertigo twist at the base of his skull, the same stale lurch as a gravity deck stuttering underfoot, except the Steady Returns held steady through hyperspace. The ship’s recycled air carried the usual tang of machine grease and old caf. Everything physical was fine.

Everything else was not.

“Got it,” Desh said from the pilot’s station.

His voice had gone flat, which meant he’d found something he hated and was already three steps into figuring out how much it would cost them. The bulkhead display flickered as he threw the back-sweep data across it—sensor traces from the meeting window, the ones he’d been running since they left the clearing.

Ben straightened in the copilot’s chair. “What am I looking at?”

“One of the Corridor’s intermediate anchor sites took a hit while we were sitting in that clearing listening to Veraine’s philosophy lecture.” Desh highlighted a cluster of signatures on the display. “Pira Kul’s work. Pressure-cut sequence in the node housing—clean, surgical. Somebody who knew exactly how the infrastructure was built and where it could be split.”

He shifted the highlight. “Vess Oran masked the traffic. False beacon hop in the local relay—anyone watching would’ve seen normal throughput. Serran Voth scraped the site archive with an old-language authentication key. And this—”

A faint residue ghosted the calibration lock. Ben didn’t need Desh to name it.

“Mira Dann,” he said.

“Low-level,” Desh agreed. “Not a Sith. Just Force-sensitive enough to finesse a lock calibrated for Jedi hands.” He paused. “She’s not the threat. She’s the tool.”

Ben stared at the display. The signatures were specific. Precise. Work that took planning, coordination, and a window when the people who might stop you were busy elsewhere.

The meeting had been cover.

Not a debate. Not a last attempt at persuasion. Veraine had sat in that clearing and counted the dead in front of Sera and asked questions she already knew the answers to—and while she held their attention, her people had been tearing down the seal’s infrastructure one system at a time.

Sincere and operational. Both true. The sincerity had made the operational part invisible.

“How bad?” Ben asked.

Desh didn’t answer at once. His hands moved over the sensor board, pulling data from the Corridor’s remaining active nodes, running degradation projections against the seal’s failure curve. The math scrolled across the bulkhead in a language Ben could read but didn’t want to.

Desh posted the number.

Hours. Not days.

The countdown numeral sat steady on the cockpit display, cold blue against the starfield. Ben felt his pulse against it—a separate thing, just out of sync with the ship’s hum. The pressure at the back of his head had settled into something constant, like altitude sickness that had decided to move in and stop paying rent.

“Veraine’s already moving,” Ben said. It wasn’t a question.

“She’s been moving since she left that clearing. She’s got a head start and a compromised seal to work with.” Desh turned from the board, his face carrying the same hard, level assessment he’d worn during the worst GAG ops—a man who’d done the arithmetic and wasn’t going to waste time wishing it had come out different. “We’re sprinting now.”

Ben pushed up from the copilot’s chair. His body wanted motion. His mind wanted control. Neither was going to get what it wanted, but standing still felt too much like surrender.

He paced into the common area. Sera stood at the galley counter with her back to the bulkhead, arms crossed, the posture she defaulted to when she was bracing for something she couldn’t fight. Kael sat near the engineering crawlspace hatch, hands open on his thighs, watching the room with the quiet attention of someone cataloging exits. Torin had taken the jump seat by the nav station, leaning forward with his elbows on his knees, warmth still on his face but worn down into something Ben had to look at twice.

“All right,” Ben said. “We’re on the clock. Desh has the countdown posted. Sera—I need you on the seal’s structural specs. Anything in Gavran’s archive that tells us how the anchor sites interact, what happens when one gets compromised mid-cascade.”

Sera nodded once. Short. Controlled. “The archive has maintenance logs. I will find what applies.”

“Kael. You know Veraine’s operational habits better than anyone here. I need—”

“I know what she trained me to see,” Kael said. His voice stayed dry, the monk’s brevity intact. “That is not the same as knowing what she would do now.”

“It’s more than I have. Work with Desh on the approach vectors. If she’s got a preferred tactical pattern, I want it before we drop out of hyperspace.”

Kael held his gaze for a beat—something unreadable moving behind it—then nodded.

Torin hadn’t moved. He was watching Ben with an expression that might have been approval, or something else entirely, something Ben couldn’t name but felt in his chest like a shift in pressure.

“Torin—”

“You’re doing it again,” Torin said. Warm. Almost amused. “Assigning tasks before anyone’s had a chance to breathe.”

“There isn’t time to breathe.”

“There’s always time to breathe. Breathing is what keeps you from making the wrong call because you’re too tired to see the right one.” Torin leaned back in the jump seat, and the warmth on his face was genuine, the same easy paternal affection he’d worn since the first day Ben had met him. “But go on. What do you need from me?”

Ben heard his own voice come out louder than the task warranted. “Approach contingencies. If we’re walking into a compromised site with Veraine already there, I want options that don’t involve walking into a trap.”

The word landed harder than he’d meant it to. He heard the amplification in his own tone—the wound pressing on his patience, narrowing the distance between thought and speech—and caught it. Didn’t explain it. Just let it sit.

Sera’s jaw tightened. “She will expect us to come in force. That is what she would do.”

“Which is why we don’t,” Ben said.

“Which is why we consider what she would not expect,” Sera shot back, and the edge in her voice was sharper than the disagreement needed. She heard it too. Her arms tightened across her chest, and she said nothing else.

The wrongness was in the room with them. Ben could feel it pressing at everyone’s composure—a constant atmospheric weight that made small frictions sound like accusations and silences feel like withdrawals. He couldn’t stop it. He could only watch it work and try not to feed it.

He paced. The common area wasn’t big enough for real pacing, but his body didn’t care. Four steps to the galley counter. Four steps back. The durasteel deck was cold under his boots, the ship’s hum steady beneath the countdown numeral burning on the cockpit display behind him.

Desh’s voice carried from the pilot’s station. “I’m running parallel approach calculations. Kael’s feeding me Veraine’s known tactical preferences. We’ll have options before we drop out.”

“Good.”

“Ben.” Desh’s tone shifted—the flatness he used when he was about to say something Ben didn’t want to hear. “The long-range sensors just picked up another ship on a parallel course toward Veranthos.”

Ben stopped pacing. “Veraine.”

“Has to be. Same vector, same speed, same destination.” A pause. “She’s not hiding anymore.”

The convergence tightened. Two ships running the same line toward the same dying seal, one of them carrying a woman who’d spent a thousand years planning exactly what she’d do when she got there. The countdown numeral on the cockpit display didn’t change. It didn’t need to.

Ben turned back toward the common area—

And Torin was standing in the doorway.

Ben hadn’t heard him move. That wasn’t unusual; Torin moved quietly for a man his size. But something about the way he stood there, a silhouette against the cockpit glow, weight settled on both feet like a man who’d already chosen his ground, made Ben’s stomach drop before Torin opened his mouth.

“We need to talk,” Torin said.

His voice was calm. Warm. The tone of someone who had already decided and was now only negotiating the announcement.

“About what happens when we get there.”

The line itself was quiet. What it announced was not.

Ben had heard that tone before. The cadence lived in a part of his memory he didn’t visit by choice—the warmth of someone who’d made up his mind and was going to tell you about it whether you were ready or not. The certainty under the ease. The finality dressed as conversation.

Jacen had sounded like that. Before the worst conversations.

Ben’s stomach stayed dropped. He couldn’t name what decision Torin had made. He only knew it was made, and that the argument Torin had just announced would happen later, and that when it came it would split something that couldn’t be put back together.

He knew the cadence. He didn’t yet know the shape.

“When we get there,” Ben repeated.

Torin nodded. The warmth on his face was still genuine. That was the worst part. “When we get there.”

Behind him, the countdown numeral held steady. Ahead of him, the passive track showed two ships converging on a point Ben couldn’t see yet but could feel—a pressure at the back of his head, the Force settling into a lower floor, a weight that wasn’t going to lift until something broke.

Torin’s silhouette in the doorway carried the exact weight of an old remembered cadence.

Ben braced himself. The shape of what was coming remained opaque.

## Scene 17.02

The argument started about hyperspace routes.

“The Darrpa approach is compromised,” Sera said, tapping the nav display hard enough to leave a smear. “Veraine’s people hit the intermediate anchor there. We have to assume she’s watching the approach corridor.”

“Which is why we use it,” Torin said. He was still in the doorway, arms crossed, his voice warm enough to sound almost reassuring. “She won’t expect us to walk through a door she thinks she’s locked.”

“She won’t expect it because it is tactically idiotic.”

The word landed wrong. Ben felt it in his chest a heartbeat before his ears caught up—a flash of heat that didn’t fit the comment. Sera had been sharp, yes, but not insulting. The reaction in him was too big for the exchange, a sudden flare of irritation reaching for anger and not caring about the difference.

“It’s not idiotic if she’s already committed her resources elsewhere,” Torin said, still calm, still easy. “She can’t cover every approach.”

“She does not need to cover every approach. She needs to cover the one we are most likely to take.” Sera turned from the nav console, and Ben saw the tight line of her jaw, the way her arms had crossed higher on her chest. “You are proposing we gamble on her incompetence. She has not been incompetent once.”

“I’m proposing we use her expectations against her. That’s not gambling. That’s—”

“That’s what you would do,” Sera said.

The silence after that felt physical. It pressed against Ben’s ears the way cabin pressure shifted before a seal failed. Torin’s expression didn’t change; the warmth stayed in place, but something behind it went still.

“Meaning what?” Torin asked.

“Meaning you think like a Battle Master. You always have. Direct engagement, force concentration, the assumption that your opponent will break before you do.” Sera’s voice climbed, and Ben could hear it climbing. “That worked against the Brotherhood. It will not work against Veraine. She is not a formation you can shatter.”

“I’m aware of what she is.”

“Are you?”

Ben opened his mouth to cut in and heard his own voice come out louder than he’d intended. “Enough.”

Both of them looked at him. Sera’s face had closed down, all brace and edge. Torin’s was unreadable—warmth still there, but thinned, like light through a door pulled almost shut.

“We don’t have time to litigate who thinks like what,” Ben said. “Desh is running approach vectors. We’ll have options before we drop out. Fighting about it now doesn’t help.”

“It helps if the option we choose gets us killed,” Sera said.

“Then we don’t choose that one.”

“That is not—”

Sera’s palm slapped the nav console.

The crack of it against metal silenced the room for a breath. The display flickered once and steadied. Her hand stayed where it had landed, fingers spread, tendons standing out pale against her skin.

Nobody moved.

Ben felt the air tighten around his ribs, the wound’s weight settling over the common area like a front moving in and deciding to stay. His pulse was too fast. His breathing had gone shallow. The anger that had spiked at Sera’s first sharp word was still there, banked but alive, and with a cold lurch he knew the heat was not entirely his.

He caught himself mid-breath.

“Sera,” he started, and stopped. The sentence waiting behind his teeth was the wrong one. Too sharp. Too much. He could feel it crowding up, louder than the argument deserved. He didn’t say it.

Sera lifted her hand from the console. The smear from her finger was still there, a faint print on the display. She didn’t look at it.

“I apologize,” she said. Her voice had gone controlled again, discipline back in place, but Ben could see the effort it took. “That was—”

“Don’t,” Ben said. “It’s not just you.”

He didn’t explain. He didn’t need to. The room had that sour edge to it now, the kind that made small frictions sound like accusations and pauses feel like slamming doors. They all felt it. They’d been feeling it for hours, since the countdown had posted and the second ship had appeared on the passive track and Torin had made his doorway announcement in that calm, decided cadence Ben couldn’t stop hearing.

Torin hadn’t moved from the doorway. His arms were still crossed. The warmth on his face had settled into something Ben couldn’t read—not cold, but distant, the way a fire looked through a viewport.

“The approach vector is a real question,” Torin said. “We should answer it.”

“We will,” Ben said. “When Desh has the data.”

“And if the data doesn’t give us a clear answer?”

“Then we make the best call we can with what we have. That’s what we’ve been doing.”

Torin nodded slowly. The motion was easy, unhurried. It didn’t match the strain in the room. It didn’t match anything.

Kael spoke.

“What’s wrong with him?”

The question came out quiet. Flat. No adornment at all. He was sitting near the engineering crawlspace hatch, hands open on his thighs, watching the room with the same still attention he always wore. He hadn’t moved during the argument. He hadn’t spoken. He’d waited, the way he always waited, until the room forgot he was there.

He wasn’t waiting now.

The question dropped into the charged silence and made the space around it seem to contract. Ben felt it in his chest—simple, devastating because it was simple, aimed at whoever would answer. Kael wasn’t looking at Torin. He wasn’t looking at Ben. His gaze rested on the middle distance, the monk’s register carrying no accusation and no mercy.

Ben knew what Kael was asking. He’d been not asking it himself for days, since the thin place, since Torin’s nostalgic comfort in the destabilized Force, since the easy warmth that had started to read as something else. He’d been avoiding the words because once they were spoken, he’d have to deal with them.

Kael had just spoken them.

Ben looked at Sera.

She was still standing at the nav console. Her hands had gone still on the edge of the display, the fingers that had struck the housing a moment ago now resting motionless against the metal. Ben knew the shape of the sentence she would have said two days ago—the defense, the explanation, the reason Torin was not what Kael was implying. He’d heard her make that defense before, in the thin place, in arguments, in the quiet moments when someone questioned Torin’s judgment and she’d stepped in to close the gap.

The sentence didn’t come.

Sera looked at the table.

Her eyes found the scratched durasteel surface of the galley counter and stayed there. Her hands didn’t move. Her jaw didn’t tighten. She didn’t look at Torin. She didn’t look at Kael. She looked at the table, and the silence where the defense should have been said more than any words she might have spoken.

Ben watched her and felt something shift in his chest that had nothing to do with the wound. It was colder than that. Cleaner.

Torin was still in the doorway. He hadn’t reacted to Kael’s question. He hadn’t reacted to Sera’s silence. His expression held the same distant warmth it had worn since the argument started, and Ben couldn’t tell if he hadn’t heard or had simply chosen not to answer.

The silence stretched.

Kael didn’t repeat the question. He’d asked it once, and the answer had come—not in words, but in the shape of the sentence Sera hadn’t said. He gave a small nod and looked back at his hands.

Ben didn’t know how long they stood there. Long enough for the weight in the room to settle from a moving front into something steadier. Long enough for his pulse to come down from the spike the argument had driven into it. Long enough for the countdown numeral on the cockpit display to tick over again.

Desh came through from the cockpit.

He was carrying four ration packs, balanced against his chest with the practiced ease of someone who’d done this before and would do it again. He set one on the galley counter beside Sera without breaking stride. He handed one to Kael, who took it with a small nod. He crossed to Ben and pressed a pack into his hands.

Ben took it. He didn’t thank him. Desh didn’t expect it.

The last pack stayed in Desh’s hand. He looked at Torin, still in the doorway, still distant. Torin shook his head once. Desh set the pack on the jump seat and sat down.

Nobody spoke.

The silence after the argument told them more than the argument had. Ben sat with his back against the bulkhead, the ration pack unopened in his hands, and felt exhaustion settle in. The pressure behind his ribs was still there, the sour edge still brushing at the corners of his awareness, but it wasn’t climbing anymore. The wound remained. The countdown remained. Veraine’s ship was still on the passive track, converging on the same point they were.

But somewhere in the middle of it all, Kael had asked the question, and Sera had not answered.

The room had changed.

## Scene 17.03

Desh said it without turning from the sensor display.

“Contact. Parallel course, bearing two-seven-one mark four. Same vector we’re riding.”

Ben was halfway across the cockpit before he realized he’d moved. His hand hit the back of the copilot’s chair, and the jolt ran up his arm, a small correction that came a heartbeat too late. He’d been wound tight since the argument in the common area, since the countdown had lit in the corner of the display, since the second ship had flickered onto the passive track and Torin had filled the doorway with that calm, decided cadence Ben still couldn’t shake. The alertness had been sitting in his chest like a coiled spring. Now it had something to bite into.

“Veraine’s?” he asked.

“Signature matches the drive profile from the bunker logs.” Desh kept his voice level, the same operational calm he’d used through every crisis since Terminus Station. “She’s not sprinting. Holding steady parallel about six light-hours to spinward. Same speed, same heading.”

“She knows we’re here.”

“She’s not trying to hide.” Desh tapped the display, and the contact brightened into a single hard point on the long-range plot. “If she wanted to lose us, she’d have jumped by now. She’s keeping station.”

Ben leaned over the nav console and pulled up the trajectory overlay. The Steady Returns’ course drew itself as a blue arc, clean and computer-precise, curving toward the blank space on the charts where Veranthos waited. He overlaid the sensor contact—Veraine’s ship, drive signature matching the bunker readings—and watched the computer think.

Two lines. One meeting point.

The numbers settled in the corner of the display. Arrival windows separated by less than four hours.

Ben’s grip tightened on the chair back. The spring in his chest didn’t loosen. It cinched down harder, the alertness narrowing into something colder. He’d been running scenarios since Gavran’s fortress—intercept courses, blockade positions, the geometry of arriving first and holding the approaches.
