Every one of them had assumed Veraine would try to beat them there, rush the seal, use her head start.

She wasn’t rushing.

She’d matched their speed and settled into formation like a ship joining a convoy. The endgame wasn’t a race. It was a scheduled arrival.

“She timed this,” Ben said.

Desh glanced at him. “Looks that way.”

“She’s been ahead of us since the bunker. She could have pushed harder, gotten there days ago, started work on the seal before we cleared the Corridor.” Ben heard how flat his own voice sounded, the words coming out as fast as he thought them, not shaped for anyone else. “She waited. She wanted us to see her.”

“Or she wanted to arrive at the same time.” Desh’s tone went careful, the way it did when he was handing over something he knew Ben wouldn’t like. “Some people like an audience.”

Ben didn’t answer. He was watching the display, the two trajectories locked now, the convergence point fixed in a system erased from every chart the modern Order possessed. The countdown numeral in the corner ticked over. Hours, not days. The vector had settled. Nothing left to adjust, no approach to revise, no window to exploit. They would arrive when they arrived, and Veraine would arrive when she chose to, and the gap between those two numbers was the only variable left.

The weight of it settled into Ben’s shoulders. Not dread—dread was for things that might not happen. This was the recognition of a door closing, the sound of a lock catching somewhere on the far side of a room you’d just stepped into.

Torin was standing in the cockpit hatch.

Ben hadn’t heard him come up. He was simply there, arms folded, shoulder against the frame, looking at the nav display with an expression Ben couldn’t name at first. It wasn’t the distant warmth he’d worn during the argument, that fire-through-a-viewport look that had made Sera slap the console and Kael ask his quiet question. This was something closer. More immediate.

Torin was watching the convergence point the way a man watched a city he’d left long ago and never expected to see again.

“She’s not running,” Torin said. It wasn’t a question.

“She’s not running,” Ben said.

Torin nodded. The motion was slow, unhurried, the same easy rhythm he’d carried since the fortress. But his eyes stayed on the display, on the two lines sliding together, and Ben saw something move behind them that didn’t match the calm.

Anticipation.

Not dread. Not the grim resignation that had settled into Ben’s own chest. Torin was looking at the endgame and seeing something he wanted to reach. Something he’d been waiting for.

Ben’s attention shifted from the display to Torin’s face and back again. The connection was there, half-formed, visible but not yet clear. Torin’s readiness and Veraine’s timing—those two things rhymed. They shared a quality Ben couldn’t name, a certainty that the convergence was not a crisis to manage but a date to keep.

He didn’t ask. The question crowded behind his teeth anyway, but he’d learned enough about Torin in the past weeks to know that asking straight out got him warmth and a story and not much else. The answers Torin gave were always true. They just weren’t the whole answer.

Ben pulled his datapad from the console slot and stepped back from the nav display. His legs took him to the jump seat near the engineering hatch without any conscious decision, the motion automatic, the way you moved in a cockpit you’d spent weeks living in. The datapad’s glow was low, the screen dimmed for night-cycle ops. He tapped it brighter, and the recovered ledger fragment resolved in pale text against the dark.

He’d read it twice already. Once when Desh had pulled it from the bunker terminal, skimming for anything tactical, and once more during the long quiet after the argument, when the room had changed and Sera had stopped defending and Kael’s question had hung there unanswered. Both times he’d read for information. This time he read because he needed to understand what they were converging toward.

The entry was short. A working note, undated, filed under a citation reference Ben recognized from the bunker archives—Karness Muur, early Sith alchemical taxonomy, the sort of source a scholar would cite without comment because the choice of source said enough. Veraine’s hand was precise. No contractions. Academic syntax that would have sounded formal spoken aloud, but on the page it was simply efficient, the register of a mind that didn’t waste words on ornament.

She had been examining the seal’s pressure dynamics. Not theorizing—measuring. The entry recorded a fluctuation pattern in the containment lattice, correlated it against the failure rate of the outer anchors, and noted that the cascade acceleration matched a model she’d built from first principles. There was no triumph in the note. No hint of victory. Just the work, laid out in clean sentences that assumed the reader would follow the math or wouldn’t, and either outcome was acceptable because the note was not written for a reader.

It was written for her. A mind recording its own motion. The residue of someone who thought at night and wrote down what she found because the writing was part of the thinking.

Ben read the last line twice.

*The interval between anchor failures shortens per the projected curve. The seal’s structural integrity is now a function of time rather than resistance. Arrival at Veranthos should be timed to the final anchor’s collapse, not to the seal’s. The seal is already gone.*

No drama. No declaration. Just arithmetic, rendered in the same measured rhythm the group had heard in the clearing, when Veraine had asked her questions and waited for answers she already knew. The voice in the ledger was the voice in the clearing. Her mind and her ship were the same shape. She thought the way her movement tracked—precise, patient, timed to a curve she’d calculated before anyone else knew the calculation was possible.

Ben set the datapad on his knee.

The cockpit hummed around him, the steady background note of the Steady Returns’ systems running through their night-cycle routines. Desh was still at the sensor display, his posture relaxed but his attention sharp, the way he looked when he was tracking something he didn’t trust. Torin hadn’t moved from the hatch. His reflection lay over the viewport, superimposed on the starfield, his expression still carrying that quiet anticipation Ben couldn’t stop seeing.

Two ships. One convergence point. A world where the Force had been torn a thousand years ago and never healed.

The countdown ticked over.

Ben didn’t reach for the datapad again. He sat with his back against the bulkhead and let the stillness settle in, the cold clarity that came from understanding an enemy’s working mind while watching her ship hold its course in the dark. No dread. No hope. Just the shape of what was coming, rendered in two trajectories and a line of handwriting that didn’t need to explain itself.

The seal was already gone. Veraine had done the math. And somewhere in the quiet of the cockpit, with Torin watching the nav display like a man coming home and Desh tracking the contact that wasn’t trying to hide, Ben understood that the only question left was what happened when they all arrived at the same time.

# Chapter 18

## Scene 18.01

Torin was laughing.

It was a good laugh—warm, easy, landing dead on the beat Desh had set up for it. Desh had said something about the coolant valve in the engineering crawlspace, the one that had seized twice since they’d left the bunker, and Torin had leaned back in his chair and let the sound roll out of him like he’d just heard the funniest thing in the galaxy.

Ben watched and filed it away.

The timing was flawless. So was the modulation. The way Torin’s shoulders loosened and his hand lifted in a loose, *you know how it is* gesture had the practiced ease of someone who’d told stories in common rooms for years and knew exactly when to let the laughter land. It was generous. It made people want to deserve the next one.

Ben’s mouth was dry. He’d been in the corner of the common area for twenty minutes, nursing a cup of caf that had long since gone cold, and somewhere in the last five minutes he’d stopped following the conversation and started reading it. The same way his training had taught him to read a face. The same way Sera read archival text. The same way he’d once watched a different man hold a room and not understood what he was seeing until it was too late.

“When did you start doing that?” Desh asked.

Not a question, really. Desh was leaning against the galley counter, arms folded, his attention on Torin but his voice pitched low enough to stay inside Ben’s corner.

Ben didn’t answer. He didn’t need to. Desh had been watching him watch Torin, and Desh had seen the same thing Ben had. The only difference was that Desh knew the name for it already.

Torin was telling a story now. A battle story—one of the New Sith Wars engagements, the sort of thing he’d told a dozen times since the fortress and never quite the same way twice. His hands moved with the words, sketching formations over the common-area table, and his voice kept the easy rhythm of a man who had been there, survived it, and was generous enough to let the rest of them survive it in the telling.

“I decided,” Torin said, and his hand came down flat on the table. “I acted.” A second gesture, a sweep that took in the whole room. “I bore the cost.”

The cadence hit Ben in the chest like a hand against a locked door.

*I decided. I acted. I bore the cost.*

It was the shape under the story, not the words themselves. Every beat of Torin’s narrative traced the same arc—the choice framed as solitary and necessary, the action framed as clean and complete, the cost framed as something he’d carried alone so no one else had to. It was the pattern of a man who believed that shouldering the burden himself was the same thing as fixing it.

Ben had heard that pattern before. He’d heard it in a voice he hadn’t heard in years, in a room he hadn’t stood in since he was a teenager, from a man who had smiled the same way Torin was smiling now—warm, certain, convinced the hard choice belonged to him alone.

The caf cup was cold in Ben’s hands. He set it down. Careful. Deliberate. The way you set something down when you don’t trust your fingers not to betray you. His breathing stayed even. His posture didn’t change. But something inside him had tightened around a shape with a name, and the name sat behind his teeth like a word he had no right to say.

Torin kept talking. The story had moved into the aftermath now—the wounded he’d carried, the line he’d held, the cost he’d borne without complaint. His voice stayed warm. It always stayed warm. That was what kept making the pattern harder to hold in place even as it became impossible to ignore. Jacen’s warmth had gone thin as the war went on, had become something polished and distant, light seen through duraglass. Torin’s warmth was still full. Still present. Still the thing that made the room feel like a team.

Then, mid-sentence, Torin reached across the table and lifted the caf pot.

He didn’t hurry. He didn’t look at Sera. He didn’t break stride in the story. His arm stretched across the table, found the pot, and poured into her cup before she could reach for it herself. Steam rose in the cool air of the common area. Sera took the cup with a small Dantari inclination of her head—automatic, almost unconscious—and the moment moved on without a word.

Torin kept talking.

Ben couldn’t move.

The gesture was nothing. That was the infuriating part. It was the smallest kindness in the galaxy—a man pouring caf for a woman who had been his comrade a thousand years ago and was his comrade still. It ought to have vanished the second it happened. It ought to have been the sort of detail Ben would note and forget, the way he noted everything else in the room, the way he’d been trained to read a space without pinning meaning to any one object.

But it didn’t vanish. It stood there and refused to be filed away.

Because the pattern Ben had just recognized—the solitary decision, the action taken alone, the cost borne personally—didn’t have room for a man who reached across a table and filled someone else’s cup before she asked. The pattern was Jacen’s pattern. And Jacen had never done that. Not once. Not in all the years Ben had known him, before the fall or during it.

Ben met Desh’s eyes across the common area.

Desh was still leaning against the galley counter. His arms were still folded. His face still held that steady calm he wore through every crisis since Terminus Station. But his eyes stayed on Ben’s, and Ben knew—with the certainty of a bond forged in a place neither of them talked about—that Desh had seen the same thing. That Desh had been tracking the same pattern. That the only question left was which of them would say it first.

One of them thought it. One of them said it.

“Like Jacen.”

Barely audible. Two words dropped into the space between them, too quiet for Torin to catch over his own voice. Ben wasn’t sure whether he’d said it or Desh had. It didn’t matter. The name was in the room now, spoken once, and it would stay there.

Torin laughed at something he’d just said. The laugh was warm. The laugh was perfect. Sera cradled the second cup in both hands, fingers wrapped around the ceramic, and her face held the same disciplined stillness it always did when Torin was talking—listening, measuring, giving nothing away.

Kael sat in the corner, silent, watching the room the way he always watched a room. He hadn’t spoken since the story began. His eyes moved from Torin to Ben to Desh and back again, and his expression stayed as dry and detached as it had since the fortress. Ben couldn’t read him. He never could.

The story went on. Torin’s voice rolled through the common area, warm and easy and perfectly timed, and Ben sat in the corner with a name he now had and a gesture he couldn’t place, carrying both like weight on his ribs.

## Scene 18.02

Desh didn’t frame it as a warning. He framed it as a debrief.

“Let me walk you through the team status.”

He pulled out his datapad and laid it on the console between them, angling the screen so Ben could read the summary columns without leaning in. The cockpit ran in night-cycle dimness, all instrument lights and blue glow: the nav display turning Veranthos’s system in a slow wireframe spin, the countdown timer Desh had stuck to the bulkhead chewing off seconds in pale blue digits. Ben had been staring at that timer for ten minutes before Desh came in. Every time he looked back, it seemed a little smaller.

“Sera’s holding,” Desh said. “Barely. She’s running on discipline and the fact that nobody’s asked her to make a call she can’t square with her oaths. That window closes the second we hit Veranthos and she has to choose between the seal and the man she spent a thousand years fighting beside.”

Ben’s jaw tightened. He kept quiet.

“Kael’s steadier than he was, which isn’t the same as steady. He’s still watching Torin like a man who knows what a bomb looks like and hasn’t decided whether to say so. He’s also the only person on this ship who’s been inside Veraine’s operational headspace, and he hasn’t volunteered a word of it.”

Desh swiped to the next entry. The gesture looked casual. The precision behind it did not.

“Torin’s the problem you already know. He’s not going to break before Veranthos. He’s going to do exactly what he thinks is right, and he’s going to do it alone, and he’s going to call it necessity. You’ve seen the pattern. You named it.”

Ben kept his hands flat on his thighs. He didn’t look at the datapad. He looked at the timer on the bulkhead instead.

“And you,” Desh said.

He set the datapad down and tapped the bulkhead timer without looking at it, a familiar motion so clean it looked like habit.

“You’re carrying this like it’s your weight to hold and nobody else’s. You’re making calls in your head before anyone else gets a say. You’re curating reality to manage the outcome you want.”

His voice stayed steady. Professional. The voice of a man delivering a threat assessment the way he’d delivered a hundred threat assessments in a career Ben had shared only the worst parts of.

Then it changed.

“You know who else did that.”

Not a question. Desh’s eyes held Ben’s, and the space between them filled with everything neither of them had said since Terminus Station.

Ben’s breath caught.

The name wasn’t in the room. It didn’t need to be. It had been there since the common area, since the caf cup and the gesture Ben couldn’t quite file away, and Desh had put a finger on it without speaking a syllable. Jacen. Torin. The pattern that started with a warm voice and a solitary decision and ended with a body count Ben had spent years trying not to count.

And himself. The third term in the equation. The part Desh hadn’t said out loud and didn’t need to.

Ben didn’t answer. There was nothing to say. Desh was right, and Desh knew he was right, and the silence between them was full of everything Ben couldn’t throw back at him.

Something shifted at the edge of Ben’s vision.

Sera stood in the cockpit doorway. Her hand had found the frame, fingers curled around the durasteel edge, knuckles pale in the instrument light. Her weight had tipped forward onto the ball of her front foot, then stopped. Settled back.

She had heard Desh’s last line. Ben knew it the way he knew the timer was still running, the way he knew the problem in the Force was still there, pulling at the edges of his senses. She had heard it, and she had started to speak.

The breath came. A small intake, the start of one of Sera’s precise, principled sentences—a defense of Torin’s character, a reminder of his sacrifice, a line drawn between the man she’d fought beside and the pattern Desh had just named.

The breath went out again.

No words.

Sera’s hand stayed on the frame. Her face was still—Dantari discipline locking every muscle in place—but her eyes were on the space between Ben and Desh, and something behind them had shifted. Not broken. Shifted. The certainty that had held since the fortress was off by a fraction now, and Ben could see the difference.

He watched her. Desh didn’t turn. He didn’t need to. He’d known she was there the moment she arrived—Ben had caught the tiny adjustment in his posture, the old GAG awareness that never quite shut off—and he’d chosen not to acknowledge it.

Sera’s hand lifted from the frame. Her weight shifted back. She turned, and her footsteps moved away down the corridor—measured, not quite steady, the rhythm of someone walking away from a sentence she hadn’t managed to speak.

Neither Ben nor Desh said a word about it.

Desh picked up his datapad and slid it into his jacket. His voice was steady again, but the steadiness had changed—not professional detachment, but the calm of a man who had said what he came to say and wasn’t going to soften it.

“The group needs a reason to stay that isn’t you telling them to. You don’t have that reason yet.”

He paused at the cockpit hatch and didn’t look back.

“Find it.”

The hatch closed behind him.

Ben stayed in the cockpit, alone, the nav display still turning Veranthos’s wireframe in slow silence. The timer kept ticking. The problem in the Force pressed at the cockpit like pressure against a hull seam, patient and off-key.

His hand went to his datapad. The Codex file was still bookmarked—the resonance passage he’d flagged and never revisited, the note Gavran had written in a hand that shook. He’d told himself he’d go back to it when he had time. When the crisis eased. When he understood enough to read it properly.

Now he knew he’d been avoiding it. The same way he’d been avoiding the name Desh hadn’t needed to say. The same way Sera had walked away from a sentence she couldn’t finish.

The bookmark blinked on the screen. Waiting.

Ben didn’t open it. Not yet. But his hand stayed on the datapad, and the weight of the knowledge settled into his shoulders—the answer he needed wasn’t in Desh’s assessment or Sera’s silence. It was in the notes he’d been too afraid to read, and the reason he’d been afraid was the same reason Desh had been right.

The corridor was quiet. Sera’s footsteps had faded. Ben sat with the ship’s recycled-air tang in his nose and the timer still counting on the far bulkhead.

## Scene 18.03

Ben slid down the corridor bulkhead until he was sitting on the deck plates, legs stretched across the narrow passage, his back to cold durasteel. He hadn’t planned it. His knees had folded, the wall had caught him, and the deck was cold through his trousers. Fine. For once, that was fine.

The ship sat in night-cycle quiet around him. Its hum lay just under hearing, the *Steady Returns’* small-ship heartbeat, steady enough to vanish until something broke it. Nothing had broken it. The nav display in the cockpit still turned Veranthos in its slow wireframe loop. Desh’s timer kept eating seconds off the bulkhead. Torin was three compartments away, asleep or pretending. And Sera’s unfinished sentence still hung in Ben’s head.

Ben let his head settle against the bulkhead. The vibration moved through his skull, a low, constant thrum from the engines doing their work without caring about cockpit arguments or unfinished talks. His shoulders ached. The back of his neck had been tight for days, and he’d stopped noticing until the deck’s cold gave him something else to feel. His body had been waiting for permission to stop. He hadn’t given it. It had taken the permission anyway.

The datapad was in his hand before he decided to pick it up. Muscle memory. The Codex file was still bookmarked—the resonance passage he’d flagged weeks ago and not come back to, the note Gavran had written in the margin in a hand that wobbled. He’d told himself he’d return to it when he had time. When the crisis let up. When he knew enough to read it right.

He’d been avoiding it. The same way he’d been avoiding the name Desh hadn’t needed to say. The same way Sera had walked away from a sentence she couldn’t finish.

The screen glowed dimly in the corridor. Ben scrolled to the bookmark. The words were familiar—he’d read them too many times for them to be anything else—but familiarity wasn’t understanding. He’d been reading the note the way he’d been reading everything else on this trip: as proof of a pattern he already believed in.

*We lacked the will or the trust to attempt it.*

Gavran’s hand had shaken when he wrote it. Ben had always read that as grief. Regret. A dying man admitting failure. *We weren’t brave enough.* That was how it had landed every time. The Jedi Lords had the technique, the knowledge, the vergence mapped, the seal designed, the anchors in place—and at the last moment, they hadn’t had the nerve to do what needed doing. So Gavran had done it alone. Carried the burden alone. Died alone.

That was the pattern Ben had been pulling out of the note. The pattern he’d been living inside without naming.

He looked at the sentence again.

*We lacked the will or the trust.*

Or.

Not *and*. Not one failure of courage. Two separate problems, set side by side like entries on a manifest. *Will* was one thing—the willingness to act, to pay the price, to do what needed doing. Gavran had had will. He’d died at his post. He’d held the seal alone for years after the others were gone.

*Trust* was different.

*We couldn’t trust each other enough.*

The emphasis shifted under his eyes. Not a confession of cowardice. A diagnosis. Gavran hadn’t been saying *we weren’t brave enough*. He’d been saying *we couldn’t work together*. The Jedi Lords had the knowledge and the technique and the vergence mapped, and when they’d needed to act as one, they hadn’t been able to. So one man had done it alone. The seal had held—for a thousand years, it had held—but it had held as a solitary thing, a weight carried by one person and then by twelve prisoners who’d never agreed to carry it. Now it was failing because one pair of hands had never really been enough.

Ben’s hand stilled on the datapad.

The trust problem was the one he’d been avoiding. Not *am I worthy enough*. Not *can I carry this alone*. Those were the questions he knew how to ask because they were the questions he knew how to answer. The real one was harder. *Can I build trust with the people I’m working with?* Can Sera trust him enough to finish the sentence she’d walked away from? Can Kael trust him enough to say what he knew about Veraine? Can Torin—

Torin was a different problem. Torin was the problem Desh had named without naming, the pattern Ben had recognized and couldn’t unrecognize. But Torin was also someone Ben had been treating as a threat to manage instead of an ally to reach. And that was the trap.

His mother’s voice came back to him. Not a Force-ghost. Not a visitation. Just the remembered shape of the way she would have said it, practical and stripped down to what mattered: *Stop wondering if you’re worthy and figure out what works.*

One short sentence. Move.

Ben had been a teenager when she’d said it—something about a training exercise he’d overthought, a problem he’d circled instead of solving. She’d had no patience for self-reproach. Self-reproach was only another way of making the problem about himself instead of about the work.

The memory tightened something in his chest. Not pain. Not the old grief that sat in the background of everything. Something closer to focus. His mother had been a pragmatist. She solved problems by looking at what was actually in front of her and doing the thing that worked, not the thing that proved she deserved her name.

Ben had been trying to prove something since Terminus Station. Maybe longer. Maybe since Jacen.

The lightsaber on his hip pressed against his thigh—a familiar weight, solid and unremarkable. He didn’t reach for it. He just let it be there, something real to brace against while the question changed shape in his head.

*We lacked the will or the trust.*

The will he had. He’d had it since the fortress, since the moment he’d decided not to call Luke. The trust was the piece he hadn’t figured out. The one he’d been reading as a failure of courage when it was really a failure of something else.

Maybe a different solution.

He didn’t know what that solution looked like. He didn’t have a plan. The exhaustion was still there, settled into his shoulders and the back of his neck, and the timer in the cockpit was still counting down, and Veraine was still somewhere ahead of them, and Torin was still three compartments away, being a problem Ben didn’t know how to solve. None of that had changed.

But he’d moved one file from the archive folder to the open one. The resonance note wasn’t a confession of failure anymore. It was a diagnosis. And a diagnosis was something he could work with.

Ben closed the datapad. The corridor stayed quiet. The ship’s hum stayed steady. The cold of the deck plates still pressed through his trousers, and his legs were still stretched across the narrow passage, and he was still tired in a way that had stopped being about sleep days ago.

He didn’t get up. Not yet. But his hand stayed on the datapad, and the weight of the lightsaber stayed against his thigh, and the question stayed open.

# Chapter 19

## Scene 19.01

The outpost looked normal on approach. A standard Outer Rim installation: landing pad, low permacrete buildings, the soft blue wash of the perimeter field catching the last light of the system’s day. Desh brought the *Steady Returns* down with the ease of a man who’d landed on a hundred identical pads, and the struts met the deck with barely a tremor.

Ben was already at the ramp before the all-clear chimed.

The Knight waiting on the landing pad was young—maybe a year or two older than Ben, maybe not. His eyes were too wide. His hands hung at his sides with the careful stillness of someone trying not to shake apart.

“Knight Skywalker,” the man said. His voice held steady. That was the first thing that felt off. “We received your approach vector. The outpost commander is in the meditation hall.”

“Is there a problem?” Ben asked.

The Knight didn’t move his hands. “Yes.”

No explanation followed. He turned and headed toward the main building, and Ben fell in behind him. The ramp sealed with a hydraulic sigh that sounded far too loud in the quiet.

Inside, the outpost smelled of recycled air and overworked filtration, with a faint trace of ozone from aging equipment. Beneath that sat something else. The same sour tension he’d felt before, only heavier here than anywhere except Kolven. It pressed against his chest like a palm he couldn’t shrug off.

Desh came up beside him. Sera and Torin and Kael were somewhere behind; he could hear their footsteps, Sera’s crisp pace, Torin’s heavier tread. He didn’t look back. The corridor opened into the training area, and Ben stopped dead.

Meditation cushions were scattered around the room, not stacked away but left where they’d been abandoned. Some still held the impressions of bodies that had stood up mid-breath and never settled back down. A lightsaber kata had frozen halfway through: a younger Knight, a Twi’lek woman, standing with her blade still lit, feet locked in the opening stance of a Soresu drill she’d never finished. The breathing exercises had died on the third exhale; Ben could see the markers on the floor where a group had been moving through the sequence. Around the room, young faces wore expressions far too old for them.

Ben’s body tightened. Not recognition yet. The shape of it. A pressure gathering behind his ribs, a pull toward the abandoned kata as if he could restart the whole room by stepping into the right place.

“Three days,” the Knight said behind him. “The drills started failing three days ago. We thought it was fatigue. A virus. Something in the water.”

“It’s not in the water,” Ben said.

“No.”

Sera moved past him into the room and stopped beside the Twi’lek woman, studying the frozen stance in silence. Kael hovered near the door, unreadable. Torin walked to the center of the training floor and stood among the abandoned cushions, looking at them the way a veteran looked at a battlefield he’d seen before.

The outpost commander was a Human woman in her forties, hair pulled back in a tight braid, eyes carrying the exhaustion of someone who’d been holding a problem together for days without understanding it. She met them in the meditation hall—a smaller room off the training area, its lighting dim, its silence somehow as wrong as everything else here.

“Thank you for coming,” she said. “I don’t—we don’t know how to describe what’s happening.”

“Try,” Ben said.

She did. The words came in fragments: meditation failing, the Force arriving in pieces, a feeling like reaching for a handhold and finding only empty air. Ben listened without interrupting. The description hit with a familiar chill.

A young Knight about Ben’s age stepped forward. Human, dark hair, the kind of face that would have been open in better circumstances. Now it was drawn tight.

“I can’t find the shape of it anymore,” she said. “The Force is there. I can feel it. But I can’t—” She stopped, hands at her sides, too still in the same careful way as the Knight at the landing pad. “I can’t find the shape of it. It’s like reaching for something in the dark and closing on nothing.”

Ben’s breath caught.

The Battlefield Node. The permanent distortion. The thing that had felt distant and wrong and impossible to name—it was here now, bleeding into occupied Jedi space and doing to these Knights what it had done to him at the wound itself. Not a passing disruption. A change that had settled in and stayed.

The young Knight was still looking at him, waiting. Ben’s hands wanted to reach out and couldn’t. He knew what this was. He couldn’t explain it without trying to rewrite the Jedi Order’s understanding of the seal in the same breath, without telling these Knights that the ground beneath their practice had been built over a system of penance older than any of them.

“Your name,” he said.

“Rina.”

“Rina.” He held her gaze. “You’re not doing anything wrong. The problem isn’t your technique.”

She didn’t look relieved. She looked like someone who had been hoping the problem was her technique, because that at least would have meant she could fix it.

Torin moved.

He crossed the room in four strides and knelt beside Rina—not in front of her, beside her, his shoulder almost brushing hers. He set a hand on her shoulder. The gesture was gentle. The weight of it, the way his palm settled against her tunic, the thumb resting without pressing—it was the softest thing Ben had seen him do.

“Listen to me,” Torin said. His voice was low, warm, the voice of a man who’d calmed frightened soldiers before. “What you’re describing—that missing shape—that’s not you. It’s something outside you. Something that’s been building a long time, and you’re feeling it because you’re close to the source. You’re not broken. Do you understand me?”

Rina’s eyes shone wet. She nodded.

Torin held her gaze a moment longer. His face stayed open, present, there with her. Then he looked up.

Over her head, past her shoulder, his eyes met Ben’s.

And the expression changed.

Not a mask slipping. Not a performance collapsing. The tenderness was still there—Ben could see it in the set of Torin’s jaw, in the way his hand stayed on Rina’s shoulder. But his eyes were measuring now. Cold and calculating, like a commander sizing up a position he already knew he’d have to take. He was looking at Ben, and at Rina, and at something else behind them, and adding it up in silence.

Ben felt the ground shift.

Sera was watching Torin too. Her face gave nothing away, but her weight had shifted onto her back foot, a fighter’s adjustment, small and automatic. Kael hadn’t moved from the doorway. Desh was somewhere in the corridor behind them, and Ben had a sudden, fierce wish he could trade places with him—stand outside this room and not feel what he was feeling, not see what he was seeing.

Torin’s hand stayed on Rina’s shoulder. The tenderness stayed in his grip. The calculation stayed in his eyes.

Both were true. Both were there at once.

Ben couldn’t fix this. He couldn’t explain it. And Torin was already weighing the cost of doing nothing against the cost of whatever solution he was considering, and Ben didn’t know what that solution was, only that he was running out of time to find a better one.

## Scene 19.02

Torin knelt beside Rina.

Ben watched his hand settle on her shoulder—steady, warm, the same hand that had anchored a thousand soldiers on a dozen battlefields before Ben’s Order existed. The tenderness wasn’t an act. It showed in the light pressure of Torin’s thumb, in the careful tilt of his head as he held Rina’s gaze.

“What you’re describing,” Torin said, low and even, “that missing shape—that isn’t you. It’s something outside you. Something that’s been building for a long time.”

Rina’s breathing had eased. Her hands, clenched at her sides when Ben came in, had loosened. She was listening the way a drowning person listened to someone who sounded certain he knew where the shore lay.

“You’re feeling it because you’re close to the source,” Torin went on. “Not because you failed. Not because your technique was wrong. Because the seal is failing, and you’re close enough to feel it tear.”

The word tore through the quiet of the meditation hall and hung there.

Ben felt it in his chest. Not the Force—the disturbance was a constant drag now, something he’d learned to breathe around—but the shape of Torin’s argument. Rina’s suffering was real. The Knights in the training room, frozen mid-kata, their drills abandoned, their faces too old for their bodies—their suffering was real too. Torin named it, and the naming was an act of compassion. That compassion was also carrying the room toward a conclusion Ben could already see.

“The seal can’t be maintained bit by bit,” Torin said. His voice hadn’t changed. Still warm. Still the voice of a man who’d calmed frightened soldiers before. “You’re not the first to feel this. You won’t be the last. The structure is failing piece by piece, and every piece that goes means more Knights like you, standing in rooms like this, wondering what they did wrong.”

He looked up.

Over Rina’s head, past her shoulder, his eyes met Ben’s. The tenderness remained—Ben could see it in the set of Torin’s jaw, in the way his hand stayed on Rina’s shoulder without tightening—but his eyes had gone measuring. Cold with decision. A commander sizing up ground he already knew he’d have to take.

“The seal was never meant to last forever,” Torin said. He was still speaking to Rina, still holding her gaze, but the words were meant for the whole room. “It was a stopgap. A thousand years of stopgap, and now the gap is widening, and the people paying the cost are standing in front of me wondering why they can’t meditate.”

Rina’s eyes were wet. She nodded.

Torin held her gaze a moment longer, then lifted his hand from her shoulder and rose. The movement was smooth, unhurried. He turned to face the room—to face Ben, and Sera, and Kael in the doorway, and the outpost commander standing stiff beside the wall.

“The Knights here aren’t broken,” he said. “They’re the first casualties of a decision we’re still pretending we don’t have to make.”

Ben’s hands wanted to move. To do something. The urge was physical—a tightening across his shoulders, a pressure behind his ribs that demanded action. But there was nothing to do. He couldn’t fix Rina’s connection to the Force. He couldn’t reverse the seal’s failure. He couldn’t offer some clean counterargument that would make Torin’s logic less sharp or his compassion less real.

The helplessness sat in him like a stone.

Sera’s jaw was working.

Ben caught it from the corner of his eye—the small flex along her jawline, the controlled rise and fall of her breathing. Her posture had gone rigid in a way that had nothing to do with discipline. She was holding still the way a fighter held still before the blow landed.

She’d heard this argument before. Ben knew it without being told. In different forms, across a thousand years, from commanders who spoke with the same warmth and the same certainty and the same willingness to name a cost and pay it with someone else’s pain. Gavran had spoken that way. Maybe not in these exact words, but in this shape. This logic. The logic that said the seal couldn’t be maintained and that someone had to decide when to let it go, and that someone was always the one doing the talking.

Sera’s jaw tightened.

Not agreement. Ben could see that much. Her eyes were on Torin, and they weren’t the eyes of someone being persuaded. They were the eyes of someone watching a door close.

Torin was still talking. Something about the containment network’s stability, about the cascade pattern they’d seen at Kolven and the battlefield node. Ben heard the words without taking them in. His attention stayed on Sera—on the way her weight had shifted onto her back foot, the fighter’s adjustment, small and automatic. On the way her breathing had settled into a rhythm too even, too controlled, like someone counting to keep from speaking.

She was watching Torin turn Rina’s suffering into evidence for mercy-through-release. Watching compassion arrive with a conclusion already in its pocket. And something in her face was changing.

Ben saw the instant it happened. Not a crack—Sera Varik didn’t crack. A shift, then. A recognition that moved through her body before it reached her expression: her spine straightening, her shoulders settling, her hands uncurling at her sides.

She turned away from Torin.

The movement was deliberate. Not a flinch, not a recoil, not the jerk of someone who couldn’t bear to look. She turned the way a soldier turned from a commander whose orders she had already decided not to follow.

And faced Ben.

Her posture changed with it—from bystander to questioner, from the woman who had been following Torin’s framework in her bones for a thousand years to the woman who was asking for a different one. Her eyes met his, clear and tired, carrying a question she had never asked him before.

“What is your plan, Skywalker?”

The formal register landed exactly where it always had. Skywalker. Not Ben. The same word she’d used since the stasis chamber, the same distance, the same discipline. But the question wasn’t conditional. It wasn’t a test. It wasn’t the careful, provisional trust she had offered him at Kolven and on the *Steady Returns* and in every moment since she woke.

It was a real request. A soldier who had just chosen her commander asking him what they were going to do.

Ben’s spine straightened. His breath steadied. The weight of her choice settled onto his shoulders—not crushing, not unwelcome, but real. A burden he hadn’t asked for and couldn’t refuse.

He didn’t have an answer yet.

But Sera wasn’t looking at Torin anymore. She was looking at him. And that was the answer she had already given.

## Scene 19.03

Kael leaned against the outpost’s exterior wall, arms folded, and watched the Knights through the open doorway the way he watched everything—from outside, measuring, cataloguing.

Ben stood three meters away at the wall console, running route-update calculations with half his attention. The other half stayed on the training hall, where two young Knights were failing a centering exercise for the fourth time in twenty minutes. Their instructor had stopped correcting them. She stood with her hands at her sides and her mouth a thin line, watching her students reach for something that wasn’t there.

The outpost’s failure had already settled into routine. Ben had been inside for less than two hours, and the dread had gone from raw to familiar. That somehow made it worse.

Kael hadn’t moved in five minutes. His stillness wasn’t rest. It was the stillness of someone reading a room and finding it wanting.

“You’re going to say something,” Ben said, not looking up from the console.

A pause. Then Kael’s voice, quiet and dry. “Was I that obvious?”

“You’ve been standing there with your arms folded since Torin finished talking. You only do that when you’re working up to a sentence.”

Another pause. Ben scrolled through the outpost’s archive index, pulling up the pre-Ruusan fragments the curator had mentioned. The console’s screen was old, its image flickering at the edges.

“They train on one foundation,” Kael said.

Ben’s hands stopped on the console. He didn’t turn.

“They build everything on the assumption that the Force will be there the way it always has been,” Kael went on. His voice stayed quiet and precise, the most words he had strung together in days. “Centering exercises. Meditation techniques. The way they teach a Knight to reach for the Force before they reach for anything else. It all assumes one point of contact. Remove the contact and they can’t stand.”

He uncrossed his arms. Ben heard his sleeve brush the wall.

“That’s not a wound problem,” Kael said. “That’s a design problem.”

The sentence hung in the air between them.

Ben’s attention split—part of him tracking Kael’s words, part of him already three screens deep in the archive, pulling up a fragment of pre-Ruusan Weave-theoretical writing the outpost’s curators had flagged as significant. The translation was uncertain in places, the argument circling something about local structure and imposed order that Ben didn’t have time to parse.

Kael’s observation registered as true before it registered as consequential. Like a navigation marker on a sensor sweep—logged, filed, not yet acted on. Ben felt a small shock of recognition that Kael had been thinking this through alone, from outside, and had said nothing.

“Design problem,” Ben repeated, still scrolling.

“I did not say it was a solution.”

“I know.”

Ben’s fingertip paused on the console.

At the bottom of the fragment, appended in a hand he recognized from the ledger recovery on the *Steady Returns*, was a short marginal note. Three lines. Unsigned. The annotation used the same spare, certain hand that had filled pages of Veraine’s working notes—no contractions, academic syntax, short working sentences, no flourish.

She had been here. She had read the same fragment the outpost’s own curators had read, and she had noted—without rhetoric, without any address to a reader—a specific structural reading of it that was not the Order’s.

Ben read it twice.

The register hit him before the content did. Intellectually calm. Precise. Curious. Inhumanly composed. She wrote for herself—no taunt, no performance, no sign that anyone else would ever hold this screen. The diction matched the ledger: the same working-mind voice, the same absence of affect, the same restless intelligence expressed as what-else-might-follow-from-this.

He checked the date on the annotation. Seven weeks ago. She had passed through this outpost before she reached Kolven, before the battlefield node, before Ben had even filed his departure manifest. She had been ahead of them in trajectory from the beginning.

Ben copied the annotation to his datapad without comment, the way a pilot filed a navigation marker without announcing it.

Behind him, Kael had gone quiet again. In the training hall, the Knights had stopped their centering exercise and clustered near the far wall while their instructor spoke in a voice too low to carry. Through the open doorway, the outpost’s recycled air carried the faint tang of sweat and old stone.

Sera appeared in the corridor, her pack slung over one shoulder. “Desh has the *Steady Returns* prepped. We’re clear to lift whenever you are.”

Ben closed the archive interface. The annotation sat on his datapad—three lines of Veraine’s working mind, copied and filed. Kael’s sentence still hung behind him, unremarked, unplaced.

Two readings of the same problem. Two meters apart. One corridor of air.

He did not put them together. The connection was there—he could feel its shape at the edge of his perception, the way he could feel the wound’s pull strengthening as the outpost’s orbit carried them closer to Veranthos—but he did not reach for it. He held both pieces and did not set them side by side.

“We’re done here,” Ben said.

Sera’s eyes moved from Ben to Kael and back. She registered something in the silence between them and chose not to name it. “Good. The longer we stay in orbit, the uglier that gets. Desh says the nav-computer is starting to throw errors it can’t explain.”

“That’s not the nav-computer,” Kael said.

No one answered him.

The *Steady Returns* lifted from the landing pad with Desh at the controls and the rest of them strapped into the crew cabin, watching the outpost shrink in the aft viewport. The compound’s lights were still on. The Knights inside were still failing their centering exercises. The seal was still failing, and the people paying the cost were still standing in rooms wondering what they had done wrong.

Ben watched the outpost become a speck and then vanish.

Ahead of them, the primary wound tugged at him. Immense. Heavy. The wrongness had stopped feeling like an atmosphere and become a direction—a weight in the front of Ben’s skull, a pressure behind his ribs, the specific absence of something load-bearing and essential. Veranthos was ahead. Veraine was ahead. The endgame was approaching, and Ben was carrying a choice he had not yet identified as a choice.

On his datapad, two files sat open. Kael’s sentence, captured as a voice note he did not remember recording. Veraine’s annotation, copied from the outpost console as a textual fragment.

The *Steady Returns* jumped to hyperspace. The outpost was behind them. The wound was ahead.

Ben closed the datapad and didn’t look at either file again.

# Chapter 20

## Scene 20.01

Veranthos filled the viewport.

Ben pressed his palm to the cold transparisteel and felt the jolt in his chest before he could put a name to it. The planet was beautiful. Blue-green oceans spread across most of the hemisphere, broken by cloud banks that caught the system’s star at a low angle. Temperate forests ran in deep green bands. Coastlines curved into sheltered bays. From orbit, it looked like a world someone would choose on purpose—the sort of place the old Republic would have flagged for colonization surveys and then argued over for three generations.

His hand stayed on the viewport. The cold seeped into his skin. Beneath his ribs, something tugged.

He didn’t turn. “Desh.”

“Already on it.” Desh’s voice came from the cockpit console behind him, steady and low. The sensor suite hummed through its startup cycle. “Give me thirty seconds.”

Ben kept his hand where it was. The planet turned beneath them, slow and silent, and its beauty sat wrong in his perception the way a door that didn’t quite latch sat wrong—close enough to miss if he hadn’t already been uneasy. The cockpit smelled of recycled air, old caf, and the faint hot-metal tang the Steady Returns always picked up when the drives had worked too hard. Someone had left a ration wrapper wedged beside the nav console. Normal things. Ship things. Details that should have anchored him.

The pull under his ribs deepened.

“All right,” Desh said. “Weather first. It isn’t following the rotation.”

Ben took his hand off the viewport and turned.

Desh had the sensor display up on the main console, half a dozen data streams running in parallel. Blue-white light from the screen lit his face from below, and his expression was the one he wore when the numbers refused to behave—calm, focused, filing each mismatch without letting any one of them shake him.

“Show me.”

Desh tapped the display. A weather overlay spread across the planetary surface in false-color bands. “Prevailing winds should be riding the rotation vector. Coriolis, standard atmospheric modeling. These are about forty degrees off. And that’s not a local storm front—the whole band’s shifted. Vegetation index is throwing errors too.”

“What kind of errors?”

“The kind where the computer thinks its own sensors are bad.” Desh brought up a second overlay. “Photosynthetic response is peaking in the wrong hemisphere. The forests are acting like they’re in direct sunlight, but the star angle says they should be in twilight. The nav computer flagged it as a calibration fault. I ran the diagnostic twice. Sensors are clean.”

Ben moved in behind Desh’s shoulder. On the display, the false-color bands shifted and re-formed as the sweep continued its arc. The data was clean. The planet was not.

“Ocean currents,” Desh said. He still wasn’t looking up. “Reversed. Not just in one basin—the whole circulation pattern. Warm water toward the poles, cold water toward the equator. Thermal gradient’s inverted.”

The cockpit went quiet except for the sensor suite’s hum and the soft chime of the nav computer logging each anomaly. Ben’s attention narrowed to a point. The awe that had pressed his palm to the viewport was still there, still pulling under his ribs, but it had company now—the cold clarity of a tactical assessment running alongside a body that knew something was wrong before the data finished proving it.

“Show me the boundary,” Ben said.

Desh’s hands paused over the console. “Which boundary?”

“The one between the anomalies and the normal readings.”

A beat. Desh pulled up a composite overlay—weather, vegetation, ocean thermals, stacked in translucent layers. The pattern snapped into view at once. A ring. Not geometric; it followed the contours of a mountain range on one continent and the curve of a deep-ocean trench on another. But it was unmistakable. Inside the ring, the data went bad. Outside it, the readings edged toward normal. Not cleanly normal—none of it was clean—but closer. The sort of readings a survey team might flag and then move past.

The boundary was failing. Ben could see it in the overlay: patches of bad data bleeding outward, tendrils of reversed current and misplaced photosynthesis reaching past the ring’s edge. The seal wasn’t holding.

He didn’t say any of that. The interpretation stayed in his body—a change in pressure he’d felt the moment they dropped out of hyperspace, a membrane he’d crossed without knowing it, the sudden drag of something immense on the far side. His Force-sense was still there, technically. He could still reach for it. But what came back was flattened, muffled, like sound on the far side of a sealed hatch.

The wound was pulling. Immense. Silent. Heavy in a way that had nothing to do with mass.

“Sera,” Ben said. “Torin. Kael. Cockpit.”

He didn’t raise his voice. He didn’t need to. The ship was small enough that it carried.

Sera arrived first, her footsteps quick and quiet on the deck plating. She stopped just inside the cockpit hatch and looked at the viewport, then at the sensor display, then at Ben. Her face didn’t change. The same composure she’d worn in the fortress, in the thin places, in every moment since she’d woken a thousand years out of time—blade-sharp, disciplined, held.

“How bad?” she said. It wasn’t really a question.

Torin came through the hatch behind her. He stopped. He looked at the planet.

Ben watched him.

Torin’s expression shifted—not much, not enough for anyone not watching closely. The warmth that usually lived at the corners of his eyes went still. His shoulders settled. His breathing eased by a fraction.

He didn’t say anything. He didn’t need to. The planet was tugging at him too, and whatever it was pulling, it wasn’t dread.

Kael entered last and stayed in the hatchway, arms folded, face unreadable. He looked at the sensor display for a long moment. Then he looked at Ben.

“That’s the boundary,” he said.

Ben nodded.

“The seal.”

“Yes.”

Kael’s gaze moved to the overlay—the ring, the bleeding edges, the bad data spreading outward in slow, patient fingers. His expression didn’t change. The silence that followed belonged to someone who’d seen containment fail before and knew exactly what came next.

Desh was still working the console. His hands moved through a sensor-sweep routine Ben recognized—the one he used when he was looking for something specific and didn’t want to say what until he found it.

“Desh.”

“Valley coordinates,” Desh said. His voice had gone flat in the way it did when he was processing something he didn’t like. “Where the seal’s central infrastructure should be. I’m getting more than one signature.”

The cockpit went still.

Desh expanded the scan. “One ship, powered down under canopy at the north end of the valley. Ash-Tithe profile match on the hull geometry. And scattered metallic returns along both ridge approaches—buried or netted over. Not natural. Older power traces too.” He glanced at Ben then, briefly. “She didn’t just beat us in. She staged this.”

Ben felt the words land and lock. Of course she had. While they had been pulling survivors out of wreckage, while they had been crawling through archives and dead outposts and trying to understand what they were even chasing, Veraine had been reading the same old Brotherhood records for routes and manifests, seeding teams ahead of herself, caching equipment where she knew she would need it, letting them spend time on answers she had already turned into motion.

“On the surface,” Desh said. “Not in approach. Not in orbit. Parked. Cold. Main thermal residue says it’s been there for weeks. The ridge returns aren’t fresh either. Surface work started before we ever entered the system.”

He zoomed the display. The valley resolved in topographic relief—a long, narrow cut between two mountain ridges, dense with forest cover, a river winding through the center. At the northern end, a heat shadow too regular to be geological. Along the valley floor and up one slope, faint lines and nodes the computer kept trying to smooth away as terrain noise until Desh forced the gain higher. Emplacements. Buried hardware. A worked path under the trees.

Veraine had not arrived with them. She had been here while they were still crossing the Corridor, while they were still piecing together what the old records meant, while Ben still thought there might be approach time left if they moved hard enough. She had used every hour they spent catching up.

The tactical recognition landed clean and sharp, cutting through the awe and the pull under his ribs. There was no approach time left to spend. No room for planning, no margin for hesitation. The timeline had collapsed into the present.

Sera was still looking at the display. Her hands were at her sides. Her face was the same face she’d worn since she stepped into the cockpit—composed, disciplined, held—but something behind it had locked into place.

Torin hadn’t moved. His eyes were on the valley coordinates. His expression was the one Ben had been watching for since the thin places, since the outpost, since the first moment Torin had relaxed into the wrongness instead of bracing against it. Not certainty. Not determination.

Longing.

Ben looked away. He filed it. He did not name it.

Kael was the last to speak. His voice was quiet, dry, the way it always was when he was delivering the thing nobody wanted to hear.

“How long until we can land?”

Desh didn’t look up from the console. “Twenty minutes if I push the descent. Fifteen if I don’t care about the thermal shielding.”

“Push it,” Ben said.

Desh’s next sweep tightened on the valley. The ship signature stayed where it was. So did the buried returns. Nothing about the readout suggested a camp being set. Everything about it said the operation was already in progress.

The cockpit hummed. The planet turned beneath them, beautiful and wrong, and the wound pulled at Ben from the far side of a failing membrane. Veraine was already on the surface at the valley coordinates. No approach time left to spend.

## Scene 20.02

The descent path cut through a low saddle between two ridges, and the moment Ben stepped past a squat outcropping of grey-green stone, the Force was gone.

Not thinned. Not shifted. Not muffled behind a sealed hatch the way it had been since orbit. Gone.

His boot was still in the air. The next step should have landed; his body knew the rhythm, the weight transfer, the little corrections that kept him upright on uneven ground. But the presence that had threaded every thought and motion since before he could remember was simply absent. The space where it had been did not fill in. It stayed open, a blank cut in the world—like reaching into a dark pocket and finding no seam, no fabric, nothing to catch the hand.

He stumbled.

His knee slammed stone. Cold shock bit through his leg, followed by the hard, sharp pain of kneecap on rock. His palms skidded over the ground. The stone was cold. The air was still and cool and carried a green, slightly off smell, vegetation that had learned its lesson in the wrong direction. Behind him, someone said his name. Sera, he thought. The voice sounded far away, the way voices did when they came from the other side of a threshold he had crossed alone.

He stayed on hands and knees for three breaths. The stone was real. The air was real. The absence was absolute.

He pushed himself upright. His knee throbbed. His hands were scraped raw. Small, ordinary hurts from a body that had no choice but to keep going.

No current. No presence. No answer.

“Ben.” Sera’s voice, closer now. She had stopped two paces behind him. She had not crossed the boundary.

He straightened. His breathing stayed even by force of habit. His hands stayed steady. The reflex to reach for the Force rose, met nothing, and rose again—a skipped beat that refused to settle.

“Give me a moment,” he said.

He didn’t turn. He looked at his hands—scraped, cold, ordinary—then at the path ahead, winding down through a thinning stand of trees toward the first ring of what had once been a city. The wrongness wasn’t in his hands. It wasn’t in the stone. It was in the space between things, and it wasn’t going to stop.

He took the next step. And the next. The group followed, crossing the boundary one by one behind him, and he didn’t look back to see who hesitated.

---

The evacuation zone started without a sign or a marker. One moment they were walking through forest—grey-green trees, undergrowth that curled in spirals instead of straight, the occasional flower opening on the wrong side of a shadow—and the next they were passing a line of ground vehicles parked beside a road that hadn’t been maintained for a thousand years.

The vehicles were intact. Their canopies were shut. Their nose cones pointed away from the city, toward the ring boundary, toward the place where the seal had once held. A dozen of them, then two dozen, then more as the road widened and the forest gave way to cleared ground that had once been a landing field. Overgrowth had swallowed most of it—vines thick as a man’s arm, trees splitting permacrete and keeping on—but the pattern was plain. Every vehicle pointed out. Every door on every structure still standing hung open.

Desh stopped beside a personnel carrier with a crumpled rear panel. He ran his hand over the metal. “They didn’t take anything.”

Ben looked at the open doors, the lined-up vehicles, the landing field that had been a staging area. “They didn’t have time.”

“Or they didn’t care about things.” Desh’s voice had gone flat in the way it did when he was working through something he didn’t like. “They just wanted out.”

Sera had moved ahead, her hand resting on the hilt of her lightsaber. She was looking at the city. What showed through the trees—low buildings, pale stone, the suggestion of streets—was intact. No blast scoring. No collapsed roofs. No sign of the kind of destruction that emptied a city.

“Kael,” Ben said. “Anything?”

Kael had stopped at the edge of the landing field. His arms were folded. His face was unreadable in the way it always was when he was reading something he didn’t want to name. “No birds.”

Ben listened. The silence wasn’t complete—there was wind, the rustle of strange-growing leaves, the sound of his own breathing—but there were no birds. No insects. No animal calls of any kind. The city ahead stood whole and silent in a way that had nothing to do with peace.

They walked.

---

The city was not a ruin. That was the horror.

The buildings stood. The streets were clean except for the overgrowth—vines climbing walls and then turning back halfway up, tendrils curling downward as if gravity had changed its mind. Trees had pushed through walls without knocking them down, horizontal splits in stone where trunks had grown through and kept going, bark and mortar woven together. Flowers bloomed on the shadowed faces of buildings, their petals opening toward the dark.

A child’s toy lay on a step. A stuffed animal, faded to grey, one button eye missing. It had been set there, not dropped. Someone had meant to come back for it.

Ben’s hand brushed a wall as he passed. The stone was cold. Real. Ordinary. The contradiction between intact city and the thing warping through it pressed at his chest in a way the Force would normally have smoothed out—a dissonance he could have reached into and named. Without it, the pressure stayed.

Desh had stopped in the middle of the street. His shoulders had gone rigid in a way Ben recognized—the stance of a man carrying weight with no name, no source, no reason to be there. A sadness about nothing. A grief that wouldn’t find its target because there wasn’t one to find.

“Desh.”

“I’m fine.” The words came out flat, automatic. “It’s just—” He broke off. His hand pressed against his sternum, a gesture that looked unconscious. “It’s nothing. I’m fine.”

It wasn’t nothing. Ben could see that in the way Desh held himself, in the shallow cut of his breathing, in the way his eyes moved over the empty street as if he expected something to answer. Non-Force grief. The body’s response to a wound it could not name.

Ben didn’t try to name it for him. He waited. After a moment, Desh let his hand fall and kept walking.

---

The ridgeline opened without warning.

One step they were in the city, passing a school with its doors standing open and its windows intact, and the next the buildings dropped away and the ground fell into a valley stretched between two mountain ridges like a scar between ribs. The river Ben had seen from orbit wound through the center, silver-grey in the late-afternoon light. The forest on the far ridge stood green and still.

And ahead, in the valley between the ridgelines: the Scar.

It was roughly three meters tall. A vertical line in the air, like heat shimmer without heat. It didn’t glow. It didn’t pulse. It didn’t move. It simply hung there—a distortion the eye could catch but the mind couldn’t settle. Straight on, it was almost invisible. From an angle, it was unmistakable. The air bent around it in a way air shouldn’t. Light on the far side arrived a fraction too late, or too early, or both.

Sight came before understanding. Ben’s eyes registered it before his mind could give it a name, and in that thin gap between looking and knowing, awe and horror hit at once. The thing every warning had been pulling him toward. The wound at the center of the seal. The tear in the Force Gavran had tried to contain and Veraine was trying to open and a dozen Jedi Lords had died to keep shut.

His breath settled. His hands hung at his sides. He did not reach for the Force because the Force was not there to reach for, and with a clarity that had nothing to do with thought, he understood it would not be there for what came next.

Behind him, nobody spoke. The silence on the ridgeline belonged to five people staring at the same impossible thing and finding no words that could make it smaller.

Ben stopped walking.

## Scene 20.03

They set camp on the ridgeline where the trees thinned and the valley opened below. From there the Scar hung in the air like a vertical wound, three meters tall, a line that should not have been there. It gave off no glow. It never moved. It simply hung above the river, bending the light around it so the eye caught it and the mind slid off.

Ben drove a tent stake into the cold ground and kept his eyes away from the valley. His hands knew the work—stake, mallet, line, repeat. The place pressed at the space where his Force-sense ought to have been, a steady weight like a hand against his ribs, and every muscle in him wanted to move, to do something, to make the camp ready for a threat that was already here. His mind knew better. Movement would not fix this. He slammed the next stake in harder than he needed to.

Desh handled the other tent. Sera laid out bedrolls with the neat, economical motions of someone who had pitched camp on hostile ground a hundred times over. Kael disappeared into the treeline without a word—scouting or solitude, Ben did not ask.

Torin stood at the ridge edge and stared at the Scar.

He had not shifted since they stopped walking. His pack sat at his feet where he had dropped it. His arms hung loose at his sides. The late light caught his profile—the hard line of his jaw, the breadth of his shoulders, the stillness of a man who had found what he was looking for and needed nothing else.

Ben finished the tent and straightened, rubbing at a knee that still ached from the fall at the boundary, a small, ordinary pain that helped keep him anchored to something sane. He looked at Torin’s back and knew he ought to say something—ask a practical question, call for watch assignments, anything that would put words into the widening silence.

He said nothing. Whatever sat on Torin’s face was not fear.

It was longing. Quiet, settled, the certainty of someone who had already chosen and was only waiting for the hour.

Ben’s hands went still on the tent line. The recognition hit with a sickening tilt, as if the ground had shifted under him: not abstract unease anymore, but fact. Not *what might happen tomorrow*, but *what Torin had already decided.* The group’s fragile cohesion, held together by momentum and necessity since Gavran’s fortress, was starting to crack. Torin was not waiting. Torin was ready.

Sera passed him with an armful of ration packs. She did not look at Torin. Somewhere in the city, Ben realized, she had stopped looking at him the way you stopped staring at a door you knew would open. Desh kept his back to the ridge, all his attention on the poles and lines and the things he could actually fix. Kael remained in the trees.

Nobody watched Torin except Ben. And Ben could not unsee what he had seen.

Dark fell fast on Veranthos. The sky slid from grey to black without a real sunset, the way light drains from a room when the door shuts. The Scar outlined itself against the stars—a line of absence, a cut in the sky where the light arrived wrong or not at all.

Ben sat in the tent with the flap open. The datapad rested on his knee, its low glow the only light. Outside, Torin’s silhouette had not left the ridge edge. He had been standing there for hours. He would still be there at dawn, Ben knew, and then he would walk down into the valley, and the thing Ben had been dreading since the first thin place would happen.

Tomorrow. The word sat in his chest like a stone.

His gaze dropped to the datapad. The file was still open from the outpost—Veraine’s second ledger entry, the one he had not read yet. He had been saving it, or avoiding it, or both. Now the waiting had nowhere else to go.

He opened it.

The entry ran maybe three hundred words. No salutation. No date. No addressee. The diction struck first—no contractions, no ornament, no softness. Short working sentences. Academic syntax cut down to its load-bearing pieces. She wrote like a surgeon recording findings: precise, certain, and not interested in whether anyone agreed.

She had walked the midpoint-era site three days before Ben’s group reached it. Her notes on the technique-lineage fragment were brief—a cross-reference to Ankhural’s third discourse, a notation on the thread-density variance between the Corridor’s inner and outer rings, and a single line observing that Gavran’s containment architecture had been built to withstand pressure from outside, not erosion from within. Then, at the end of the entry, a structural observation:

*The keystone’s load-distribution pattern assumes a twelve-anchor array in full contact with the local field. The ninth anchor’s chamber shows stress fractures consistent with asymmetric loading—the field pulling against a void where the anchor should be. Gavran did not name this feature. It is the point at which his arithmetic fails.*

Ben read it twice.

The ninth anchor. Torin’s chamber. The structural feature Gavran had never named was the thing that would fail first—not because the seal was weak, but because it had been built on the assumption that all twelve anchors would hold. And one of them was standing at the ridge edge, already gone.

He closed the file.

The tent stayed quiet. Outside, the Scar hung in the dark like a seam that had come undone. Torin’s silhouette had not moved. Desh was in the other tent with his instruments, Sera not watching Torin watch, Kael awake and still somewhere in the dark. The clock had run down to hours.

Ben set the datapad aside. Veraine’s voice stayed with him—precise, certain, detached in a way that was not cruelty but arithmetic. A mind at work. A mind ahead. She had seen the same structural feature he was now staring at from a tent on a ridge, and she had written it down without comment, without triumph, without anything but the cold clarity of someone who had turned suffering into a problem and solved it.

He could not close the knowledge. He could only sit with it, and with Torin’s stillness, and with the line of absence in the sky that would still be there when morning came.

# Chapter 21

## Scene 21.01

Ben woke to the wrong light.

The tent fabric glowed with the thin gold of dawn on Veranthos, but the shadows fell at angles that pulled his eye sideways, along lines that never quite settled. He sat up slowly, one hand on the cold ground, and let the disorientation wash through him. The Scar. The place had been at work on all of them since they arrived, and sleep didn’t blunt it.

He pushed through the tent flap and stopped.

Torin stood on the ridgeline in the same place he’d occupied all night. His pack still sat where he’d dropped it. Against the valley below, his outline was unchanged from the shape Ben had watched through the dark hours—broad-shouldered, still, a man waiting for something only he could see.

But his Force presence had changed.

The heaviness that had clung to Torin since the fortress—that warm, jagged pressure Ben had learned to read as grief held under a lid—was gone. In its place was something lighter, sharper, polished clean. The presence of a man who had settled something in the dark and walked out the other side.

Ben’s body tightened before his mind caught up. The urge to move, to close the distance, to do anything at all, rose in his chest like a physical shove. Torin should not look rested. No one slept well on Veranthos. The calm rolling off him was wrong in a way Ben felt in his bones before he could put words to it.

He crossed to the ridgeline.

Torin didn’t turn. The Scar hung below them, outlined now by sunrise as it had last night by stars—a vertical seam of absence three meters tall, bending the light around it so the eye caught and the mind slid off. The river ran beneath it, silver in the dawn, indifferent.

“You’re leaving,” Ben said.

It wasn’t a question. His own voice came out flat, controlled, the tone he used when he was trying to keep something from breaking.

Torin turned. Slow, unhurried. His weight barely shifted; he was already grounded, already decided. His face was calm. Not the calm of resignation, or of someone hiding something. The calm of a man who had made his peace and needed nothing from anyone else.

“Yes,” Torin said. “I am.”

“Now. This morning.”

“Within the hour.”

Ben felt the ground move under him. Not the ridgeline—that was solid—but the footing of the conversation, the assumptions he’d brought to it. He’d expected resistance. He’d expected Torin to still be wrestling with this, caught between what he wanted and what he knew. He’d come armed for a man in conflict.

Torin wasn’t in conflict.

“You can’t,” Ben said. “Torin, you can’t go down there alone. We don’t know what happens when someone approaches the wound directly. We don’t know what the technique does to a failing seal. We don’t—”

“I know.”

“You don’t know everything. None of us do. Veraine’s already ahead of us, the seal is degrading faster than Gavran’s model predicted, and if you walk into that valley and do what I think you’re planning to do—”

“I am planning to do exactly what you think I am planning to do.”

The words landed without heat. Torin said them the way he might have said the sun was rising, or the river was cold. Plain statement. No challenge in it. No defiance.

Ben’s chest tightened. “Then you know I can’t let you.”

Torin looked at him. The warmth in his eyes was genuine—the same warmth Ben had seen in the fortress, in the Steady Returns, in every moment when Torin had made the group feel like a unit. It was still there. It had not gone cold or distant or resigned. That was the worst of it.

“Ben,” Torin said, his voice kind, “you are not going to stop me.”

“I’ll argue with you until you listen.”

“I know you will. You should. It’s what you do.”

“Then listen.” Ben stepped closer, close enough that the conversation turned private, the two of them on the ridge with the Scar below and camp behind. “The group needs you. Sera needs you, whether she’ll say it or not. Kael is barely holding together. Desh is doing everything he can, but he’s not Force-sensitive and he can’t—”

“Desh is the steadiest person in this camp.”

“That’s not the point.”

“It is exactly the point. You have people who can do this without me. You have Sera’s expertise, Kael’s instincts, Desh’s judgment. You have your own senses, which are sharper than you give yourself credit for. You do not need me to walk into that valley with you.”

“I’m not talking about what we need. I’m talking about what happens if you go down there and try to—”

“I know what happens.”

The quiet certainty in Torin’s voice stopped Ben cold.

“I have known what happens for a thousand years,” Torin said. “I knew it when Gavran asked me to stand in that chamber. I knew it when the seal closed around me. I knew it the first time I used the technique, on a battlefield you’ve never heard of, against an enemy whose name I’ve forgotten.”

He paused. The Scar hung behind him, a seam of wrong light, and his Force presence sat steady and clean and utterly at peace.

“It was beautiful,” Torin said.

Ben’s throat tightened.

“The technique. The first time I used it. I was young. Younger than you. The battle had been going for hours and we were losing, and I reached for something I’d only read about in Ankhural’s third discourse—a way of touching the weave directly, not through the Force as we knew it but through the structure beneath it. And it opened.”

His voice had changed. Not the warmth—that remained—but something under it, a resonance Ben had never heard before. Not confession. Not apology. Not a plea to be understood.

Statement.

“The clarity was absolute. No light, no dark, no categories. Just the weave itself, and my hands inside it, and the understanding that everything I’d been taught about boundaries was optional. That the limits weren’t natural. That they had been placed there.”

“Torin—”

“It was the truest thing I’ve ever felt.”

The words hung in the dawn air. Ben could hear his own heart. He could feel the strange angle of the shadows. He could feel Sera’s presence at the edge of his awareness—she had come out of the tent at some point, and now stood at the tent mouth with one hand on the bulkhead frame.

“I’m not asking for permission,” Torin said.

Five words. Nobody moved.

Ben felt them land. Not like a blow—Torin’s voice was too warm for that, too reasonable—but like a door shutting. The argument he’d been building, the appeals he’d been lining up, the weight of everything he wanted to say about the group and the seal and the cost of going alone—all of it ran into those five words and stopped.

Torin wasn’t asking because he’d already decided. He wasn’t hiding. He wasn’t manipulating. He was telling Ben the honest shape of a choice made a thousand years ago and confirmed in the breach zone, and he was doing it with the same warmth he’d always shown.

Something in Ben’s chest dropped. The clarity was sharp and cold.

He understood, in that moment, that argument alone would not bring Torin back.

At the edge of his vision, Sera’s hand came off the bulkhead.

She had been standing at the tent frame. Ben didn’t know how long. Her weight had shifted slightly toward Torin’s Force presence without any visible gesture toward him—the posture of someone reading a threat she’d hoped not to find. Her eyes had been closed. Ben hadn’t seen her close them.

Now her hand left the frame and her eyes opened, and her jaw set in a way only someone trained to see would catch. The tiny change of a Dantari practitioner who had just run a diagnostic read and found what she hadn’t been looking for.

She didn’t speak. Her eyes found Ben’s.

The contact lasted the space of a breath. It carried everything Ben needed: that Torin’s presence held prepared architectures, countermeasures, defenses built for anyone who tried to lock him. That the man on the ridgeline was not merely leaving. He was ready for them to try to stop him.
