Sera’s eyes returned forward. Her hand stayed at her side.

Ben’s training caught what her face did not say. The recognition hit like a second blow—Torin had anticipated this. Anticipated Sera specifically. Built something into his own presence that she had just read, silently, while Ben was still trying to find a line that would work.

He had to trust a warning he only half understood.

Torin hadn’t moved. The Scar hung behind him. Dawn light fell at the wrong angles. The air between the three of them had gone thin enough for Ben to feel in his lungs.

Nobody spoke.

Sera did not speak.

She did not need to.

## Scene 21.02

Torin walked.

He went down the ridgeline at the same measured pace, unhurried, as if he’d settled the matter somewhere dark and private and no one else’s opinion could touch it. Below him, the Scar hung like a vertical seam of bad light, three meters of warped dawn that made Ben’s eyes slide away from it. The river ran silver under it, indifferent and too far away to care.

Ben moved before he thought. One step. Then another. The urge to close the distance, to put himself between Torin and the valley, hit him like a shove to the sternum.

He caught himself at the edge of the drop.

Torin’s Force presence trailed after him as he descended, lighter than it had been since Ben woke, stripped of the heaviness Ben had come to read as grief held under a lid. It felt like a man walking toward something he wanted. Not duty. Not sacrifice. Something he’d been reaching for long before Ben had any idea what it was.

The valley felt off-kilter under Ben’s boots. He sensed it through his soles before he could put words to it—a subtle lean in the field, the Scar’s dead zone tilting toward Torin as if it recognized his shape. The seal was failing faster because Torin was walking toward it. The cascade acceleration wasn’t theory anymore. It was happening now, in real time, and Ben stood on a ridgeline watching it happen.

His hands itched to move. Lightsaber. Comlink. Anything that would let him do something instead of stand there helpless. The vertigo returned, that sick sense of the ground shifting under everything he’d counted on, all his footing sliding out from under him.

A hand closed around his forearm.

Desh. Ben hadn’t heard him come up beside him. Quiet feet for a man with no Force talent.

Desh’s other hand held his datapad, chrono-drift readings running in a neat scroll Ben recognized without reading a single number. The pattern matched what Sera’s face had said earlier—the prepared architectures, the countermeasures, the defenses Torin had built into himself for anyone who tried to lock him down. Desh’s display confirmed it from the outside, through instruments and math instead of the Force, and the two pictures snapped together in Ben’s head into a shape he refused to name.

Desh didn’t speak. His grip on Ben’s arm lasted only a second, practical and steady—the sort of grounding you gave someone on a bad ledge when you knew silence could become a hazard. Then he let go and stood there with the datapad open, watching the valley with a flat, professional focus, as if catastrophe could be read in the numbers if you stared long enough.

Ben’s attention narrowed. The pressure that had been driving him toward action—toward stopping Torin, seizing control, choosing violence because violence felt like control—turned inward. He wasn’t fighting the urge to move anymore. He was holding still against it, and that took more strength than moving would have.

Kael stood ten meters farther down the ridge, where the path widened before the descent. His hands hung loose at his sides. Not relaxed. Loose the way a man stood when he had decided, fully and deliberately, not to grab for anything. His weight was back on his heels, shoulders square, face unreadable in the way Ben had learned meant Kael was feeling more than he would ever say.

He wasn’t watching Torin.

He was watching Sera.

Sera stood at the tent frame. Her hands were clenched at her sides. Not the tightness of someone forcing herself not to act—the tightness of someone holding a line she had drawn long ago and would not redraw now. Her jaw was set. Her eyes followed Torin’s descent with the precision of a Dantari practitioner reading a threat she had already decided not to answer.

Ben saw then that she wasn’t frozen by uncertainty. She was held still by the clearness of what she would not do.

Torin reached the valley floor. The Scar’s warped light caught him, bent around him, and for a heartbeat his outline blurred—a man walking into a wound that knew him. His stride never faltered. His Force presence never twitched. He crossed into the dead zone like someone stepping into a room he’d been waiting years to enter.

The silence on the ridge was the loudest thing Ben had ever heard.

It held. Ten breaths. Twenty. It held through the moment Torin’s figure shrank to nothing but a dark shape among the rocks, through the moment the bad light swallowed him and he was gone.

No one moved.

Sera’s voice came at last, barely above a whisper. It didn’t break the silence. It filled it.

“I was the one they sealed.”

Her hands stayed at her sides. Her gaze stayed on the valley. Her voice was steady in the way a blade is steady—not because it can’t shake, but because it has been forged to keep its edge under pressure.

“I cannot seal another.”

Four words after the first five. Nine in all. Ben felt them hit and settle in his chest.

He understood. The understanding came whole, without debate, without bargaining, without the need to offer alternatives or make this easier. Seizing Torin would repeat the crime they were trying to escape. Sera had said it once, and the group had heard her, and the choice was made without anyone calling it a vote.

The agony of doing the right thing too late and at too high a cost sharpened into something clear and cold. Ben carried three reasons for letting Torin go—the moral line Sera had spoken, the tactical reality Desh’s numbers confirmed, the wound Sera carried that no argument could close—and he knew he would only say one of them. The other two would stay in the silence, in the space between the people on this ridge, in the shape of a group made smaller by the loss of its strongest member.

He nodded.

The motion was small. Sera might not even have seen it. But Kael’s weight shifted by a fraction, and Desh’s datapad dipped an inch, and Ben knew the group had read him anyway.

Desh broke the silence that remained. His voice was flat, practical, the first full sentence after Sera’s line settled in.

“So. What now?”

Ben had been waiting for that handoff without wanting to think of it that way. The pressure moved off the choice itself and onto its cost: Torin was gone, the group was diminished, and the path ahead had no map. The seal was failing faster. They were behind already. The only answer that didn’t repeat the crime was the one that left them weaker.

The clarity hurt. But it was clear.

Ben looked at Sera, at Kael, at Desh. The Scar’s broken light touched the edges of his vision. The river kept running silver below, indifferent. The ridge held its silence now not as helpless watching, but as the stillness of four people who had chosen restraint at the highest possible price and were still standing in it.

He didn’t have the answer yet. But he knew, with the certainty of the first solid ground since Torin had spoken, that he wouldn’t find it alone.

## Scene 21.03

Ben’s hands were shaking.

He didn’t notice until he brought up the Codex files and his fingers snagged on the datapad’s slick surface, the motion sensors catching half-formed commands and throwing them away. The camp had gone so quiet it seemed to press against the skin. The ridge held its silence the way it had since Torin’s shape vanished into the Scar’s bad light, and nobody had moved from where they stood.

He scrolled past the technique-lineage fragments Sera had pulled from the archive. Past Kael’s framework notes from the outpost—the structural insight that had lodged in his mind like a splinter he couldn’t quite work free. Past the seal schematics Desh had annotated in his neat, non-Force-sensitive hand, all practical questions and pressure tolerances.

Gavran’s marginalia.

The handwriting was cramped and angular, a thousand years old and still sharp enough to cut. Ben had bookmarked this passage in the fortress archive and read it again in the thin place, when the wrongness had been pressing at his senses and Torin had been standing too close to the edge. Back then he’d been looking for something he could use alone. A technique. A lever. Some answer a Skywalker could turn into a weapon.

He read it now with different eyes.

The words were the same. *We lacked the will or the trust.* Gavran had written the line beside Ankhural’s resonance theory, a single sentence that had read like an admission of failure the first time Ben saw it. A commander’s postmortem. Ben had taken *will* as the important word—the Jedi Lords hadn’t been strong enough, hadn’t been committed enough, hadn’t been willing to do what needed doing.

Now his breath caught on *trust*.

His chest tightened. Not as an idea, but as a physical thing, landing in him before his mind had a chance to name it. His focus narrowed. It felt as if a door had swung open somewhere he hadn’t known was locked. His hands settled on the datapad, the trembling draining out of them while his eyes stayed fixed on the word.

Trust. Not bravery. Not strength. Not the thing Skywalkers were supposed to have by the bucketful.

Gavran had known. A thousand years ago, standing in the same wreckage and facing the same failure, he’d written the answer in the margin and then died at his post because he couldn’t act on it. Because he’d had the theory and lacked the one thing the theory needed.

Ben’s shoulders eased down. He hadn’t ordered them to. The release came on its own, a tension he’d carried so long he’d stopped feeling uncoiling between his shoulder blades. His breathing deepened. The datapad’s blue glow threw Gavran’s handwriting across his face in pale lines.

He pulled out two more datapads.

The motion started as habit, his hands moving before he’d fully decided what he was doing. Kael’s framework notes went on the left. Sera’s technique-lineage fragments on the right. Gavran’s resonance theory in the center. Three screens. Three sources. Three traditions that had never cooperated, never trusted each other, never even lived in the same century.

He laid them out on the camp table. Cross-referenced a passage from Kael with a notation from Sera. Brought up the seal-pressure readings Desh had been logging since they’d made camp. The pieces didn’t snap together—that was the wrong image. They settled into place the way a structure found its load, each part taking the strain it was built to carry.

His hands were steady now. Not calm. Steady. The difference mattered.

The change in his body was as plain as a bruise. A tension he couldn’t name easing out of him. The load that had been driving him since the first bad note in the Darrpa Sector—the need to solve, to master, to carry—was still there, but it wasn’t his alone anymore. It couldn’t be. The pattern on the three datapads made that plain.

Three datapads. Three people.

He’d been trying to be Gavran. Trying to be the one who held the seal alone, who carried the knowledge and the cost and the failure. Trying to be his father. Trying to be the Skywalker who fixed things.

The word in Gavran’s margin said trust.

Ben turned.

Sera stood at the tent frame where she’d been since Torin walked. Her hands were still clenched at her sides—not the strain of someone fighting herself not to move, but the discipline of someone who had drawn a line and wouldn’t redraw it. Her eyes met his and held. She’d been waiting. Not for an order. For something else.

Kael was ten meters down the ridge, where the path widened before the descent. He hadn’t shifted since Torin vanished. His weight rested back on his heels, his hands loose at his sides in the way Ben had learned meant Kael was feeling more than he would ever say. He wasn’t watching the valley anymore. He was watching Ben.

Desh stood closest to the camp table, his own datapad still open to the chrono-drift readings he’d been running. He’d known. Ben saw it in the set of his shoulders, in the way his datapad was angled—not toward the valley, not toward the threat, but toward Ben. He’d been waiting for this turn for a day or more, and he’d said nothing because saying nothing was what Desh did when the moment hadn’t arrived yet.

Three faces. Three people who had chosen to stay when walking away would have been easier.

Ben opened his mouth and heard his own voice as though it belonged to someone standing a step behind him.

“I need to show you something.”

It came out steadier than he expected. Clearer. The command tone that had carried them through every crisis so far was gone—not pushed down, not forced aside, simply absent. In its place was something quieter. A reach instead of a decree.

“We find another way.”

He paused. The word was there, waiting. He let it land once and didn’t give it a second chance.

“Together.”

Sera’s hands unclenched. The motion was tiny, just enough to show the grip she’d been holding since Torin spoke his last line and walked. Her jaw stayed tight. Her eyes stayed on Ben’s. But something in her stance changed, the way a blade shifts when it’s no longer in guard.

Kael’s weight came forward onto the balls of his feet. He didn’t speak. He didn’t need to. The movement said enough—a man who’d been braced to go his own way, resetting himself for something else.

Desh lowered his datapad. His face stayed unreadable in the way Ben had learned to read anyway—the flat, professional focus of someone who had spent years in operational silence and knew when a room had changed. He didn’t smile. He didn’t nod. He simply set the datapad on the camp table beside the other three and began unpacking his instruments with the quiet efficiency of someone who had known where this was going for a day or more.

The Scar’s light touched the edge of Ben’s vision—that vertical seam of warped dawn, still bleeding its bad color into the valley below. The seal was still failing. Veraine was still moving. Torin was still walking toward something Ben couldn’t stop.

But the load had shifted. The answer was on three datapads spread across a camp table on a ridge in the middle of a catastrophe, and the people who would build it were still here.

# Chapter 22

## Scene 22.01

The datapads sprawled across the camp table like a sabacc hand nobody had bothered to collect.

Ben had been at it for three hours. The chrono on his wrist said so, but the number had stopped meaning much once Torin had walked into the Scar’s bad light. Time on Veranthos had gone strange after that. Ben trusted the ache behind his eyes more than the display, and the stiffness in his lower back from hunching over Gavran’s marginalia. He trusted, too, the way Sera’s handwriting had started turning up on his datapad without either of them bothering to say that was all right.

She worked across from him, her side of the table as crowded as his: three screens, three traditions, one problem refusing to sit still. Kael’s framework notes on her left. Gavran’s resonance theory in the center. Her own technique-lineage fragments on the right, the archaic NSW notation Ben had spent the first hour squinting at, the second hour learning to read, and the third hour treating like a language he should have known from the start.

She hadn’t asked permission. Two hours ago she had simply reached over, dragged his primary datapad closer, and started annotating Ankhural’s fragment in her own hand. Her script flowed beside his modern Force analysis—her angular pale-blue strokes beside his white standard characters, two disciplines sharing the same screen without grinding each other flat.

Ben watched her for a few seconds.

Three. Four.

She didn’t look up.

“The third harmonic won’t hold,” she said. Her stylus tapped the screen once. “Not if we treat it as a single anchor point. The NSW framework assumes a central keystone—one practitioner carrying the seal’s shape while the others reinforce it. Ankhural’s fragment doesn’t use a keystone. It spreads.”

“I saw that.” Ben pulled the datapad closer and angled it so they could both read. “The spread is the point. No single tradition dominates the containment. The resonance comes from the overlap, not from anybody holding the center.”

“Then the NSW notation is wrong for this.”

The words hung between them.

Ben looked at her. The blue glow from the datapad left her face hard to read, but her hand had gone still. The stylus hovered over the screen as if she’d reached the edge of a ledge and was deciding whether to step off.

“The notation isn’t wrong,” Ben said. “It’s built for a different shape. The question is whether you can bend it.”

Sera’s jaw tightened. Not anger. The look Ben had learned to read as thinking. She was weighing something, testing it, deciding whether it would hold.

“The NSW discipline,” she said at last, “was made to contain breaches. Single-point failure. One practitioner anchors the seal. The others support. It works because someone stays in the center and does not let go.” Her eyes met his. “Ankhural’s fragment has no center. If I force one onto it—if I make the keystone model fit a distributed structure—the resonance will fail. The traditions will fight instead of reinforcing each other.”

“Can you work it on its own terms?”

The question settled between them.

Sera lowered her gaze to the datapad. To her handwriting beside his. To the fragment she’d been circling for two hours, trying to press it into a framework that had kept her alive through a war and a millennium of stasis and a waking nightmare she still hadn’t named aloud.

She set the stylus down.

Then she picked it up again and wrote.

Ben watched the change happen. Not a translation. An adaptation. She wasn’t forcing Ankhural’s fragment into NSW notation anymore. She was letting the fragment’s logic set the terms, building a new notation around it that borrowed from her discipline without belonging to it. Her handwriting changed as she worked. The angular precision stayed—Sera would never stop being precise—but the rhythm shifted. Faster. Less guarded. The strokes came in bursts, as if she’d stopped checking each line against some internal standard and was simply following the shape of the answer.

Something in Ben’s chest eased. He didn’t put a name to it. He didn’t need one.

He pulled up the seal-pressure readings Desh had been logging since they made camp and started cross-referencing them against the resonance requirements Sera was sketching. The numbers were ugly. The power draw alone would strain every portable generator they’d brought, and if the containment had to hold longer than a few minutes, they’d need to route through the *Steady Returns*’ reactor by way of a relay Desh hadn’t built yet.

“Desh.”

Desh looked up from his own datapad. He’d been on the supply manifests for the last hour, his face flat in the way of a man who’d done this math before and disliked where it led.

“Tell me we have enough.”

“We don’t.” Desh turned the datapad so Ben could see the figures. “Portable generators get you maybe twelve minutes at the draw you’re projecting. After that, you’re living off whatever’s left in the cells, and the cells weren’t built for sustained resonance output. They’ll overheat. You lose containment.”

“Can we route through the ship?”

“Through a relay, yeah. I’d have to build it. Six hours, maybe eight. The parts are there—barely—but it won’t be pretty. If the relay drops mid-containment, you lose everything.”

Ben took that in. Twelve minutes of portable power. A relay that might fail. A resonance structure that needed three traditions and had never been tested outside Ankhural’s theory.

“The relay’s the right call,” he said. “Build it.”

Desh nodded once and went back to his manifest. No argument. No hesitation. Just the clipped efficiency of someone who’d spent years making impossible logistics work on timelines that didn’t care what he thought.

Sera finished her notation and pushed the datapad across the table.

Ben looked down.

The resonance structure was there—not as theory, not as a fragment, but as a plan. Three anchor points, spread out. Modern Jedi perception at one vertex, NSW discipline at another, Sith-trained precision at the third. The overlap would build a containment field no single tradition could make alone. The seal would hold because nobody was trying to dominate it.

It was beautiful.

It was also impossible without Kael.

Ben felt the weight of that settle into his chest. Not an idea, but a pressure under the ribs that made the next breath harder than the last. The resonance needed three traditions. They had two. Sera’s willingness to adapt had turned a theoretical fragment into something they could use, but the plan had a condition, and the condition was a man who had spent a thousand years as a tool and had no reason to trust anyone asking him to be one again.

Without Kael, there was no resonance.

Without resonance, there was only Torin’s sacrifice—the Battle Master going into the wound alone, holding the seal with his body the way Gavran had, the way the Jedi Lords had, the way Ben had been trying to since the first bad note in the Darrpa Sector. Or Veraine’s destruction—the wound opening on her terms, the transition casualties she’d already counted and accepted, the seal breaking because a Sith alchemist had decided the arithmetic justified the price.

Ben kept both possibilities in mind at once. The plan that worked. The plan that failed. The distance between them was Kael’s choice, and Kael’s choice wasn’t something Ben could control.

He looked at Sera. She was watching him, hands still on the datapad, her face unreadable.

“It works,” he said.

“I know.”

“Kael—”

“I know.” She held his gaze. “Ask him, Ben.”

The name landed cleanly. Not Skywalker. Ben. A small shift, almost nothing, but it was there. She’d said it the way she might have said Desh’s or Torin’s, like the name belonged to someone she trusted.

Ben didn’t comment. Neither did she.

The Scar’s light touched the edge of the camp through the tent flap, that vertical seam of warped dawn still bleeding its bad color into the valley below. The seal was still failing. Veraine was still moving. Torin was still walking toward something Ben couldn’t stop.

But the plan was real. The datapad on the table held an answer that hadn’t existed three hours ago, built by two people who had stopped working alone.

Ben stood.

“Desh. The relay.”

“Already on it.”

“I need to find Kael.”

Desh looked up from his manifest. His face stayed flat in the way Ben had learned to read anyway—the stillness of a man who knew exactly what hung on the next conversation and wasn’t going to say it out loud.

“Good luck,” Desh said.

Ben headed for the tent flap. The plan worked in theory. It required three Force traditions. They had two who were willing.

Kael was the variable.

## Scene 22.02

The kilometer to the ridge passed in silence.

Not the silence of people with nothing to say. The silence of people who had already said it back at the camp table, with datapads spread between them, and were now walking toward the thing that would make the plan real or break it.

Ben felt the folded Codex notes in his jacket pocket, a small pressure against his ribs, pointless now. The intellectual work was done. Sera had rewritten the notation. Desh had run the power calculations. The resonance structure sat on the datapad, three anchor points spread across traditions that had spent a thousand years trying to kill each other.

Now came the physical part.

Sera’s stride matched Desh’s on ground neither of them had grown up on. She moved like someone who had learned to walk where the terrain wanted you dead and never lost the habit. Desh moved like someone who had spent years in places where a wrong step got you noticed, and getting noticed got you dead. Different wars. Same economy of motion.

The mast rose ahead of them, taller than Ben had expected from the schematic. The load-distribution antennas curved out from the central column in a pattern that didn’t match any manufacturer’s standard loadout. Hand-shaped, not stamped. Someone had stood here and bent metal to a purpose that had nothing to do with efficiency and everything to do with amplification geometry.

Sera stopped ten meters from the base.

Ben stopped with her. Desh peeled off toward the clearing’s edge without being asked, datapad already out, approach telemetry overlaid on the array schematic. Three component locations lit up as his fingers traced them. He didn’t look up.

Sera circled the mast once. Slow. Her eyes moved over the base plate, the assembly marks, the salvage labels half scraped away. Ash-white tally lines at the bottom edge—Pira Kul’s pressure-cut neatness, Vess Oran’s salvage tags, the crew’s handwriting all over the lower third of the structure.

She didn’t pause for any of it.

Her hand found the base plate at a point two-thirds up from the ground, where the harmonic geometry was subtle enough that Ben had to look twice to catch it. Not the crew’s work. The design logic underneath the assembly. The thing that had decided where every component would sit before anyone touched the metal.

She laid her palm flat against it and closed her eyes.

Ben watched her face.

The change was small. A slight shift at the corner of her mouth. The skin around her eyes tightening, then easing. Recognition settling into certainty the way a key settled home in a lock.

She exhaled, a small Dantari breath, the end of the reading.

“This is her mind.”

She didn’t open her eyes. Didn’t add anything. The four words landed and stayed there.

Ben felt them hit his chest. Not fear. The weight of understanding that Sera had just named something that couldn’t be unnamed. Veraine’s authorship of the array was no longer a deduction, a tactical probability, a thing they had reason to believe. It was there. In the metal. In the curved antennas. In the geometry Sera had read with her palm flat against the base plate while the ridge wind tugged at her sleeves.

He didn’t answer. The statement didn’t ask for one. Sera had read something he couldn’t read, and she had delivered it without softening it. The isolation of her expertise stood plain in the space between her hand and the metal, in the fact that she still hadn’t opened her eyes.

Desh straightened from his datapad.

Ben turned. Desh’s expression had gone clinical—the shift from observer to actor that Ben had seen before, in other places, under other pressures. The moment when someone stopped waiting and started moving. His posture had changed too. Shoulders set. Weight balanced. The datapad held at his side like a tool he was done with.

“That’s my job.”

Ben opened his mouth.

Desh cut him off. “Let the Force-users do the Force thing. I’ll handle the part that requires hands.”

The words came matter-of-factly. Not heroic. Not grim. Desh’s tone made it sound like he was assigning cargo, not volunteering for the most exposed piece of the plan.

Ben looked at him. The three component locations on the datapad. The approach telemetry. The relay Desh was supposed to build back at camp—six hours, maybe eight, the parts barely there. And now this. The sabotage. Alone. Against armed opposition. The most exposed task in a plan that depended on both parts working.

Desh met his eyes. The clinical expression wasn’t coldness. It was focus. The same focus Ben had seen in the *Steady Returns*’ cockpit when Desh had mapped the bearing that pointed toward uncharted space. The same focus in the fortress control room when Desh had read the containment network display and understood what nine dead nodes meant before Ben finished explaining it.

He’d already calculated the cost.

Ben didn’t argue. Desh had volunteered before anyone asked, which meant the choice was his. Arguing would have been for Ben’s comfort, not Desh’s. And Ben had spent enough of his life letting other people protect him from the weight of their choices.

“All right,” Ben said.

Desh nodded once and looked past him, toward the mast. Toward Sera, who had opened her eyes and was watching them both with her hand still flat against the base plate.

Nobody commented on her four words. Nobody needed to.

The ridge wind picked up, pulling at the antennas with a sound like breath across a bottle’s mouth. Below them, the valley stretched toward the Scar—that vertical seam of bad light still bleeding its bad color into the sky. The seal was still failing. Veraine was still moving. Torin was still walking toward something Ben couldn’t stop.

But the plan had two parts now. The resonance trio needed Kael. The sabotage needed Desh alone against armed opposition. Both plans failed without both parts.

Sera lifted her hand from the base plate. The metal where her palm had rested was warm—Ben could see the faint condensation fading at its edge. She didn’t look at it. She turned and started back toward camp, her stride matching the ground the way it had on the walk out.

Desh fell in beside her. Ben followed.

The silence on the return walk wasn’t uncomfortable. It was the silence of three people who had just made choices that could not be unmade, carrying the weight of them without comment because the weight was the point.

Sera’s hand on the mast base still echoed in Ben’s chest. The warmth of her palm on the metal. The small exhalation when the design signature resolved. The four words she had delivered without opening her eyes.

This is her mind.

Ben walked. The Codex notes in his pocket pressed against his ribs, pointless now. The intellectual work was done.

## Scene 22.03

Kael sat at the camp’s edge with his legs dangling over the ridgeline, facing the valley and the Scar below.

He didn’t turn when Ben approached. He’d felt him coming—the way anyone with Force-sense felt another presence in a place this thin, where the wound leaked through everything and made distance between minds shorter than it should have been. His shoulders were loose. His hands rested on his thighs. He looked like a man who’d been sitting long enough for the cold to work into his joints and had decided he didn’t care.

Ben stopped three paces back. First light touched the far ridgelines now, gold on gray stone, while the valley floor stayed pooled in shadow. The Scar cut through it like a seam of bad color—not quite red, not quite violet, the sort of wrongness that made his eyes want to slide away. It pressed at the edges of his Force-sense, a low throb he’d learned to ignore and had learned, just as quickly, that ignoring was still a choice.

His instinct was to speak. To fill the space between them with the plan, with urgency, with the weight of what had been building since the mast and Sera’s palm flat on the metal and Desh’s matter-of-fact volunteer. The impulse rose in his chest—explain, persuade, arrange the next few minutes so they could only end one way. He saw, with abrupt clarity, how easy that would be. A trimmed version of the truth. The right emphasis. Kael slotted where Ben needed him, one more hidden brace under a structure only Ben could see.

Jacen would have known how to do that.

Ben let the thought burn off and took one slow breath. Not that. Not here.

He walked the last three paces, sat down beside Kael at arm’s length, and let his legs hang over the ridge. His hands settled on his knees. Stone chilled through his trousers. Below them, the Scar gave one slow, ugly pulse that made something behind Ben’s eyes tilt, then steadied again.

Kael said nothing.

Ben let the silence stand. Thirty seconds. Maybe longer. The place made everything heavier than it should have been, but he’d learned to carry weight he hadn’t chosen. The first light kept creeping down the far ridge. Somewhere back in camp, Sera would be checking the notation one last time. Desh would be going over his equipment. Torin was gone—walking toward something Ben couldn’t stop and couldn’t follow. The plan had three commitments and one open question.

Kael was the open question.

“There’s a resonance structure,” Ben said. “Three anchor points. Three traditions. The Codex called it Weave-Resonance—Gavran’s note said Ankhural thought it could strengthen the seal by spreading the load. They never tried it. They didn’t trust each other enough.”

He kept his eyes on the valley, on the Scar’s slow bleed of light, and laid it out the way he had for Sera and Desh at the camp table. The theory. The requirements. The risks—and there were plenty of them, starting with the fact that no one had ever done this before and ending with Veraine trying to stop them. His own role: the Skywalker anchor, the Jedi component, the inheritance he hadn’t asked for and couldn’t refuse. Sera’s role. Desh’s role—alone at the array, exposed in a plan that depended on both parts holding.

And then Kael’s.

“You’d be the third anchor,” Ben said. “Sith-trained. Brotherhood methodology. The resonance needs three traditions because the seal was built to hold all of them—Jedi, Sith, and the ones that didn’t fit either category. Without the third anchor, the spread collapses. The seal fails. Veraine wins.”

He stopped. The words had come out flat and precise, like a mission brief stripped down to the bone. He hadn’t planned the flatness. It was simply what happened when you told someone the truth and didn’t leave anything out—no spin, no pressure, no clever scaffolding built to make the answer you wanted feel like the only one on the table.

If Kael said no, the plan failed, and Ben would have to find another way or watch the seal come apart.

He didn’t say that. Kael already knew it.

The silence stretched. Kael’s Force presence stayed guarded—not hostile, never hostile, but closed in a way Ben had learned to read over the weeks since the fortress. The wound made it harder to read than it should have been, smearing the edges of perception the way heat haze blurred a horizon. He could feel Kael thinking. He couldn’t feel what Kael was thinking about.

His breath steadied. He waited.

Somewhere below, a bird called—thin and reedy in the thin air, carrying too long, too hollow. The Scar pulsed again. Ben’s stomach lurched for half a second, then settled.

Kael’s fingers moved.

It was a small thing. His right hand, resting on his thigh, shifted to the grass beside him. His fingers closed around a few blades and pulled—not hard, not angry, just the deliberate motion of a man turning something over in his head. He did it again. A third time. Small green tufts came up, roots and dark soil clinging together.

Ben watched without really watching. His peripheral vision caught the rhythm—pull, pause, pull, pause—and he knew what it meant. Kael wasn’t listening to the plan anymore. He’d stopped listening somewhere around the third sentence and started weighing the person offering it.

The shift was tangible. Ben felt it the way he’d learned to feel a room full of people who didn’t trust each other—a subtle change in pressure, the moment when the decision stopped being about what was being asked and became about who was asking. His chest tightened. Not fear. The knowledge that he’d done everything he could do, and the rest wasn’t his to control.

He didn’t fill the silence.

Thirty seconds. Maybe a minute. Light spread farther down the ridge, picking out the valley contours, the dark line of trees, the silver thread of a stream Ben hadn’t noticed before. The wound pressed at the edges of everything, making the place feel slightly off, like a holorecording played a hair too slow. His hands stayed on his knees. His breath stayed even.

Kael pulled another blade of grass.

Then he turned his head. Not all the way toward Ben—just enough to acknowledge him, enough to show the line of his profile against the lightening sky. His expression was unreadable. His Force presence stayed shut down, but something in the set of his shoulders had changed—a loosening, maybe, or a settling. The difference between a man bracing for a blow and a man deciding the blow was worth taking.

His question came without preamble.

“Why should I believe this one is different?”

# Chapter 23

## Scene 23.01

Ben pulled the datapad from his belt and laid it on the grass between them.

Kael didn’t look at it. His gaze stayed on the valley, on the Scar’s ugly pulse below the ridgeline, on sunlight spreading gold across the far peaks. His hands rested on his thighs, loose and still. The grass he’d been worrying lay in a small green scatter beside his knee.

“The resonance structure needs three anchor points,” Ben said. “Three traditions. Jedi, Sith, and a third that doesn’t map cleanly to either—Sera’s tradition, the one Gavran tried to bury. The Codex called it Weave-Resonance. Ankhural thought it could stabilize the seal by spreading the load across incompatible approaches. They never tested it. They didn’t trust each other enough.”

He tapped the datapad. A schematic bloomed: three nodes in a triangle around the Scar’s mapped perimeter, with Desh’s amplification array marked at the fourth point, offset and exposed. Blue and amber lines sketched force and counterforce; beneath them, Sera’s precise notation packed the math tight.

“I anchor the Jedi component. Sera anchors the third tradition. Desh runs the array—he’s the practical anchor, the non-Force component that keeps the resonance from folding back on itself. Without him, the whole thing collapses in about ninety seconds.”

Kael’s expression didn’t change. His Force presence stayed shuttered—never hostile, just closed in the way Ben had learned to read over the weeks since the fortress. A door held shut from the inside.

“You’d be the third anchor,” Ben said. “Sith-trained. Brotherhood methodology. The resonance needs all three because the seal was built to contain all three. Without that third anchor, the spread goes asymmetrical. The seal fails. Veraine wins.”

He stopped. The words had come out flat and exact, the way a mission brief sounded once you stripped away everything that wasn’t true. He hadn’t meant to sound that way. It was what happened when you told someone the truth and left nothing out.

“The risk,” he said, “is that no one has ever done this. The resonance could cascade. It could kill all three of us. It could kill Desh, who’ll be standing at the most exposed point with no Force-sense to warn him. The math says the odds of a stable resonance on the first attempt are low. Sera’s best estimate puts them below forty percent.”

He let the number hang.

The Scar pulsed below them, a slow, ugly throb that made something behind Ben’s eyes tilt before settling again. Morning light had reached the valley floor now, picking out the dark line of trees, the silver thread of the stream, the wrong-colored seam that cut through everything like a wound that had forgotten how to close.

Kael’s fingers moved. Not toward the datapad. Toward the grass beside his knee—another blade, pulled slow, roots and dark soil clinging.

“You’re asking me to stand in a resonance structure built on math no one has tested,” Kael said. “With a Jedi and a Jedi Lord’s lieutenant and a pilot who can’t feel the thing that might kill him. Against a Sith alchemist who’s been planning her move for a thousand years. On odds below forty percent.”

His voice was dry. Level. The same register he always used—the monk’s brevity, the understatement that could land like a blade if you weren’t ready for it.

“Yes,” Ben said.

Kael pulled another blade of grass. Silence stretched between them—ten seconds, twenty, the pressure at the edges of everything making the air feel heavier than it should. Ben kept his hands on his knees. Open. Visible. The datapad between them like an offering, not a demand.

“What are the actual odds?” Kael asked.

The question came out flat. Not a challenge. Not a test. A man asking for the number because he’d been given numbers before and learned, over a thousand years of being used, that numbers were always the first thing institutions lied about.

Ben felt the weight of it settle in his chest. The answer he couldn’t soften. The trust he couldn’t manufacture. The invitation that could only be answered by telling the truth and letting Kael decide what it was worth.

“Honestly?” He met Kael’s eyes. “Lower than forty percent. Sera’s estimate assumes optimal conditions. We won’t have optimal conditions. Veraine will be actively working against us. The seal is degrading faster than the model accounts for. And the resonance has to hold long enough for the seal to catch—we’re not just triggering it, we’re sustaining it. Sera thinks maybe thirty percent. I think she’s being optimistic.”

The words came out the way they’d formed in his head—unvarnished, unspun, the same flat precision he’d used for the rest of the briefing. He didn’t add a qualifier. He didn’t say but we can do this or I believe in us or any of the things a commander said to make the odds feel less like a gamble. The odds were the odds. Kael deserved to know them.

Kael’s face shifted.

It was a small thing—a change in the set of his jaw, the focus of his eyes. The careful blankness he’d been holding cracked along a seam Ben hadn’t seen until it opened. Underneath was something raw and unfamiliar. Not hope. Not yet. The thing before hope—the recognition that the person asking had just handed him the truth and trusted him to hold it.

Ben felt the moment land. Not in the Force; the pressure made everything smear at the edges, perception sliding off surfaces that should have been solid. He felt it in his body. The tightness in his chest. The stillness in his hands. The knowledge that he’d done everything he could do and the rest wasn’t his to control.

He didn’t fill the silence.

Kael looked away. Back toward the valley, the Scar, the light spreading across the far ridge. His fingers found another blade of grass and pulled—slow, deliberate, the motion of a man turning something over in his head that was too heavy to hold still.

The wrongness pressed down. Ben’s stomach lurched—the Scar pulsed again, a low throb that made the morning light look wrong for half a second, the colors bleeding toward violet before snapping back. He steadied his breathing. Kept his hands on his knees.

Thirty seconds passed. Maybe more. Kael’s Force presence stayed shuttered, but something in the set of his shoulders had changed—a loosening, or a settling. The difference between a man bracing for a blow and a man deciding the blow was worth taking.

When he spoke, his voice was dry. Level. The driest Ben had ever heard it.

“You’re the first person in a thousand years who’s told me the truth about what it would cost.”

He didn’t say yes. He didn’t say no. The words hung in the thin air between them—an acknowledgment, not an answer. Recognition that this ask was different because it was honest, and that the honesty was what separated it from every other institution that had ever demanded his service.

Ben let the silence hold. The Scar pulsed below them. The light kept spreading across the valley, beautiful and wrong, and Kael sat with the weight of a choice that was actually his to make.

## Scene 23.02

Kael stood.

He rose without hurry, deliberate as ever, as if every motion had already been judged and found worth the effort. He didn’t look at Ben. His attention stayed on the valley, on the Scar’s ugly pulse below the ridgeline, on sunlight spilling gold across the far peaks. The datapad still lay in the grass between them, its schematic faintly lit against the morning glare.

Then he started walking.

Not toward camp. Toward the ridge edge, where the ground broke away into the long slope that fell to the Scar. His stride stayed even, unforced. A man moving because he needed room to think, not because he needed to go somewhere.

Ben’s body moved before his mind did. He was halfway up when he caught himself—one hand planted on his knee, every muscle gone rigid, the urge to follow hammering through his nerves like a proximity alarm he couldn’t shut off.

*Don’t.*

The word hit hard and stayed there. Ben lowered himself back onto the grass. His hands returned to his knees, open and visible, the same posture he’d kept through the whole briefing. The posture that said I’m not leaving and I’m not forcing you to stay, both at once.

Kael didn’t glance back. The space between them widened—ten meters, fifteen, the grass whispering under Kael’s boots. Ben tracked him through the Force without meaning to, feeling that familiar knot of tight-held walls and efficient stillness around Kael’s presence. Still shut. Still guarded. But the closed-off feel of it had changed. Not defensive now. Private. A door held from the inside while the person behind it dealt with something that needed silence.

The Scar pulsed below. Ben’s stomach tightened as the valley’s ugliness pushed up through the morning, a vibration too low to hear and too deep to ignore. For a heartbeat the light looked wrong across the slope, then settled again. He kept breathing. Kept his hands on his knees. Not following took effort. It sat in his shoulders and jaw, a hard ache he couldn’t let go, because letting go might mean moving, and moving was the one thing he couldn’t afford.

Kael reached the ridge edge and stopped.

He stood with his back to Ben, a dark shape against the bright spread of the valley. The Scar lay below like something patient—an old break that had waited a thousand years and could wait a few minutes more. Its edges throbbed with that off note in the land, making the trees look off-color and the stream’s silver thread bend in ways it shouldn’t.

Kael looked at it. Ben watched him look.

Then Kael’s gaze shifted.

It was only a turn of the head, a change of focus. He was no longer staring at the Scar. He was looking down at the camp, where Desh Rolan was working on the amplification array.

Desh had his back to the ridge. He knelt beside the primary node housing with a spanner in one hand and a diagnostic pad in the other, checking connections with the patient care of a man who made sure things worked before anyone asked them to hold under pressure. He had no idea he was being watched. His presence—the warm blankness Ben had learned to recognize in the first weeks of the mission—remained steady and ordinary, the feel of a mind locked on the work in front of it and a life that depended on hands, tools, and stubborn follow-through.

Kael stood motionless at the ridge edge, looking down at him.

Ben felt the shift before he understood it.

It began as a loosening—a small easing in the tight boundaries around Kael’s Force presence that had held since the moment Ben first sensed him in the stasis chamber. Not a collapse. Not surrender. Just a release, like a breath finally let out after being held too long. The edges of Kael’s presence, hard and deliberately closed for weeks, softened a fraction. Opened a little. For the first time since Ben had found him, it wasn’t braced for a blow.

Ben’s own breath caught. He didn’t move. Hands on knees. Back straight. Face neutral—the kind of discipline that came from years of close-hold trust and operational silence, from learning that some moments couldn’t be fixed. Only watched.

*What are you seeing?*

The question rose and vanished without answer. Ben couldn’t read Kael’s thoughts—the Force didn’t work that way, and the Scar’s distortion would have made any reading a mess anyway. But he could see where Kael was looking. Desh, kneeling in the dirt with a spanner. Desh, who had no Force-sensitivity and no destiny and no institution telling him where to stand. Desh, who had signed on for a survey mission that had turned into something else and stayed anyway, not because anyone had ordered him or persuaded him or dangled a reward in front of him, but because he’d decided the work mattered.

The non-Force-user who chose to be here.

Ben got it then. Not through the Force—the Scar smeared that, blurred the edges of everything—but through the way Kael held himself still, the angle of his head, the fixed attention on Desh. Kael Drenn, conscripted by Sith and captured by Gavran and turned into living infrastructure for a seal he’d never agreed to hold, was looking at the one person in the group who had no reason to be here except that he’d chosen it.

The parallel landed without words. Ben felt it settle in his chest, not as revelation but as recognition. Desh had shown Kael something he’d never had in front of him before. Not power. Not conviction. Not destiny. Just purpose, chosen freely and carried without ceremony. The freest person in the group was the one who couldn’t feel the Force at all.

Kael stood at the ridge edge for a long moment. The Scar pulsed below. Light kept spreading across the valley, beautiful and wrong. Desh kept working, unaware, his spanner ticking against the node housing in the quiet morning air.

Then Kael turned.

His stride was different. Ben saw it at once—the change in the way he moved, the shift from careful to deliberate. Not rushed. Not resigned. The walk of a man who had finally set something down in his own mind. His boots found the same path through the grass he’d taken on the way out, but the weight behind them had changed. Less guarded. More certain.

He stopped a few meters from Ben. Close enough to talk. Close enough that Ben could see his face clearly for the first time since he’d walked away.

The careful blankness was gone. The dry detachment Kael wore like armor had split along a seam Ben hadn’t seen until now. Under it was something raw and unfamiliar—not hope yet, but the ground hope came from. The knowledge that the man in front of him had told him the truth and trusted him to carry it. The knowledge that he could choose, and that choosing was the one thing he’d never truly been allowed.

Ben didn’t name it. Didn’t ask. He met Kael’s eyes and waited, hands still on his knees, posture still open, the space between them unfilled.

Kael’s face had changed. Not the careful blankness. Not the dry detachment. Something new. Something that looked, against all odds, like choice.

## Scene 23.03

Kael walked back.

Ben saw the answer in his stride before Kael got close enough to speak. The careful way he had been placing each step since the stasis chamber—the habit of testing the ground before he trusted it—was gone. What had replaced it was something more deliberate, more decided. A man who had stopped measuring and started choosing. His boots hit the same path through the grass he’d used on the way out, but they landed with a different weight.

Ben’s chest tightened. His hands stayed on his knees, the open posture he’d held through the briefing, but his shoulders shifted forward, readying themselves. The Force around Kael felt less clenched than before, the walls around him easing from private to something nearer open air. Still guarded. Still Kael. But the coiled expectation that had clung to him since the stasis ring was gone.

Ben didn’t put words to it. His body had already taken the measure; his mind was only catching up. The waiting was over.

Kael stopped a few meters away, close enough to talk. Ben could see his face clearly now—the careful blankness was gone, and the dry detachment had cracked along a seam Ben hadn’t noticed until it split. Beneath it was something raw. Not hope. The ground hope grew out of.

Sera had risen from her seat by the array housing. Desh had stopped working, spanner still in one hand, diagnostic pad in the other. Neither of them spoke. Morning light lay flat across the grass, and the Scar’s ugly pulse pressed up through the valley floor below.

Kael looked at Ben and held his eyes for a long moment. Not measuring now. Just acknowledging. Then his gaze cut to the valley, to the distortion visible at its base, and back again.

“Someone should probably do something about that.”

The line came out drier than anything Ben had ever heard from him. Level. Unhurried. It landed like a door shutting on a decision already made. The humor was real—Kael’s deadpan timing still as sharp as a snapped tool—but it carried something enormous. The deflection didn’t hide the commitment. It brought it forward.

Ben caught both at once. The dry delivery, the choice underneath it. A man who had never volunteered to put himself in danger for someone else’s work was presenting his commitment as if he were making an offhand remark about a problem in the distance. The armor was still there. It just had a different purpose now—not to keep people out, but to make the vulnerability possible to bear.

Sera let out a breath. Not a laugh. More like air she’d been holding since Kael turned back. She didn’t speak. Her Force presence, blade-sharp and disciplined, shifted in Ben’s perception—not softening, exactly, but settling into a new angle. The way a fighter adjusted her stance when the unknown finally picked a side.

Desh looked at Kael for a beat, then at Ben. His face stayed flat in that practical, all-business way of his, but something moved behind it. He rolled the spanner once in his hand, then set it down beside the node housing with the quiet care of a man who knew a moment didn’t need help to stand on its own.

Kael didn’t elaborate. Didn’t explain. The line hung in the morning air, dry and understated, and then he was moving—not back toward camp, but down toward the valley slope, his stride still deliberate, still decided.

Ben rose. His knees cracked from the long stillness in the grass, the sound plain and grounding. He didn’t look at Sera or Desh. He didn’t need to. The others were already pivoting around him—Sera gathering her pack in efficient motions, Desh doing one final check on the array connections before splitting off toward the first amplification node in the distance.

They moved.

The valley opened below them as they descended, and the Scar’s distortion grew from a background pulse into something Ben could feel in his teeth. Light bent oddly along the tree line. The stream’s silver thread twisted in ways his eye kept trying to reject. But they held formation—Sera on Ben’s left, Kael on his right, Desh already a dark figure on the far ridgeline, working his way toward the node position at a steady pace that said he knew his job and didn’t need reminding.

Ben felt them through the Force. Sera’s presence was a honed edge, centuries of discipline compressed into one bright line—blade-sharp, precise, carrying the weight of everything she’d been forced to hold and refused to drop. Kael’s was different. Still efficient, still tight-knotted, but the knot had loosened into something that could flex. The walls were still there, but they weren’t prison walls anymore. They were the boundaries of a self that had finally chosen its own shape.

And Ben’s own presence—tired, warm, the Skywalker bloodline running through it like a current he’d stopped trying to separate from—sat between them. Three traditions. Three ways of touching the Force that had never been asked to work together before.

Sera’s bright clarity, Kael’s grounded efficiency, Ben’s tired warmth—each distinct, each suited to the others. But they didn’t yet blend. They sat beside one another, three separate tones that ought to have made something larger and instead just occupied the same space. Ben could feel the gaps between them, the places where the harmony should have caught and didn’t.

The Scar pulsed below. The disturbance in the Force pressed up through the valley floor like a note played slightly flat, a vibration too low to hear and too deep to ignore. Ben’s stomach tightened. The strain of that incompleteness sat in his shoulders and jaw—not doubt, but the knowledge that what they were walking toward had never been tried. Three Force-users from three different centuries, carrying three different relationships to the thing they were about to hold together. The discordance was already waiting for them.

Desh’s figure reached the first node position on the far ridgeline and stopped. Ben couldn’t feel him through the Force—the warm blankness of his presence was too far now, swallowed by the Scar’s distortion—but he could see him. A small dark shape against the bright spread of the valley, a non-Force-user walking straight into the worst of it because he’d decided the work mattered.

The group kept moving. The slope leveled out ahead, the valley floor opening toward the source. The clock was down to minutes.

Ben walked between Sera and Kael, and the valley rose to meet them.

# Chapter 24

## Scene 24.01

The valley opened below them like a wound that had forgotten how to close.

Ben took in the geometry in one sweep, the same tactical habit drilled into him before he was old enough to name it. Veraine’s distortion shimmered at the Scar’s base, a heat haze that bent the tree line into a bad angle. Torin was descending the far ridgeline, unhurried, his stride carrying the same warm certainty it had held since the *Steady Returns*. And the resonance group—Sera on Ben’s left, Kael on his right—moved down the mid-valley slope in formation.

Desh was already gone, a dark shape working the high ground toward the first amplification node, his pace steady and professional. Ben couldn’t feel him through the Force anymore; the Scar’s pressure had swallowed the warm blankness of his presence. But he could see him. A non-Force-user walking into the worst of it because the work needed doing.

Morning light lay flat across the valley floor. The stream below caught it in a silver thread that twisted wrong where it ran near the Scar, its surface bending at angles Ben’s eyes kept trying to reject. The same wrongness pressed up through his boots now, a vibration too low to hear and too deep to ignore.

Sera’s presence sat at his left shoulder—blade-sharp, centuries of discipline compressed into one bright line. Kael’s sat at his right—still efficient, still tight-knotted, but loosened enough to bend. Three traditions. Three wounds. Three ways of touching the Force that had never been asked to work together.

The notes were there. They just didn’t blend yet.

Ben’s attention stayed on Torin. His body wanted to move faster, to close the distance, to find the words, to reach across the valley and stop what was already in motion. His mind knew the math. The ridgeline was too far. The timing was fixed. The window was closing, and he was running out of sentences.

He called anyway.

“Torin!”

His voice carried across the valley, bounced off the far slope, and came back thin. Sera’s stride hitched—a half beat of hesitation—then she kept moving. Kael said nothing. Ben felt them both register the call and choose not to intervene.

Torin stopped on the ridgeline.

He turned. Not quickly—there was no startle in it. The motion had the same unhurried quality as his descent, the same ease Ben had first taken for stability and now recognized as something else. Torin’s posture didn’t waver. His shoulders stayed square. His hands stayed loose at his sides. He looked across the valley at Ben the way a man looks at a friend he’s about to disappoint.

Ben’s chest tightened. The desperate hope that had pushed him to call was still there, but it had nowhere to go. Distance and timing were fixed. The only thing left was what he could say, and he didn’t have much.

“Don’t do this.” The words came out raw, stripped of the tactical framing he’d been building in his head since they left camp. “Whatever you think this solves, it doesn’t. It can’t. You know what the technique does to the seal.”

Torin held his eyes across the distance. Morning light caught his face, and Ben saw something there he hadn’t expected—not anger, not defiance. Warmth. The same genuine warmth Torin had carried since the moment he’d woken in the stasis ring, the warmth that had made him feel like the steadiest of the survivors.

It was still there. That was the worst part.

“I understand what you’re trying to do, Ben.” Torin’s voice carried cleanly across the valley, no strain in it. He wasn’t shouting. He didn’t need to. “It’s the right impulse.”

The use of his name landed like a hand on Ben’s shoulder. Not a title. Not Skywalker. Ben. The refusal was affectionate, and that made it worse than anger could have. Torin wasn’t confused. He wasn’t coerced. He’d heard everything Ben had said—on the ship, in camp, across the valley—and he’d chosen a different answer.

“But some problems only have one solution.”

Torin didn’t elaborate. He didn’t justify himself. He held Ben’s gaze for one more beat—long enough for Ben to see the certainty in his posture, the way his weight was settled and his hands were still—then he turned back toward the Scar.

Sera’s presence sharpened at Ben’s left. She didn’t speak. She didn’t need to. The grief that landed in Ben’s chest was the recognition that Torin had heard him and rejected him anyway—not from confusion, not from coercion, but from a different conviction about what the situation required. The desperate hope collapsed. What replaced it was heavier.

Ben kept walking. His boots found the slope, and his body carried him forward because stopping wasn’t an option. Sera and Kael moved with him, their presences steady at his shoulders. The valley floor opened ahead, the Scar’s distortion growing from a background pulse into something he could feel in his teeth.

Then Torin began the technique.

Ben felt it before he saw it—a shift in the Force that started at the base of his spine and ran upward, a bad turn that wasn’t the Scar’s bad turn but something adjacent to it. Active. Deliberate. The Weave near Torin leaned inward.

Not toward Torin. Toward the technique. The local structure of the Force bent at an angle that shouldn’t have been possible, threads of it drawing together and thinning as they approached the point of activation. A pull. A suction. The sensation hit his body first; his stomach dropped the way it did in freefall, his inner ear reporting motion his eyes couldn’t confirm.

Like standing beside a drain.

The image came whole and unwanted. The Weave near Torin was collapsing, thread by thread, the local field drawing down into something Ben couldn’t see and didn’t want to understand. The violence of it registered in his body before his mind could catch up—jaw tight, hands half-curled at his sides, weight shifting forward as if bracing against a current.

The technique was active. The seal’s infrastructure had begun to shift. The three-way convergence at the Scar was locked into motion, and there was no more time for appeals.

“He’s committed,” Sera said from Ben’s left, low and steady.

Ben didn’t answer. He was still watching the ridgeline, still feeling the drain-pull of the technique in his bones. Torin’s figure kept descending, unhurried, certain. The warmth was still there in his posture. The affection was still there in the way he’d said Ben’s name.

The relationship ended in love. That was the worst version of it.

“We need to move,” Kael said for the first time since the valley had opened.

Ben nodded. The motion came automatically. His body was already shifting toward the Scar, his stride lengthening, the pressure of the technique’s activation pressing against him like a wind he couldn’t feel on his skin. Sera and Kael fell into formation at his sides. Desh’s dark shape had reached the first node on the far ridge and stopped.

The group kept walking. The clock was down to minutes. The notes were in place, but not yet a chord.

## Scene 24.02

Desh was already out ahead of them on the broken ground.

The node-cases rode his back in a compact rack, matte cylinders locked tight against one another, and his datapad was in his left hand. He crossed the basalt slabs with the same steady economy he brought to every hostile patch of terrain Ben had ever seen him tackle—unhurried, checking his position against the pad every few strides, never wasting motion. The ground pitched under him at angles that didn’t match any geological process Ben recognized. Desh compensated without a word.

He reached the first spec’d position and dropped to one knee. The node-case came open with a snick of pressure seals. He took one cylinder into his hand—forearm-long, plain, its base plate catching the morning light in a dull gleam—then set it against the rock and locked it down. The click was quiet and exact, the sound of a mechanism built to stay put.

Ben heard it across thirty meters of broken ground. Felt it, too. The sound hit his chest like a latch sliding home on a door he hadn’t known was open.

Sera was half a pace behind his right shoulder. Kael walked at the line’s edge to his left, loose in the stride but sharp-eyed. Neither of them spoke. The three of them were walking into something they couldn’t yet see, and the only person moving with purpose was the man who couldn’t feel the Force at all.

Ben’s mouth twitched. Even in his own head, the irony refused to turn into humor. It just tightened the muscle between his ribs.

They crossed the first node’s position. Ben’s boots found the same basalt Desh had knelt on, and the cylinder was behind him now, seated and locked and doing whatever it had been built to do. He didn’t turn to look. He didn’t need to.

The pitch was under the wind.

Not a sound. A presence with a note to it—faint, held, a single tone stretched at the edge of awareness. It sat beneath everything else: below the scrape of their footsteps on broken stone, below the distant distortion-hum of the Scar, below the pressure of Torin’s technique still tugging at the Weave from the far ridgeline. The node was there. It had not been there before.

Ben’s breathing changed. His body took in the new texture in the Force before his mind caught up. He kept walking. The pitch stayed behind them, steady, the way a beacon stayed steady after you’d passed it and not looked back.

The second node clicked home.

Desh had moved another forty meters down the line. Same economy of motion—kneel, open, seat, lock. Same quiet click. The second pitch arrived under the first, a slightly different note, and the two of them didn’t blend. They sat at an interval. A spacing. A rhythm that wasn’t a chord and wasn’t random, either.

The third node locked. A third pitch. Three notes behind them now, each distinct, each holding its own place in the air. They didn’t merge. They sat in sequence, a spacing with logic under it, a placement asking to be read.

Ben noticed that he was noticing.

The realization slowed him for half a stride. He had never felt infrastructure this way before. He’d felt the Force in living things, in places, in the residue of violence and the quiet after healing. He had never felt a piece of hardware seat into rock and register as a note in a pattern he could hear without his ears. The capacity was new. It had arrived without announcement, without his asking for it, and it was already mapping the valley behind him in a language his body understood before his mind could name it.

His hand wanted to sketch the geometry in the air. He didn’t let it. He kept walking and let the pattern settle into his spine, his shoulders, the soles of his feet.

At the third node, he knew where the fourth would be.

He didn’t look. He didn’t reason it out. The knowledge was simply there—the next pitch already audible in the shape of the silence where it would sit, forty meters ahead and a little left of Desh’s current line. The position wasn’t marked. It didn’t need to be. The array was telling him where it wanted the next note.

Sera’s eyes found his.

She was still half a pace behind his right shoulder, and she hadn’t made a sound, but her gaze met his with the weight of a door opening. Her face was the one she’d worn at the mast—the same reading, the same recognition, the same quiet certainty that what she was seeing was real and shared. She didn’t speak. Neither did Ben.

The wordlessness hit harder than speech would have. Another exchange between them about Veraine’s work, and again no words required. Again the reading matched. Ben felt the confirmation settle into his chest beside the click of the first node—another latch closing, another door he hadn’t known was open.

He drew a breath. Not audible. Just felt.

They kept moving.

The array’s design signature rose through the soles of his boots. The spacing wasn’t regular; the intervals varied, the lateral offsets following a logic that wasn’t grid logic or radial logic or any of the standard configurations Ben had been trained to recognize in tactical briefings. The inter-node rhythm was Veraine’s. The architectural logic matched the mast Sera had read at the overlook, the same signature she’d traced in the air with her hand and named without naming. Ben walked through it now. It felt like moving through the foundations of a building too large to see all at once, something he was inside before he fully understood it existed.

Veraine’s thinking made material. Her design pressed into the valley floor, the node spacing translating principles he could feel but not yet put into words. She wasn’t there. He felt no consciousness in the array, no lingering trace of her mind. She was the architect, not the occupant. The ground was her argument, and he was walking through it.

A deep calm settled into him. His hand still wanted to trace the geometry, but now he knew why. The pattern was asking to be read, and his body knew how to read it. No triumph. No speech. Just the ground made legible beneath him, the valley floor turning into something he could parse.

Kael shifted at the edge of his awareness. He’d been tight-knotted since the *Steady Returns*, efficient and watchful and held close to the body. Now he was anchoring at a specific texture—a frequency Ben hadn’t felt from him before, something that sat at the edge of the array’s rhythm without joining it. The resonance’s preconditions were forming. Ben registered it and still didn’t know what it meant.

They reached the mid-valley position.

Desh was a hundred meters ahead, kneeling at the sixth node site. His hands moved through the same sequence—open, seat, lock—and the click reached Ben half a second after the pitch registered in his Force-sense. The sixth note settled into the array behind him. He could feel all six without turning around. Five behind, one ahead, the whole configuration holding its shape in the Weave like a framework waiting for weight.

Sera’s hand brushed his shoulder. Not a gesture. A confirmation. Her fingers touched the fabric of his tunic and withdrew, and then she was moving to her position, her stride straight as a blade. Kael took his without a word, that new texture still threading through his presence.

The plan was in place.

## Scene 24.03

The Scar widened.

Ben saw it happen—not a trick of light, not something he caught only through the Force. A seam in the air above the valley floor opened by a centimeter, maybe two, and held. The edges had a color that wasn’t a color, like a migraine aura hanging at the edge of sight. It was real. It was getting worse.

His body took the fact in before his mind did. The urge to move, to answer, to do something surged ahead of thought, and for half a heartbeat he was all readiness—heart kicking faster, weight shifting onto the balls of his feet, hands loosening at his sides. Then the rest of him caught up, and that was worse.

Three operations. At once. Pulling the Force in three different directions.

Veraine’s ritual pushed from the Scar’s base, an organized pressure with the same cold logic she’d used to space the nodes behind him. Her work bent the wound-site toward itself, deliberate and exact, the valley floor and the distortion above it leaning into her design.

Torin’s void came down the far ridgeline. Ben couldn’t see him at this distance, through that broken stretch of terrain, but he felt the technique moving—a zone of unweaving sliding downhill. Space around Torin was coming apart thread by thread, and the void carried a personal hunger that Veraine’s ritual didn’t. It was expanding.

And the trio’s position was a triangle of reaching hands that had not yet linked. The resonance had not yet begun. One operation still idle.

The six nodes sat behind them, their ground-tone steady under the discord. Ben could feel all six without looking. Five behind him, one ahead where Desh had locked the last cylinder into place. The rhythm held—not a chord, not a joining, just a spacing with its own logic under it. Desh’s movement showed up as a pressure-shape inside that rhythm now, running the guarded route between the amplification points, not visible but legible in the register the nodes had taught Ben to hear.

Sera moved to his right. Kael to his left.

Neither of them spoke. Sera’s stride was straight as a blade, her boots finding broken ground with the same precision she brought to everything. Kael walked looser, but his eyes stayed sharp; the new texture still threaded through his presence, that frequency Ben had felt anchoring at the edge of the array’s rhythm, something that hadn’t been there before the walk through the valley.

They took their places. Hands outstretched.

The formation settled around him. Sera’s presence was rigid and defined, the specific shape of Dantari Force-lock residue carrying her signature—a thing built to hold, to contain, to refuse to bend. Kael’s was fluid and sharp, the pressurized mobility of a man who had spent a lifetime in confined spaces and learned to move inside them without scraping the walls. Both of them turned toward him, toward each other, toward the work.

Ben reached for them through the Force.

The word came out without hesitation. Not a question. Not a hope. A commitment spoken aloud because the act needed a voice to anchor it.

“Now.”

Three hands linked.

The collision hit him in the chest.

Not pain. Pressure. A sound that wasn’t sound, three notes striking together and refusing to blend. Sera’s note—low and held, the deep register of a lock closed for a thousand years and not certain how to open. Kael’s note—higher, sharper, a frequency that moved inside its own boundaries without breaking them. His own note—reaching, pulling, trying to find the point where the others would meet.

They didn’t meet.

The sound of it in the Force was three instruments playing in three different keys. Not a chord. Not even close. Three separate lines running beside each other without touching, each one clear and distinct and completely alone.

Ben felt the resistance as a shove back. A refusal. The three textures wouldn’t blend under his reach, and the longer he held them, the more the discord built—not fading, not settling, but climbing with a momentum of its own.

His instinct fired.

He reached to lead.

The motion was automatic, the way a hand caught a falling object before the mind decided to catch it. Twenty-eight years of Skywalker training poured into the reach—harmonize, impose order, find the pattern and make it hold. He drew Sera’s note toward the center. He drew Kael’s note toward the center. He pulled with the full weight of his will, the way he’d been taught to pull, the way his father pulled, the way every Jedi he’d ever trained with pulled when command was needed.

The resonance resisted.

The discord worsened.

Ben felt it as a kind of falling—not downward, but inward, the three presences slipping away from each other instead of toward unity. Sera’s note stayed rigid, defined, locked in its own register and refusing to shift. Kael’s note slid sideways, fluid and sharp and impossible to grip, moving the way water moved around a closed fist. His own note was the loudest thing in the mix now, pulling so hard it was drowning out what it was meant to join.

He leaned harder.

His body locked against the resistance. Shoulders tense. Jaw set. The reach became a push, the pull became a command, and the command became the thing making it worse.

Sweat beaded at his temples. The cold of it was specific—concentration-cold, the temperature of effort crossed into strain. The air around him smelled of stone and sealed centuries, and the wind had stopped, and the Scar widened again.

Another centimeter. Visible. Real.

Veraine’s ritual was building. He could feel it at the Scar’s base, the organized pressure climbing in a steady rise, her architecture of action running exactly the way she’d set it up. Torin’s void was still descending, still expanding, the zone of unweaving eating its way down the far ridgeline. Both operations were gaining momentum.

The trio’s was the one not running.

Sera’s hand was rigid in his grip. Kael’s was still—not resisting, not pulling away, just not joining. Neither of them was the problem. The problem was the hand trying to make them join, the will trying to force three keys into one chord by sheer insistence.

The realization came not as thought but as sensation. His own effort had become the obstacle. The harder he pulled, the more the resonance refused. The more he commanded, the less it obeyed. The pattern that had defined him for twenty-eight years was meeting a problem it couldn’t solve, and the meeting felt like a door slamming shut.

He was trying too hard.

He had been trying too hard for twenty-eight years.

It was not working. The discord was worsening. Veraine’s ritual was building power. Torin’s void was expanding. And the resonance was falling apart under his hand, three notes that would not resolve, three presences that would not combine, three people trusting each other with their lives and their Force connections and failing to harmonize because one of them was pulling for a lead.

# Chapter 25

## Scene 25.01

The Scar widened.

Ben felt it happen—not through the Force, not as a ripple in the Weave, but as a hard physical fact his body registered before his mind caught up. Another centimeter. The seam above the valley floor pulled open and held, its edges the wrong color, the sight almost slippery, as if his eyes wanted to slide off it.

Sera’s jaw set. Kael’s hands shook.

The three notes still refused to blend.

Ben pulled harder. The reach had become automatic now—the Skywalker reflex running on pure momentum, like a hand clamped around something that had already started to break. He drew Sera’s note toward the center. He drew Kael’s note toward the center. He shoved his will into the space between them and demanded that it close.

The resonance shoved back.

Not resistance. Refusal. The three frequencies slid past one another without meeting, each clear and distinct and alone, and the harder he pulled, the worse the discord grew. Sera’s note stayed rigid in its low register—the Dantari lock, closed for centuries, refusing to budge. Kael’s note moved the way water moved around a fist, fluid and sharp and impossible to hold. His own note now dominated the mix, pulled so hard it was drowning out the very thing it was meant to join.

His shoulders locked. His jaw tightened. The cold at his temples turned from concentration to strain, and the Scar widened again.

Veraine’s ritual was building at its base. He could feel the ordered pressure climbing, her architecture of action moving exactly as she’d planned. Torin’s void was still descending the far ridgeline, the unweaving zone eating downhill with a hunger that felt personal. Both operations were gaining momentum.

The trio’s wasn’t.

Sera’s hand was rigid in his grip. Kael’s was still—not resisting, not pulling away, just not joining. Neither of them was the problem.
